## OCCULTISM, by Robert Amadou (Julliard).

Mr. Robert Amadou has been set ablaze through his contact with Hermes Trismegistus, Pythagoras, Avicenna, Albertus Magnus, Paracelsus, Jacob Boehme, and Eliphas Levi... We already owe him an essay on Saint-Martin and Martinism <note>Louis-Claude de Saint-Martin (1743–1803) was a French philosopher known as "The Unknown Philosopher"; Martinism is the mystical tradition following his teachings.</note>, and he is currently preparing a *Literary Anthology of Occultism* with Mr. Robert Kanters. He is, therefore, a specialist—if one can call a "specialty" a science, an art, or to put it plainly: a philosophy, which (analogically) contains everything.

The author begins with this definition: Occultism is the collection of doctrines and practices founded on the theory that every object belongs to a single whole and possesses, with every other element of that whole, relationships that are necessary, intentional, non-temporal, and non-spatial.

Then we travel through, or rather fly over with him, the immense landscape that his patient clearing of the land has allowed him to synthesize: Doctrines, Practices, and Religions. The bibliographic notes are abundant and very well executed.

This is an excellent introductory work <note>original: "ouvrage initiatique"</note> that will whet the appetite of those minds tormented by a currently fashionable curiosity. <insert>P. L.</insert>

<summary>This text is a brief book review of Robert Amadou's "L'Occultisme," praising the author's expertise and his comprehensive definition of occultism as a philosophy of universal interconnectedness.</summary>
<keywords>Robert Amadou, Occultism, Esotericism, Hermes Trismegistus, Martinism, Philosophy of Nature, Book Review</keywords>

### PREFACE.

The subject of occultism—by which we mean those sciences called transcendental and magical, the knowledge of which has been passed down and gathered in secret, or is contained in books that have an inner or hidden meaning—has been dealt with very thoroughly in recent years by various eminent students. But the works of these well-equipped investigators are, in most instances, unsuited for a beginning reader, and they are all somewhat expensive. It has remained for the results of their studies to be condensed into a portable volume, which will lead the inquirer into the entrance of each branch of “the occult sciences,” and place within his reach the proper means for conducting his research further in any desired direction. It is this modest but useful task which we have set for ourselves in the present volume, which covers, we claim, in a condensed and summarized form, the entire range of occult knowledge, expressed in the language of a student.

We have tried as much as possible to distinguish between theory and practice, between facts that can be verified and explanations where opinions may differ. We have checked our individual judg-

<vocab>occultism, transcendental, magical, occult sciences</vocab>

<summary>In this preface, Arthur Edward Waite explains that his book is intended to be an affordable, accessible introduction to the occult sciences, condensing the complex work of previous scholars for the general reader. He emphasizes the book's goal of distinguishing between verifiable facts and theoretical explanations.</summary>

# INTRODUCTION.

<margin>a</margin>
The claims of Hermetic philosophy to the consideration of serious thinkers in the nineteenth century are not to be confused with those merely of an exalted intellectual system, or of a sublime and legitimate <margin>Joe Blaise</margin> aspiration. These may, indeed, be urged on its behalf with the force of unadulterated truthfulness, but not as the principal point. What the philosophy—which is indiscriminately called transcendental, Hermetic, Rosicrucian, mystical, and esoteric or occult—submits in its revived form to the investigator of life and its problems as a sufficient and rational cause for its revival, and as an adequate ground for its recognition, is concisely this:—<insert>[</insert>That it comprises an actual, positive, and realizable knowledge <margin>realize</margin> concerning the worlds which we call invisible, because they transcend the imperfect and rudimentary faculties of a partially developed humanity, and concerning the latent potentialities which constitute, by the fact of their latency, what is termed the interior man.<insert>]</insert> <insert>[</insert>In more strictly philosophical language, the Hermetic science is a method of transcending the phenomenal world and attaining to the reality which is behind phenomena.<insert>]</insert> At a time when many leaders of thought have substantially abandoned all belief in the existence of intelligence outside of the visible universe, it is almost unnecessary to say that the mere claim of the mystics has an irresistible magnetic attraction for those who are conscious that deep down in the heart of every man there exists the hunger for the supernatural.

<insert>[</insert>The mode of transcending the phenomenal world, as taught by the mystics, consists—and to some extent exclusively—of a form of intellectual ascension or development, which is equivalent to a conscious application of selective evolutionary laws by man himself to man,

<summary>This page defines Hermetic and occult philosophy as a practical science for accessing invisible realities and developing the "interior man." It argues that this path allows an individual to transcend the material world through a self-directed process of intellectual and evolutionary development.</summary>
<keywords>Hermetic philosophy, transcendentalism, Rosicrucian, occultism, interior man, phenomenal world, supernatural, spiritual evolution</keywords>

<margin>[</margin>Those latent faculties identified as Psychic Force pass, through this training, into objective life; they become the instruments of communication with the unseen world and the modes of existence within it. In other words, the individual's conscious evolution has produced a new sense that enables them to perceive what cannot be perceived by the physical senses.

<margin>[</margin>The powers of the inner man and the possibility of communication with the unseen are the subject of historical magic, which is filled with thaumaturgic accounts of experiments with these forces and the results of this communication. Whether these alleged occurrences should be accepted as proven facts is not the point the enlightened mystic wishes to emphasize. The evidence supporting them may be, and is, important; it may be, and is, overwhelming; but it is not solely on the wonders of the past that the Hermetic claim seeks to be established or demands recognition in the present. Whatever the evidential value for the success of the psychic experiments conducted by investigators of old, they can at least be said to constitute <insert>a sufficient basis for a new series of scientific inquiries</insert> by those who are devoting their intelligence and energy to solving the great mysteries of existence. <margin>|</margin>Otherwise, <insert>transcendental philosophy would simply be the revival of an ancient</insert> faith and would be completely unsuited to the needs of today. <margin>|</margin>It should be remembered, however, when speaking of scientific inquiry, that the reference is not limited to the professional scientists of the time, but includes everyone capable of precise observation who can appreciate the significant nature of the issues involved.

The standpoint indeed is this: the successful experiments of the past can be repeated in the present, and those who doubt it have the opportunity to be convinced by their own experience. In one of his most mystical sayings, Christ is recorded as having said that there are those who are eunuchs from birth, and that there are those who become eunuchs in the interests of

# INTRODUCTION.

the Kingdom of God; so also there are natural magicians and magicians who are the product of art. Yet, generally speaking, the magician—unlike the poet—is not born but made, for the same potentialities abide in the whole of humanity, and they can ultimately be developed in everyone. What is needed, therefore, is not merely people possessed of the gifts of clairvoyance, or even of lucidity, of prophetic foresight, or of the qualities called mediumistic, but those who—by the nature of their aspirations and by the help of a favorable environment—are able to apply the arcane laws of evolution to their own interior selves. But there is another and indispensable condition: namely, the power to distinguish between Hermetic truth and the shameless frauds which have surrounded it from time immemorial. At present, the intellectual world is substantially divided into those who reject esoteric doctrine and practice as entirely fraudulent, and those whose credulity identifies its worst impostures and most childish perversions with its highest forms of truth. Transcendentalism is concerned with the development and application of certain powerful forces resident in the interior man; and as these forces have been developed and applied in various directions, from many motives, and with a variety of ends in view, historical mysticism is very diverse in its character. It is often childish, superstitious, dangerous, malevolent, and obscene, and by its very nature has always been peculiarly liable to the counterfeits of charlatans.

Certain sections of modern mystics have expressed somewhat too freely their indignation against the Christian churches for the abuses and corruptions which they have generated during the undermining process of the ages. Now, the history of no doctrine and of no religion can compare in its abuses and corruptions with that of Magic; for every species of abomination, of "unnatural love and more unnatural hate," has been fostered under the dark wings of the goetic part of mysticism. There, as in other matters, the height of aspiration finds its exact counterpoint in the depths of spiritual degradation. It is the custom with many to shield occultism from the responsibility of these dishonorable histories

<summary>The author argues that magical ability is a latent potential in all humans that must be developed through the application of arcane laws, while warning that the history of magic is filled with as much corruption and fraud as any religion.</summary>

by means of literal interpretations, just as it is the custom among the more gullible section of spiritualists to disguise every stage of fraud among "mediums" by attributing the deceptions of many of those who pose as channels of communication between the seen and the unseen to the "spirit world." In the current periodical literature of our newest and most developed mysticism, one can find attempts to elevate Cagliostro, the Sicilian mesmerist, into an adept of divine magic, on the plea that a person accepting the notorious facts of his life and character as historical truth would be adopting a shallow and childish view. Even the most obvious and direct contradictions which are to be found in the French mystics have been justified and excused by dividing the conflicting statements into different levels of thought. In the same manner, at a number of private *séances* where the amateur channels of communication refused all payment, and where, as a consequence, the essential element of fraud might be safely considered absent, the most constant and awkward trickery has been explained by the hypothesis that the unpaid "channels" were in a complete trance and unconscious—that they were used in this manner by the "holy spirit world" in order to save psychic force while "form-manifestations" were being witnessed.

But nevertheless, the transcendental philosophy is the one hope of an age which is sick to death of its own useless speculations. <insert>[pencil bracket on right]</insert>It requires no faith; it offers certain knowledge. But it is well that we should recognize the existence and closeness of its darker side.<insert>[/pencil bracket]</insert> It is also well for all who approach the subject, and desire to preserve the even balance of an unbiased and well-regulated mind, to discount much of the grandiose claims put forward by modern mystics concerning the ancient masters. High priests who are supposed by their admirers to have reached all heights ought surely to have enjoyed a freedom from the "cosmogonic" ignorance of their age, whereas their writings reveal them as by no means advanced students of physical science. <insert>[pencil bracket on left]</insert>Those who are believed to have stood on the threshold of the Divine might have expressed theological views of a<insert>[/pencil bracket]</insert>

<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

broader character; nevertheless, we find that in most questions of religion, they appear to have adhered to the doctrines that were current during their particular eras. These facts suggest limitations which will, we fear, be conclusive proof to many thinkers that the achievements of past adepts are somewhat imaginary in nature. However, a comparison of their claims with the known facts of modern psychology will establish the general truth of their statements; and the higher and unexplored possibilities that are indicated today by psychic science—and which are known to advanced experimentalists—will be found, upon comparison, to be precisely in the direction of the mystics' more exalted claims. But in spite of the achievements of some brilliant exceptions, there is little reason to suppose that the *<term>turba philosophorum</term>* <note>The Assembly of the Philosophers</note>, as a whole, did much more than extend our actual psychic experiments—mesmeric, hypnotic, and spiritual—to a marked but measurable distance beyond ourselves. It seems that the grand heights of occultism, of which we write and dream, were the Promised Land of their own aspirations and were not attained during their lives.*

This being the case, it is better for now to mainly confine our attention to the repeatable experiments of the mystics. For serious students, it is perhaps permissible—with extreme discrimination and caution—to extend the circle of investigation to some branches of <term>Black Magic</term> (such as the control of evil spirits), provided that knowledge, and not gain, is the only goal. The alleged dangers regarding magical practices are insignificant compared to the ends to be achieved, and the existing secret brother-

---

<note>* There is, however, an alternative view that is only fair to mention. It is argued that the hierophants of the past were forced by necessity to speak the cosmological language of their day. When transcendental doctrine was expressed in the language of current philosophy, the mystic could not seem *much* above the errors of the time. Otherwise, his superior science would have been even more incomprehensible. On the transcendental plane, he probably knew everything, but in physical science, he may have been limited by the knowledge of the period. In either case, the translation of secret wisdom for public use could only be accomplished by simplifying it to the public's level, both theologically and philosophically.</note>

<summary>The author argues that while past occultists were limited by the religious and scientific knowledge of their time, their psychic claims align with modern psychological discoveries. He suggests that students might cautiously explore even "Black Magic" for the sake of knowledge, as the potential rewards outweigh the risks.</summary>

# THE OCCULT SCIENCES.

hoods, more especially <term>Masonry</term>, which is founded on <term>esoteric doctrine</term>, could be utilized by the mystics in service to these ends.

The common assumptions established by modern science against the spiritual nature of man and the existence of spiritual intelligences outside the visible universe are based on a sharp distinction between the substances respectively called material and spiritual. This offers a unique instance of the verbal tyrannies to which we subject our minds. No greater source of intellectual misunderstanding and error can be found than what is contained in the two words <term>SPIRIT</term> and <term>MATTER</term>. Believers and skeptics alike have exhausted the methods of <term>philosophy</term> in their attempt to establish these two concepts at opposite poles of thought. It is now universally admitted that we are exclusively limited to an acquaintance with the appearances of this world which we call material; of matter in its ultimate nature, we know nothing whatever. No scientific analysis can throw light upon its eternally inscrutable problem. It is also admitted that, in the intellectual order, man realizes that he is a conscious being by a reflex and not a direct act, and the ultimate nature of the <term>ego</term> is a book permanently sealed. Once more, we are familiar only with certain modes of manifestation; the reality always escapes us. It is therefore impossible rationally to establish fundamental distinctions between substances about which we are fundamentally ignorant. Since, for all practical purposes, mind is identical with the vague concept which we call spirit, we may state the following axiom with complete truth:—*The distinction between matter and spirit is philosophically futile and frivolous.* A direct consequence follows: those who affirm the existence of matter and deny spirit may be unconsciously contradicting themselves. Although they are linked by the same mystery that surrounds them, it is, of course, philosophically impossible to claim they are identical in substance; therefore, withholding judgment is the only prudent course. This reservation must also be extended in another and important direction. Considering that the phenomena called <term>thaumaturgic</term>, <term>magical</term>

<summary>The author argues that the scientific distinction between matter and spirit is a linguistic fallacy since the ultimate nature of both remains unknown. Consequently, he suggests it is philosophically impossible to dogmatically deny the existence of the spiritual while accepting the material.</summary>
<keywords>Masonry, esoteric doctrine, spirit, matter, philosophy, ego, consciousness, dualism, thaumaturgic, magical</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical argument from page 6, exploring the idea that matter and spirit may be different manifestations of the same unknown substance.</meta>

<page-num>7</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

...and spiritual may be simply uninvestigated modes of manifestation in one force infinitely differentiated in the universe, it is unreasonable to deny their possibility on *à priori* grounds, for the possible modes of manifestation in an ultimately unknown substance cannot be theoretically limited. In fact, we are unconsciously led into pure *Spinozism*, for it is one of the tenets of this grand and unique thinker that matter and mind are but two finite manifestations of one infinite substance, which may be capable of an infinite number of other finite manifestations of which we can and do know nothing. It is outside the present scope of inquiry, but these considerations lead us to touch briefly on a still more important subject. The existence of a creative mind outside the visible universe, standing in relation to it as its almighty author and architect, has been debated for long ages without the possibility having occurred to debaters on either side that the efficient cause of matter and mind may be something so totally transcending them both—equally in nature and substance—that it cannot be identified with either. If the pantheistic identification of deity with the titanic forces of the Cosmos is a narrow and inadequate solution to the mystery of God, the anthropomorphic identification with mind is liable to the same objection.

If there is any philosophic accuracy in this method of reasoning, it follows that by virtue of our absolute and irretrievable ignorance concerning the fundamentals of matter and mind, mere speculations on the problems of life—on the possibility of intellectual existence without a physical organism (in other words, on the existence of disembodied humans and of hierarchies of spiritual beings above or below humanity)—can never result in any solid knowledge; they will always be speculations only, devoid of conviction and satisfaction. It follows, also, that there is no possible refuge from permanent agnosticism except by a formal act of faith in some system of alleged revelation, or by experimental research, if possible, into the nature and powers of the mind. But faith is no longer what it was in the days of St. Paul, “the substance of things hoped for, the

<summary>The author argues that matter and spirit may be different manifestations of a single, unknown infinite substance, rendering dogmatic distinctions between them irrational. Consequently, without revelation or experimental psychological research, questions regarding spiritual beings and life after death remain unsolvable through speculation alone.</summary>
<keywords>Spinozism, pantheism, agnosticism, matter and spirit, deity, anthropomorphism, St. Paul, philosophy of mind</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introduction’s argument regarding the limitations of speculative philosophy and the potential for a scientific approach to spiritual existence, moving from the possibility of disembodied intelligences to the claims of Hermeticism.</meta>

<page-num>8</page-num>
<header>THE OCCULT SCIENCES.</header>

"evidence of things not seen;" it is not a source of scientific conviction. It is aspiration formulated as a creed, and by its nature, it is unable to provide true intellectual certainty.

It is at this point that transcendental philosophy appears. In the name of a thousand histories and tens of thousands of traditions and legends, it declares it is possible to know through experimental research that disembodied humans can and do exist; that there are hierarchies of intelligence above and below humanity; and that in this life, and within this environment, the potential of the inner man may be so developed as to put him in communication with forms of intellectual existence that transcend his normal state. It claims that the positive knowledge resulting from such research can be attained now just as it was attained in the past, and that a scientific solution to the problems of life is actually within the reach of every earnest man.

Such are the claims of the <term>Hermetic philosophy</term>, and such is the scientific basis of mysticism. The spiritual future of the world may reasonably be considered to depend on these claims. On this basis—if on any—must the religion of the future be built, if by "religion" we mean the establishment of a vital and life-giving connection between that which is highest in man and that which is supreme in the universe. The choice lies between agnosticism and the science of the mystics. If mysticism is a true science, the prospect awaiting the psychic man is grand and boundless. If it is grounded in superstition and fraud, we may find a cold consolation even in agnosticism itself; for the mystery of the universe is so insoluble that no aspiration can be dismissed as completely impossible to fulfill. Even within that insoluble mystery, there is room for a faint hope.

<summary>The author argues that while faith lacks scientific certainty, Hermetic philosophy offers an experimental path to prove the existence of higher intelligences and the potential of the inner man. He posits that the future of religion depends on whether mysticism is a true science or if humanity must settle for the "chilly consolation" of agnosticism.</summary>
<keywords>transcendental philosophy, Hermetic philosophy, mysticism, agnosticism, psychic man, intellectual existence, spiritual hierarchies, experimental research</keywords>

<meta>This page marks the beginning of Part I, moving from the general philosophical justifications of the previous section to specific definitions of the occult sciences, establishing "Magic" as an umbrella term for various arcane practices.</meta>

# PART I.

## DEFINITIONS.

Every branch of the occult or secret sciences may be included under the word MAGIC, with the sole exception of astrology. As important and interesting as astrology is, it can hardly be called a branch of arcane wisdom, as it depends solely on complex astronomical calculations and on the assessment of those influences which are supposed to be spread by the planets and the starry heavens over the lives of nations and individuals.

But the doctrines concerning the nature and power of angels, ghosts, and spirits; the methods of summoning and controlling the spirits of the dead, elementary spirits, and demons; the creation of talismans; the production of gold through alchemy; all forms of divination, including crystal gazing; and all the mysterious calculations that constitute <term>Kabbalistic</term> science, are all parts of magic. It is necessary to make this statement at the beginning to prevent misunderstanding, because in an introductory handbook it would clearly cause confusion to include subjects that seem so different under a single general title;

and we have therefore decided to make a few introductory remarks about magic viewed as a whole, and then to treat each of its branches under specific titles. These will be easily understood by those who are seeking an introduction to the mysteries of the esoteric sciences for the first time.

The popular meaning attached to the term magic differs greatly from the interpretations offered by its students. By the term magic, according

The author explains the plan to categorize these subjects individually for clarity in this introductory text.</summary>

to common opinion, it generally implies one of two things: either that it is the art of producing effects through causes that seem inadequate to produce them, and are therefore in apparent defiance of the known laws of nature; or that it is the art of summoning spirits<note>Four classes of intelligences called "spirits" are recognized by the science of the magi. There are the *Angels*, who are the offspring of the first creation—created and not born. There are the Devils or *Demons*, the angelic hierarchies who fell from their original status. There are the *Elementary Spirits*, who inhabit the four elements of ancient physical science and are divided into *Sylphs*, *Undines*, *Gnomes*, and *Salamanders*—intelligences who reproduce their kind in the same way as humans. Finally, there are the *Souls of departed men and women*, whose actual location in the unseen world is described in various ways. Angels are summoned in the higher branches of white magic, and Devils in the practices of the black arts. Elementary Spirits are the classes most easily commanded, and they were the "familiars" of the Middle Ages. The Souls of the Dead were commonly summoned to reveal earthly secrets, occasionally to disclose future events, but most frequently out of the longing of those who are grieving.</note> and forcing them to do the operator's bidding. The second option can essentially be reduced to the first, because the summoning of invisible intelligences is inseparably connected in the common mind with a certain "hocus-pocus" of ridiculous rituals and formulas. These include the speaking of strange and, to them, meaningless words, which certainly seem inadequate to produce such a massive effect as a direct manifestation from a hidden side of Nature. 

Now, establishing communication with worlds that are normally beyond our reach is undoubtedly among the great claims of the magus. The art of summoning spirits, understood in its true and highest sense, is the pinnacle of Magic. However, it is not actually a violation of unchangeable natural laws; the causes set in motion by qualified initiates are truly adequate to the effects produced, as wonderful and incredible as they may seem. The popular idea of Magic—even when it isn't confused with the tricks of frauds and the pranks of charlatans—is entirely absurd and crude.

<page-num>11</page-num>
# DEFINITIONS.

in his *History of Magic*, “if anyone would condescend to return to its ancient origin, it could no longer be confused with the superstitions that slander its memory. Its name is derived for us from the Greek words *MAGOS*, a Magician, and *MAGEIA*, Magic, which are merely variations of the terms *MOG*, *MEGH*, *MAGH*, which in Pehlvi and in Zend—both languages of the ancient East—signify ‘priest,’ ‘wise,’ and ‘excellent.’ It was from there also that, in a period prior to historical Greece, the Chaldean name *Maghdim* originated, which is equivalent to ‘supreme wisdom’ or sacred philosophy. Thus, simple etymology indicates that Magic was the synthesis of those sciences once possessed by the Magi or philosophers of India, Persia, Chaldea, and Egypt, who were the priests of nature, the patriarchs of knowledge, and the founders of those vast civilizations whose ruins still support, without faltering, the burden of sixty centuries.”

Ennemoser, in his “History of Magic” (as translated by Howitt), says: “Among the Parsees, the Medes, and the Egyptians, a higher knowledge of nature was understood by the term Magic, with which religion, and particularly astronomy, were associated. The initiated and their disciples were called Magicians—that is, the Wise—which was also the case among the Greeks. . . . Plato understood Wisdom to be nothing less than a worship of the Divine, and Apuleius says that *Magus* means, in the Persian language, a priest. . . . India, Persia, Chaldea, and Egypt were the cradles of the oldest Magic. Zoroaster, Ostanes, the Brahmins, the Chaldean sages, and the Egyptian priests were the primitive possessors of its secrets. The priestly and sacrificial functions, the healing of the sick, and the preservation of the Secret Wisdom were the objects of their life. They were either princes themselves or surrounded princes as their counselors. Justice, truth, and the power of self-sacrifice were the great qualities with which each one of these had to be endowed; and the neglect of any one of these virtues was punished in the most cruel manner.”

<margin>✓</margin>
Dr. Franz Hartmann, a member of the advanced school who is said to be both a practical and theoretical student, also claims to have successfully performed obscure alchemical experiments by applying spiritual forces to material things. Therefore, he should at least be competent enough to provide us with an adequate definition of his art. At the beginning of one of his books, he makes the following assertion: “Whatever misinterpretation ancient or modern ignorance may have given to the word *Magic*, its only true meaning is *The Highest Science, or Wisdom, based upon knowledge and practical experience.*” This definition assigns an absolute value to a term that it does not historically possess. For although Magic is undoubtedly derived from a word that means Wisdom, it is equivalent only to Wisdom as it was understood by the Magi; and as far as philosophy is concerned, Magian Wisdom may or may not be identical to absolute and eternal Wisdom.

<margin>✓ [</margin>
Magic, says Éliphas Lévi, is “the traditional science of the secrets of Nature which has come down to us from the Magi,” a definition that is sensible and almost reaches perfection; its only flaw, we believe, is that it limits the source of esoteric knowledge solely to the Persian religious leaders.

Based on these definitions, it is clear that Magic is not merely the art of summoning spirits, nor is it only concerned with establishing communication with other forms of intelligent existence in the countless realms of the transcendental. If such summoning is possible, and if such communication can truly be established, it is clearly through the intervention of certain hidden forces within the person communicating—that is, man. Now, it is reasonable to assume that these same forces can be applied in other directions. Magic, in the full, perfect, and comprehensive sense of that much-abused term, consists of the synthesis of methods and processes by which these forces are used in various fields of experiment, combined with a further synthesis of methods
<margin>]</margin>
and processes by which the hidden potential of various physical substances is developed into active manifestation.

<page-num>13</page-num>
<header>DEFINITIONS. 13</header>

...ated from the word Magic its original and sublime significance. Once, in the dead language of starry Chaldea—the solemn sanctuary and the cradle, if not the birthplace, of the sciences called transcendental—it was the equivalent of supreme wisdom. Once its practitioners were the priests, the wise, and the excellent; but the science has become confused with the deceptions that have surrounded its history, and its initiates are now identified with a rabble of rogues and charlatans. So it would almost seem as if the term Magic has become a word whose accepted meaning is a libel against the science it signifies and a slur on the memory of its great masters. Fortunately, however, it is not the only term used to describe that science; esoteric wisdom, occult knowledge, and transcendental philosophy and practice are interchangeable terms that all signify Magic. They are used indiscriminately throughout this volume, less to avoid repetition than to minimize the negative impact of a word that is now degraded by connecting it with synonyms that reflect its original and true significance.

We have already explained in the Introduction to this work what we believe to be the goals of the current revival of mysticism and the exact nature of its claims for the consideration of the nineteenth century. The origin and destiny of humanity are the absorbing and vital problems that, in the present age, demand a complete and satisfactory solution more urgently than ever. It is claimed that Magic offers such a solution. That science affirms that latent energies, undeveloped faculties, and generally unknown possibilities actually reside within human beings. Through their effective development, it is said that the horizon of a person's energies and perceptions can be so enlarged that they extend over a new range of existence. It is demonstrated that one's physical body is not the real self; that a person can transcend it without destroying it, and can establish a direct connection with countless forms of intelligence who are separated from their perishable bodies, as well as with others of every rank who have never been joined with flesh. The longing of the ages is promised complete fulfillment in this sublime program of a forgotten knowledge.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the definition of Magic, moving from its historical terminology to the psychological and ceremonial methods used by past masters to achieve spiritual communication and exaltation.</meta>

The psychological experiments of the past masters of mysticism are alleged to have brought them into communication with various classes of intelligences, such as angels, elementary spirits, demons, and the disembodied souls of men. The fundamental principle of this communication was the exercise of a certain occult force residing in the Magus and strenuously exerted to establish such a connection between two planes of Nature as would achieve his desired end. This exertion was termed the evocation, conjuration, or calling of the spirit, but what was actually raised was the energy of the inner man; tremendously developed and exalted by combined will and aspiration, this energy produced by sheer force a new intellectual faculty of perceptible psychological awareness, and enabled the prepared mystic to see into a new world and communicate with its various populations. To assist and to stimulate this energy into the most powerful possible operation, artificial means were almost always used. The ordinary faculties and senses were worked upon, and frequently the narrow line that lies between exaltation and frenzy was crossed in the boldness of the process. The appeal to the senses by a magnificent and overwhelming ritual, which has been used with great success in the hierarchical religions of Christianity, was also made by the hierarchical magic of the past. <margin>Pencil bracket in the margin from "The synthesis..." to "...spiritual nature."</margin>The synthesis of these methods and processes was called <insert>Ceremonial Magic</insert>, which in effect was a tremendous forcing-house for the latent faculties of man’s spiritual nature. Undoubtedly the goal was occasionally accomplished by violent and unnatural means, for intellectual exaltation can be achieved by laudanum and hashish as much as by divine grace applied to the soul; but the ethical value of the goal cannot be challenged by the use of discreditable methods, though the operator may be personally discredited and permanently harmed by them.

The gospel according to the mystics has, as will be seen, its darker side. Just as the known forces of modern material science can be used to preserve or destroy, so can the arcane powers developed by magic be directed to a good or an evil end. In the suggestive language of

According to the alchemists, coals may be turned into gold, but it is also possible to convert precious metal into coal. We can rise into communion with the exalted understanding of the angels; we can sink into correspondence with the psychic deformities of the devils; we can compose the Universal Medicine and the hidden poison of the second death.

There is white and there is black magic. <insert>The lawful application of the secret forces known to esoteric science constitutes **White Magic**; the lawless and vicious application of these same forces is the **Black** or infernal Art.</insert>

The authority of the law resides in the intention and will of the practitioner. That which is well-intended must eventually work well. Actions must be appraised by their intention and not by their effect alone, just as the significance of words is extended, contracted, or changed by reference to their linguistic origin. <insert>**Black Magic** has two dominant elements—the diabolical, and the superstitious or absurd. The use of the term "diabolical" is not to be interpreted in a strictly theological sense.</insert>

The contrast provided by the labels "white" and "black" may justify their use, but such symbolic language has frequently been misapplied. We also have "red magic," which is characterized as the "cream of the secret sciences," along with other imaginative designations.

As it is impossible to conclusively determine that such heights have ever been truly scaled, the modern mystic would be unwise to insist on their existence, though he may feel personally assured of the fact.

For merely historical references to magic among the Arabs, the Romans, the Chinese, the early Christians, the Egyptians, the Greeks, the Israelites, the Germans, the Laplanders, and the Orientals, the reader is referred to Ennemoser's work, translated by Howitt and published under the title of "The History of Magic." There is also a learned work, translated in 1877 from the French of Lenormant and called "Chaldean Magic," in which some curious incantations in the cuneiform character are translated. Del Rio's *Disquisitionum Magicarum*, a volume of 1,100 pages published in 1657, is an early authority and contains a chapter on fascination. An antiquarian work called "Narratives of Sorcery and Magic from the Most Authentic Sources" was published by Thomas Wright, F.S.A., in 1851. Francis Barrett's "The Magus," an expensively illustrated work printed in 1801, is the work of one who styled himself a "professor" of occult science. In France, the writings of Eliphas Lévi—especially his *Histoire de la Magie* and his *Dogme et Rituel de la Haute Magie*—are justly esteemed.

Pererius in 1598, Tiedemann in 1787, Freytag in 1710, and Christophorus in 1711 have all written tracts on the subject of magic which are sometimes encountered.

<sig>B</sig>

# WHITE MAGIC.

## THE EVOCATION OF ANGELS.

THE highest orders of intelligence known to the Christian mystics—derived, like their general conceptions of the Cosmos of matter and mind, from their Jewish initiators—were the messengers of Scriptural tradition, who are called in the Greek *angelos*, and in the Hebrew *malak*. The role of the ambassador has long been removed from our conception of these beings, who, in a condition of ecstatic adoration and in an unchanging permanence of bliss, seem to have outlived their *raison d’être* <note>Reason for existence</note>. According to the Rabbinical commentary on Genesis, written by Rabbi Jacob, the angels have no free will, “for they, being of a pure understanding, and having an inclination to good only, cannot be otherwise than good;” and this is an actual doctrine of the Latin church. In practice, however, the angels of Catholic history and legend, in spite of their consummate perfection, would appear to rejoice in the full possession of that freedom which an abstract theology denies them. They respond to invocations, they perform miracles, they gather the prayers of the faithful, they watch over human beings, and they engage everlastingly in a spiritual warfare with the emissaries of perdition and darkness.

This practical aspect of orthodox angelology corresponds to that of western magic, both being undoubtedly derived from the ancient faith of Israel, which in turn was indebted for the elements of its spiritual hypotheses to Egypt, Babylon, and Chaldea. It was from there that the doctrine of the Incommunicable Name, with its powers and virtues, was derived by Rabbinical theosophists. The latter evolved out of its various combinations—

<margin>Handwritten bracket in the left margin</margin>
The <term>Incommunicable Name</term> came to be considered a fountain of mysterious power, and, by an easy and natural transition, it was eventually regarded as the source of life. The letters that composed it were considered especially productive in the creation of intelligence, enlightenment, and harmony. A measure of their power was extended to the entire <term>Hebrew alphabet</term>, which was endowed with a profound mystical significance. It is necessary to explain this point in order to understand the angelic doctrines of the <term>Jewish Magi</term>. Each of the alphabet symbols represented a vital and creative principle residing in the intelligible world. Just as the vocabulary of a great language is developed from the letters of an ordinary alphabet, so from the mysterious powers signified by the Hebrew signs, the variations of an infinite existence were divinely elaborated. Now, the assumption of an exact correspondence between the mysterious powers in question and the signs by which they were represented—as well as between the inexhaustible life-forces of the Cosmos and the language developed from those signs—constituted the magical character of the Jewish language.

<margin>✓ || (red ink annotation)</margin>
In an interesting and valuable manuscript entitled "The <term>Cabalistic Science</term>, or the <term>Art of Knowing the Good Genies</term>," there is the following explanation of the mysteries contained in the <term>Hebrew alphabet</term>.

By the sequence of symbols extending from <term>Aleph</term> to <term>Jod</term> was symbolized the <term>Angelic World</term>, or the hierarchy of sovereign intelligences directly derived from the first Eternal Light, and attributed to <term>Jod</term> in primary and superior correspondence. By the sequence from <term>Caf</term> to <term>Tsad</term> were represented the several orders of angels who inhabit the visible, or astronomical and astrological worlds, mystically attributed to <term>Jah</term>; each individual sphere was under the special protection of a particular presiding intelligence. The sequence from <term>Tsad</term> to <term>Thau</term> is in mysterious correspondence with the elementary world, which is attributed by philosophers to <term>Jaho</term> in the paramount

order. The destinies of humanity are dispensed in the elementary sphere, and its angelic intelligences preside over animated Nature.

Orthodox Christian theosophy enumerates *nine choirs of angelic beings* which differ from each other in order and in degree of glory, but are all of the same nature. In the mystical writings attributed to the apostolic Areopagite, an account of these hierarchies is given in an extended form, and it is undoubtedly to this transcendentalist that theology is indebted for the angelic doctrines subsequently developed by St. Thomas. Now the advanced mystic who borrowed the name of *Dionysius* was indebted in turn to the Kabbalists for his pneumatic hypotheses, as will be seen in the following tabulation of the attributes of the Divine Names, and of the intelligences in correspondence with each of the Hebrew letters.

I. The first letter of the Hebrew alphabet is called *Aleph*; it corresponds with *EHEIEH*, the source name of God, which is interpreted as *Divine Essence*. Its seat is in the world called *Ensoph*, which signifies Infinity, and its attribute is *Keter*, the Crown. It rules over the Angels called by the Hebrews *Haioth-ha-Kodesch*, or The Living Creatures of Holiness, who are otherwise named Seraphim, and constitute the first and supreme choir.

II. The second letter is *Beth*, and the Divine name which corresponds to it is *BACHOUR*, or *electus juvenis* <note>chosen youth</note>. It is the sign of the *Ophanim*, who are the Angels of the second order, and the Cherubim of exoteric theology. By their ministry, Jehovah unfolded and cleared the primordial chaos.  
<margin>|</margin>The attribute of BACHOUR is *Hocmah*, or Divine Wisdom.

III. The third letter is *Ghimel*. It corresponds to the name *GADOL*, which signifies Grand or Great, and is assigned to the Angels of the third order whom the Hebrews called *Aralym*, the mighty and strong. These are the Thrones of the Kabbalists, and the third choir. By their ministry Tetragrammaton Elohim establishes and maintains the form of the Fluidic Matter. Its attribute is Binah, or Intelligence.

By their ministry, the elements were evolved by <term>Elohim-Gibor</term>, whose numeration is <term>Pachad</term>, which signifies Fear and Judgment, and whose attribute is <term>Geburah</term>, which signifies Strength and Power.

VI. The sixth letter is <term>Vau</term>, from which is developed the name <term>VEZIO</term>, *cum splendore* <note>with splendor</note>; it stands for the Angels of the sixth order, the <term>Malakim</term>, or <term>Virtues</term>, by whose ministry <term>Eloah-Vaudahat</term> produces the metals and other substances which belong to the mineral kingdom. His attribute is <term>Tiphereth</term>, which signifies Beauty and Splendor.

VII. The seventh letter is <term>Zain</term>, which originates the name <term>ZAKAI</term>, equivalent to *purus mundus* <note>pure world</note>. It corresponds to the Angels of the seventh order, the Kabbalistic <term>Children of Elohim</term>, who are the <term>Principalities</term> of the orthodox faith. By their ministry, the vegetable kingdom was produced by <term>Tetragrammaton-Sabaoth</term>, whose attribute is <term>Nezah</term>, which, interpreted, is Triumph and Justice.

VIII. The eighth letter is called <term>Cheth</term>. It designates the name <term>CHASID</term>, which is equivalent to *misericors* <note>merciful</note>. It corresponds to the Angels of the eighth choir, the <term>Bene-Elohim</term>, or <term>Sons of God</term>, who are identical with the archangelic host. By their ministry, the animal creation was developed by <term>Elohim-Sabaoth</term>, whose attribute is <term>Hod</term>, which is Praise.

IX. The ninth letter is <term>Teth</term>. It corresponds to <term>TAHOR</term>, or the *Mundus purus* <note>pure world</note>, and to the <term>Cherubim</term> or ninth choir of Angels, who preside at the birth of man

X. The tenth letter is <term>*Jod*</term>, which gives power to the name <term>JAH</term>, which is equivalent to *Deus* <note>God</note>. It designates the tenth numeration of the Hebrews—<term>*Adonay-Melech*</term>, or the God-King, whose attributes are the Kingdom, the Temple, and the Empire. Its influences extend to the <term>*Issim*</term>—strong, happy, and blessed men located in the sphere of the spirit. Through their ministry, intelligence, industry, and the knowledge of divine things descend as an influx to embodied humanity.

The Angelic World is completed with the tenth letter, but the rest of the Hebrew alphabet corresponds to individual princes of intelligence who are governors of innumerable hosts, each playing an important part in the economy of the mystical universe. <term>*Mettatron*</term> corresponds with <term>*Caf*</term>, the eleventh letter; through his ministry, the perceptible world receives divine virtues. He belongs to the first Heaven of the Astronomical World. There is also the final <term>*Caf*</term> <note>the form of the letter used at the end of a word</note>, which corresponds to the Intelligences of the second order who govern the Heaven of the fixed stars, and especially the zodiacal signs. Their supreme chief is <term>*Raziel*</term>. <term>*Lamed*</term>, the twelfth letter, corresponds to the Intelligences of the third Heaven who preside over the sphere of Saturn. Their lord is <term>*Schebtaïel*</term>, whose attribute is the Hidden God. <term>*Mem*</term>, which is the thirteenth letter, corresponds to the fourth Heaven, or the sphere of Jupiter. The sovereign Intelligence who governs this planet is called <term>*Tsadkiel*</term>. There is also the final <term>*Mem*</term>, which is analogous to the fifth Heaven, or the sphere of Mars, with <term>*Camaël*</term> as its supreme Intelligence. He is the strength and fire of God and presides over many princes. <term>*Nun*</term> is the fourteenth letter and corresponds to the sixth Heaven, which is that of the Sun. Haniel as its sovereign lord, who represents the love, justice, and grace of God. Samech, the fifteenth letter, corresponds to the eighth Heaven—which is that of the planet Mercury—and is governed by Michael. The sixteenth letter is Oin, which is analogous to the ninth Heaven, the sphere of the Moon, governed by the messenger-intelligence Gabriel. The astrological and astronomical worlds end with this letter, and the following sequence of arcane correspondences concerns the Elementary Plane.

Thus Phé, the seventeenth letter of the Hebrew alphabet, refers to the first of the mystical elements, which is held to be Fire, and its sovereign Intelligence is Seraphim. The final Phé corresponds to Air, which is the home of the Sylphs, whose lord is Cherubim. Tsadé, the eighteenth letter, refers to Water, which is the home of the Nymphs. The Queen of the Nymphs is Tharsis. Koph is the nineteenth letter; it corresponds to Earth, which is the sphere of the Gnomes, with Ariel as its presiding Intelligence. Resh, which is the twentieth letter, applies to the animal kingdom, including humanity; Schin corresponds to all vegetable substances; and Tau, the last symbol of the Hebrew alphabet, refers to the world of minerals.*

In addition to the celestial hosts enumerated in the table above, the mystical calculations of the Hebrews establish the existence of other angelic sequences—for example, the angels who surround the Great White Throne, whose names are entirely derived from three mysterious verses in the fourteenth chapter of Exodus. Such elaborations of many titles, created through Kabbalistic computations and endowed with corresponding powers and specific periods of influence, were valued by rabbinical writers as containing the keys to the government of the universe.

It is not necessarily for their own merits that we have included these speculations in a practical handbook of occultism, although they are held in almost total veneration by a certain section

# WHITE MAGIC.

...of modern mystics. But they are vitally important for establishing the exact nature of Kabbalistic concepts regarding those worlds of invisible intelligences whose outer edges are adjacent to our own horizon. They had glimpses of these worlds just as we do, but they had little real knowledge and could formulate no adequate hypothesis. The inadequacy of their speculation is embarrassing to those who believe in the infallibility of the Hebrew mystics, but it offers no obstacle to those who are investigating the actual scope of ancient knowledge concerning the facts of psychology and who can distinguish the landmarks of true experimental progress amid a wilderness of distraught speculations. <insert>handwritten bracket mark</insert>

The rites to be used in the conjuration of the more exalted intelligences are found in the "Key of Solomon," an excellent edition of which has been issued by Mr. Mathers. Intelligences of this nature are generally called angels, but they often take on the character of superior elementary spirits, and this is undoubtedly the case with most of those who are supposed to be controlled by the commands and threats of the Magus.

<margin>v</margin> An anonymous German work published at Frankfurt in 1686, entitled *Theosophia Pneumatica*, appears to contain, in a relatively small space, the most satisfactory formulas for the invocation of the supreme angels. A manuscript translation made by Dr. J. M. Rieder has recently come into our hands, and we have made it the basis for the following citations. Students will notice that it is somewhat similar to the treatise by Arbatel on "Magic." The classifications and names of the angels, as they exist in this curious work, do not correspond with those already given; but as it is acknowledged by mystics that the true names—which is equivalent to saying the real and ultimate natures—of all unseen beings are inaccessible to human research, importance should not be placed on any of these variations. Titular distinctions in matters of magical practice have little more than literary utility. It is said that once in the presence of an angel, the soul has no need for speech, much less for the ordinary methods of social address.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the *Theosophia Pneumatica* and its classification of angelic beings, transitioning from the introductory context of mystical criticism to the specific hierarchy of the Olympic spirits.</meta>

# THE OCCULT SCIENCES.

The magical treatise in question has the common disability of all works of its age and class; its aspirations, its intentions, and its practical value as a ritual must be carefully separated from its crude philosophical setting. Whatever spiritual knowledge the Magus of old attained, it was seemingly insufficient to raise him above the intellectual limitations of his time, and it must always be remembered that the modern scheme of mystical criticism—which seeks to account for such obvious philosophical inadequacies as exist in the ancient mystics by assuming that, despite superior attainments, they condescended for concealed reasons to support current opinions, scientific or religious—is without any actual warrant in known fact.

In "Pneumatic Magic," the names of the angels are classified under the title of Olympian, or Celestial spirits, who dwell in the firmament and the supreme constellations: "it is their function to acknowledge the Fates and to administer the inferior destinies. Each Olympic spirit accomplishes and teaches whatever is foretold by the star in which he is placed. Yet he can do nothing of his own power, nor without a special command from God.

> “The heavens and their inhabitants come voluntarily to man and often serve even against the will of man, but how much more if we implore their ministry. That evil and troublesome spirits also approach men is accomplished by the cunning of the devil, at times by conjuration or attraction, and frequently as a penalty for sins; therefore, he who would abide in familiarity with celestial intelligences shall take pains to avoid every serious sin; he shall diligently pray for the protection of God to vanquish the impediments and schemes of <term>Diabolus</term>, and God will ordain that the devil himself shall work to the direct profit of the <term>Theosophist</term>.

<term>Arathron</term> is the celestial spirit of <term>Saturn</term>; he can manage natural things prepared by astrological influences; he may change everything into stone, whether animals or plants, though they will preserve their exterior appearance. He changes treasures into coals and coals into treasures. He provides familiar spirits with definite power. He teaches <term>alchemy</term>, <term>magic</term>, and <term>natural philosophy</term>. He joins men to <term>gnomes</term> and earth spirits. He renders people invisible. He governs fertility and conception; he teaches the discovery of lead, its manipulation, and its change into gold. He teaches the art of curing the diseases of smaller animals, such as goats, poultry, etc. He gives news of prisoners and of sick people, dispatches ministering spirits who serve in his own manner, enlarges the understanding, gives excellent advice on all elevating subjects, and is most exact in his calculations.

He must be invoked on a Saturday, at the first hour of sunset during the waxing moon.

<term>Bethor</term> administers the influence of <term>Jupiter</term>; he who can obtain his assistance may rise to the highest dignities; he dispenses treasures, subjects the spirits of the air to the <term>Magus</term>, and gives true answers. These intelligences carry all things—even precious stones and marvelous medicines—from one place to another. The spirit of <term>Jupiter</term> gives other ministering spirits of the firmament: he can extend life to seven hundred years, God willing. He has subject to him forty-two kings, thirty-five princes, twenty-eight dukes, twenty-one counselors, fourteen servants, seven messengers, and two thousand nine hundred legions of spirits; he instructs judges in the administration of equal justice to the poor as well as to the rich; he inspires his <term>Magus</term> with the love of justice, gives him true, vision-filled dreams, and assists in the attainment of venerable offices and dignities. He gives understanding to the old, and even to fools and the simple-minded, strengthens a weak memory, beautifies men under his influence, endows them with eloquence before princes and the great of this world, and makes them gentle,

<meta>This page concludes the description of the spirits' powers, continuing from the previous page's discussion of the lunar spirit Phul, and transitions into the requirements and spiritual exercises necessary for a Magus to communicate with these angelic intelligences.</meta>

...according to the ordinary course of nature, or by the more mystical exercise of willpower, and each can achieve in a very short time what would otherwise require much time and preparation without his intervention. Each of these intelligences must be invoked between seven and eight in the morning, or between two and three in the afternoon, on any day of the week, but only during the hours he governs while the moon is waxing.

Simply knowing the names and duties of these spirits is not enough to enter into a state of communication with their high intelligence. Many preparations for the successful <term>evocation</term> of angels are described by the authority I have cited, some of which are systematically listed in the following way:

### I.

The <term>Talmid</term> (otherwise the <term>Magus</term>, in his earlier grade of initiation) shall meditate day and night on the method of attaining a true knowledge of God—not only through the Word which has been revealed since the beginning of the world, but also through the laws of the <term>Cosmos</term> and the wonderful practical secrets that may be learned from the study of God's visible and invisible creatures.

### II.

The <term>Magus</term> shall learn to know himself, to distinguish between his mortal and immortal parts, and the realms to which they each belong.

### III.

Through his immortal nature, he shall strive to love God, and to adore and fear Him in spirit and in truth. But in his mortal body, he must also strive to be useful to his neighbors and glorify his Creator. These are the highest commandments of Magic, by which you shall obtain true <term>Pneumatology</term> through Divine Truth and Wisdom, and you shall be served by angelic beings not only in secret, but openly and face to face.

<meta>This page continues the numbered instructions for the aspiring Magus, transitioning from the internal preparations of the soul to the practical discernment of one's calling, the requirement of secrecy, and the boundaries between divine magic and superstition.</meta>

<page-num>30</page-num>
<header>THE OCCULT SCIENCES</header>

### IV.

As everyone is destined from his mother's womb for a certain way of life, the disciple should pause to consider whether he is born for Magic, and to which branch. By noting the degree of success in the different areas of operation, he may soon determine the second point.

### V.

An aspiring <term>Magus</term> shall be discreet and faithful; he shall never reveal what he has been told by a spirit. Daniel was commanded to set a seal on several matters; Paul was forbidden to reveal what he beheld in his ecstasy. The importance of this rule cannot be exaggerated.

### VI.

Say very frequently with David:
> Do not take your Holy Spirit of strength away from me. Lead me not into temptation.

### VII.

Accustom yourself to the <term>evocation</term> <note>The act of calling forth or summoning a spirit or deity.</note> of spirits. You cannot gather grapes from thorns, nor figs from thistles. Test all things and hold fast to that which is good. Avoid everything that is contrary to the will of God.

### VIII.

Avoid all superstition, which consists of attributing divine power to things which lack divinity, and in the acceptance of divine service without a command from God. The illusions of diabolical magic, which claims for the prince of darkness the worship due to God, are included under this name.

### IX.

Avoid the deceitful imitations of the devil, who, by the perversion of the <term>theurgic</term> powers concealed in the Word of God, becomes a fraudulent imitator of things which belong to God.

<header>WHITE MAGIC.</header>

When these conditions have been fulfilled, and the practitioner begins the practice, he should devote a special preliminary period to profound contemplation on the serious and sacred business which he has voluntarily undertaken. He must strive to present himself before his Supreme Teacher with a pure heart, a clean mouth, and innocent hands: "he must bathe and cleanse his body of all impurity, wear freshly washed garments, confess his sins, and abstain from wine and all sexual immorality" for a period of three days. He should also give alms to the poor, and when the eve of the operation has finally arrived, he must eat moderately at noon and have only bread and water for supper. Then on the following day, he must seek a secluded and pure location where, free from observation, he can recite the prescribed invocations, *e.g.*:

->*Prayer.*<-

<margin>X</margin>
> Holy, Holy Father! Increase my faith and strengthen me so that I may believe steadfastly in Your willingness to grant me what I am now asking through Jesus Christ Your Son. Amen.

And further:—

When the spiritual exaltation of the <term>Magus</term> has been accomplished by these and other prayers (varied, of course, to suit the purpose intended), and by various ceremonial practices, the spirit is, in magical language, compelled to appear. That is to say, the operator has passed into a condition where it would be as impossible for a spirit to remain invisible to him as for an ordinary mortal to conceal himself from our common sight, without an intervening shelter, in the blaze of a noonday sun.

When the angel has arrived, continues the Ritual, briefly make known what you wish, write down his answer; do not ask more than three questions; preserve what you learn in your memory, and consider it as sacred. As soon as your answer has been obtained, say to the angel:—

> “Since you came peacefully, thanks be to God the Lord, in whose name you are commissioned, return now in peace to your rank and sphere, and come again when I invoke you according to the order and functions which the Almighty has granted you. Amen.”

Thus we see that the magician of old who had any desire unfulfilled, through his knowledge of the different spiritual potencies and their respective functions, was able—by evoking that one whose powers corresponded to his own desire—to obtain whatever he sought. # WHITE MAGIC.

## THE EVOCATION OF THE SPIRITS OF THE ELEMENTS.

To the old magical doctrine of the Spirits of the Elements, we owe some of the most graceful and enchanting fictions in literature. It provided Alexander Pope with the supernatural machinery for his matchless “Rape of the Lock,” while an old Latin treatise by Paracelsus suggested the foundation for “Undine,” one of the most charming and delicate of all German fairy tales. Oriental romance is also filled with the active wonders of these and similar beings, for the Peris, Jinn, and Afreets of Arabian imaginative writers are essentially the same as Western concepts regarding Elementary Spirits.

The possible existence of such intelligences has been seriously debated even by the scientific writers of this era. In the book “Unseen Universe,” a chapter is filled with speculations about the association of delicate cosmic processes in this visible universe with the operations of unseen intelligences living within it.

The belief in the connection of personal agents with the more startling natural phenomena is admitted to have been widely prevalent during the Middle Ages, when imagination peopled the “four elements” with intelligences—normally unseen—“some of them friendly to man, some of them his deadly enemies. They are powerful and conscious of their power, but at the same time profoundly and mournfully aware that they are without a soul. Their life depends upon the continuation of some natural object, and therefore they have no immortality. Sometimes, however, an elementary spirit obtains a soul by means of a loving union with someone of the human race. At other times, the reverse

<summary>The author discusses the influence of the "Spirits of the Elements" on literature and science, explaining their role in folklore and their status as powerful but soulless beings.</summary>
<keywords>White Magic, Spirits of the Elements, Paracelsus, Undine, Alexander Pope, Rape of the Lock, Peris, Jinn, Afreets, Unseen Universe, four elements, immortality, Elementary Spirits</keywords>

...happens, and the soul of the mortal is lost. This mortal, leaving the haunts of men, associates with those soulless, but often amiable and affectionate beings.

On the other hand, there are writers who, even today, believe, or claim to believe, that "the planets of our system may be directed in their <insert>red underline</insert>continual speaking changes by their several crowds of governing spirits"—that "spirits are everywhere the directors of matter"—that "there exist upon the earth reasonable creatures other than man, having like him a body and soul, being born and dying like him"—that these are "the forces of the wind, the fire, and the flood"—that, in fact, whatever superstition generated in the past, or whatever imagination has endowed with a home and a name, has an invisible but certain existence. The doctrine of planetary intelligences has been supported as hypothetically possible even by Mr. W. S. Lilly, who is one of the most cultured and thoughtful of later philosophical writers.

The idea of living forces existing in the elements is <insert>red ink opening bracket</insert>not merely of medieval, or even of Gnostic, origin, <insert>red ink closing bracket and underline under "not merely"</insert> for this belief was cherished in the earliest ages of the world's history. As a proof of this, we may point to two very ancient Akkadian or early Assyrian incantations, addressed to the Elements of Fire and of Water. The originals were translated by Mr. Ernest Budge, the Egyptologist, and published in "The Records of the Past."

<insert>double vertical red line in margin</insert>The 108th chapter of the Ancient Egyptian "Ritual of the Dead," the "Per-M-Hru," is called "the Chapter of Knowing the Spirits of the West." Several of the chapters which immediately follow are devoted to the knowledge of the Spirits of various denominations: the Spirits of the East, the Spirits of Tu, the Spirits of An, etc.

Thus we see that the most ancient nations believed in the existence of certain living forces of Nature, whose chiefs they named and invoked; and we shall find the same ideas prevalent in both Scandinavian and Indian mythology.

The discerning disciple of mysticism, however, while admitting the complete possibility of the existence...

...of intelligences both inferior and superior to humanity, will do well to remember that some of the elaborate hypotheses concerning them are devoid of scientific value. These are structures equally clumsy and inadequate which have been based upon partial experiment.

The most popular presentation of the doctrine of Elementary Spirits is found in a little book entitled the “*Comte de Gabalis*,” a work of the Abbé de Villars, published at the close of the last century. It possesses the merit—which is rare in a popular handbook—of being quite representative and accurate so far as it goes; and although it is so doubtful in its character as to have frequently passed for a satire, it is an excellent tract for citation within its individual lines.

<insert>vertical red ink bracket in margin</insert>It is sufficient for our purpose to state—what is very generally known—that the four official elements have from time immemorial been supposed to swarm with a variety of, on the whole, sub-human intelligences, who are formally grouped into four broad species. The air is inhabited by the amiable race of <term>Sylphs</term>, the sea by the delightful and beautiful <term>Undines</term>, the earth by the industrious race of swarthy <term>Gnomes</term>, and the fire by the exalted and glorious nation of <term>Salamanders</term>, who are supreme in the elementary hierarchy. There is a close analogy in the natures of all these intelligences with the more lofty constitution of certain angelic choirs; for in the “Compendious Apology for the Society called <term>Rosicrucian</term>,” written by <term>Robert Fludd</term>, it is stated that the <term>Seraphim</term>, <term>Virtues</term>, and <term>Powers</term> are of a fiery character, the <term>Cherubim</term> are terrestrial, the <term>Thrones</term> and <term>Archangels</term> are aquatic, while the <term>Dominations</term> and <term>Principalities</term> are aerial.

“When you shall be lifted among the children of the philosophers,” says the “*Comte de Gabalis*,” “when your eyes shall have been fortified with the use of the most sacred medicine, you will immediately discover that the elements are inhabited by singularly perfect beings, of whose knowledge the sin of Adam has deprived his most wretched posterity. That immense space which intervenes between earth and heaven has far more noble inhabitants than birds or gnats; those vast seas have in—

Yet you cannot help but pity these unfortunate creatures when I tell you that their souls are mortal, and that they have no hope of enjoying that Eternal Being whom they know and religiously adore. Made of the purest parts of the elements which they inhabit, and having no conflicting qualities, they live, it is true, for many ages; yet what is time in comparison with eternity? They must eventually return to the abyss of nothingness. This thought afflicts them so much that it is frequently hard to console them. But God, whose

> "The Salamanders are composed of the most subtle parts of the sphere of fire, massed together and organized by the universal fire—so called because it is the principle of all the motions of Nature. In the same manner, the Sylphs are composed of the purest atoms of air, Nymphs of the most ethereal particles of water, and Gnomes of the most refined earth. The original Adam was in communication with these perfect creatures because, being composed of the finest materials of the four elements, he contained in himself the physical perfection of each of these four races, and was therefore their natural king. But when sin had cast him down among the dregs of the four elements, this harmony was shattered; he became coarse and impure, and no longer bore any resemblance to these pure and subtle substances."

Though not definitely stated in the *Comte de Gabalis*, it is obvious that the goal of magical communication with Elemental Spirits is to restore our Edenic connection between man and the harmonious world of spirits, which in effect would be a restoration of the celestial condition of original humanity. Now, the traditions of primitive perfection and golden splendor may possibly be untrue regarding the past, but they are certainly prophetic for the future; they are the ultimate stage of evolution predicted in remote periods, and while taking form as memories of a vanished glory, they are also a dim foreshadowing of a glory which is still to come.

The direction for Salamandrine perfection is provided in the “*Comte de Gabalis*;” it is completely unintelligible in its literal sense, while the lighthearted nature of the entire work will not reasonably justify the assumption that it contains a secret meaning, though it may be admitted that its wording is suggestive from a mystical standpoint. The recipe is as follows:—If you would 
<margin>A red pencil bracket marks the following recipe text.</margin>
regain control over the Salamanders, purify and exalt the NATURAL FIRE that is within you. Nothing is required for this purpose but the concentration of the FIRE OF THE WORLD by means of concave mirrors in a glass globe. In that globe is formed a SOLARY POWDER, which being of itself purified from the mixture of other elements, and being prepared according to ART, becomes in a very short time a sovereign process for the exaltation of the *Fire that is within you*, and will transform you into a fiery nature.

It is clear that a great part of the secret and mystery lies in the nature of the mirrors and in the nature of the glass vessel, and those who devote themselves to the unraveling of this Gordian Knot should direct their special attention to these points, which, if allegory may be credibly assumed, should not be very far to find.

We are assured by the Abbé de Villars that by using this recipe the inhabitants of the Sphere of Fire will become our inferiors, and delighted to behold our mutual harmony restored, they will have the same love and friendship for us that they have for their own kind.

Amidst the rich imagination of these fantastic daydreams, the student will do well to remember that the experiments of the old magicians brought them into communication with many classes of otherworldly intelligence, and that they developed many hypotheses to account for them.

But it must be further remembered that among Elemental Spirits themselves there exist many classes and natures in each category. For instance; in the case of the Fire-Spirits there are those which are friendly, hostile, and neutral regarding humanity; there are those which are composed of a purer essence, sharing in the nature

Then there are those again who are pure living Spirits of Flame, operating in all things of a fiery nature and directing the currents of that element. Besides all these, there are those who more closely resemble the spirits of fairy-lore, and who may perhaps be best described as a species of fiery humanity having a more physical organization than certain other classes. Yet again, there are some whose natures form a link between the Spiritual World and the Vegetable Kingdom; others again control the fiery nature in the Mineral World; and others are volcanic in nature and operation, for which reason they were called "Ætneans" by certain medieval writers. 

But even these classes are few in comparison with the enormous number of subdivisions in each Element alone. And besides this, it must be remembered that there are millions upon millions of Elemental Spirits of the Planets, of the Zodiac, and of the Fixed Stars; so that the mind is bewildered in the effort to fully trace all the classes even within one Element. Then, in each Element, there are Spirits participating in all forms—mixed and simple—of the Animal Kingdom: every form of bird, beast, fish, reptile, and insect, sometimes in a simple form, but more often combined with each other and with Man—*for example*, a common form of certain Fire-Spirits is a combination of a Man, a Lion, and a Serpent. 

Some of the most repulsive Elemental forms, however, are the various combinations of huge insect or crustacean shapes with either human or animal heads. Such are true nightmares, and Darwin’s "missing link" seems to be easily found on the Elemental plane. The snakes, dogs, and insects seen by some sufferers of "delirium tremens" are—according to a modern occultist—simply forms taken by some of the lowest Elementals who have been attracted to the alcohol-soaked atmosphere of the habitual drunkard, following the same law by which vultures and jackals are drawn to rotting and putrefying carcasses.
