...as incorrect to call even the lowest and worst of the Elementals “devils,” as to say that a scorpion or an adder was *really* a fiend.

Another and more difficult matter to grasp regarding the Elemental Spirits is that they partake only of the nature of one element respectively; in that element they can live, breathe, and move, but another element—especially one opposite in nature—is to them a bar and obstacle.

Although the evocation of elementary spirits was an important part of theurgic practice during the Middle Ages, we are unacquainted with any ritual which explains the method of communicating with these intelligences in any complete way. A large portion of so-called angelical magic was actually devoted to the control of the Spirits of the Air, who are the Sylphs of Paracelsian mythology, and it is frequently difficult to distinguish between the angels and the "elementaries" of old writers because of this consistent confusion. Much of the Black Magic of the past was also undoubtedly concerned with the lower hierarchies of Nature Spirits. Just as the “*Comte de Gabalis*” identifies the gracious creations of the classical Pantheon—its Astartes, Dianas, and Egerias—with the beautiful tribe of Nymphs, so did the devils of the Sabbath, through a variety of characters and qualities, resemble the lowest of the elementals; they actually had very little in common with the fallen angels of orthodox Christian theology.

A method for the questioning and government of elementary intelligences is provided in the initiations of Eliphas Lévi, but the source from which it was taken is unnamed, and it is very fragmentary. It is, however, the most available of its kind and, with a few supplementary recipes, may be sufficient for practical purposes.

To dominate the Elementary Spirits and thus become the king of the occult elements, it is necessary, says the last of the French initiates, to experience the four trials of ancient initiation; as these initiations no longer exist, they must be replaced by similar processes.

He who is afraid of the water will never reign over the <term>Undines</term>; he who shrinks from the flames will never command <term>Salamanders</term>; let him who is subject to vertigo leave the <term>Sylphs</term> in peace, and refrain from irritating the <term>Gnomes</term>, for inferior spirits will obey no power which has not asserted its supremacy in their own specific element.

Water is exorcised by the laying on of hands, by breathing, and by speech, mixing consecrated salt with a little of the ash remaining in the incense pan. The salt and the ashes of the incense must be separately consecrated. The Prayer of the <term>Undines</term> should follow.

The earth is exorcised by the sprinkling of water, by breathing, and by fire, with the perfumes proper to each day, and the prayer of the <term>Gnomes</term>.

It must be kept in mind that the special kingdom of the <term>Gnomes</term> is in the north, that of the <term>Salamanders</term> in the south, that of the <term>Sylphs</term> in the east, and that of the <term>Undines</term> in the west. They influence the four temperaments of man; that is to say, the <term>Gnomes</term> influence the melancholic, <term>Salamanders</term> the sanguine, <term>Undines</term> the phlegmatic, and <term>Sylphs</term> the bilious. Their signs are—the hieroglyphs of the Bull for the <term>Gnomes</term>, who are commanded with the magic sword; of the Lion for the <term>Salamanders</term>, who are commanded with the forked rod or magic trident; of the Eagle for the <term>Sylphs</term>, who are ruled by the holy pentacles; and, finally, of Aquarius for the <term>Undines</term>, who are evoked by the cup of libations. Their respective sovereigns are <term>Gob</term> for the <term>Gnomes</term>, <term>Djin</term> for the <term>Salamanders</term>, <term>Paralda</term> for the <term>Sylphs</term>, and <term>Necksa</term> for the <term>Undines</term>.

Like so much of occult nomenclature, these names are of a generic and arbitrary kind and appear to be of no real importance; in this instance, they are borrowed from folklore.

It must be remembered that the words “<term>Elemental</term>” and “<term>Elementary</term>” are not exactly interchangeable terms; the latter is frequently used by “<term>theosophists</term>” to denote the astral remains, or “shell,” of a deceased person who has led a coarse and evil life on earth, and whose vanishing personality remains for some time in the earth's atmosphere, seeking to annoy the living.

When an <term>elementary</term> spirit torments, or, at any rate, troubles the inhabitants of this world, continues <term>Eliphas Lévi</term>, it must be commanded by air, water, fire, and earth, by breathing, sprinkling, the burning of perfumes, and by tracing on the ground the <term>Star of Solomon</term> and the sacred <term>Pentagram</term>. Then, holding the <term>pentacle of Solomon</term> in the hand and taking up the sword, rod, and cup in turn, the <term>Conjuration of the Four</term> should be repeated.

This Conjuration should be preceded and ended with the sign of the cross, made in the <term>Kabbalistic</term> manner. Raising the hand to the forehead, the <term>Magus</term> should say: “Yours is,” then, bringing it to the breast, “the kingdom;” transferring it to the left shoulder, “justice;” finally, to the right shoulder, “and mercy;” then, joining both hands, “through the generating ages.”

To conquer and subjugate the elementary spirits, we must never be guilty of the faults that characterize them. A capricious and changeable mind will never be able to rule the <term>Sylphs</term>. A soft, cold, and fickle disposition will never be qualified to govern the <term>Undines</term>; anger irritates the <term>Salamanders</term>, and gross greed makes those whom it enslaves the sport and plaything of the <term>Gnomes</term>. But we must be prompt and active, like the <term>Sylphs</term>; flexible and observant as the <term>Undines</term>; energetic and strong like the <term>Salamanders</term>; and hardworking and patient, like the <term>Gnomes</term>: in a word, we must overcome them in their strength without ever being overcome by their weakness.

---

Medieval magic abounds with the histories of familiar spirits attached to the persons of magicians, frequently performing their commands without any apparent reward and without demanding from their mortal master any of the spiritual sacrifices that are usually supposed to be required from those who deal with the infernal world. The evidentiary value of these histories has been weakened with time, but they are absolutely paralleled in the wonders of modern <term>Spiritualism</term>, and there is a mass of contemporary testimony confirming a domestic ministry of unseen intelligences, who in their characters and performances are substantially identical to the familiar spirits of the past. The ritual for the evocation of familiars is therefore not to be identified with infernal

Therefore, the following ancient ceremony for the control of <term>familiars</term> may be recorded here.

<margin><image-desc>Red pencil "X" and vertical line in left margin</image-desc></margin>First, choose a spot suitable for your purpose, which must be either a subterranean vault draped in black and lit by a magical torch; or else in the center of some thick wood or desert, or on some vast, unfrequented plain; or among the ruins of ancient castles, abbeys, monasteries, etc., or among the rocks on the seashore; in some private, secluded churchyard, or any other solemn, melancholy place, between the hours of twelve and one at night, either when the moon shines very brightly or else when the elements are disturbed by storms of thunder, lightning, wind, and rain. For in these places, times, and seasons, it is argued that spirits can manifest themselves to mortal eyes with less difficulty and remain visible with the least pain in this elemental world.

When the proper time and place are decided upon, a <term>magic circle</term> is to be formed, within which the master and his <note>The word 'associates' is underlined in red pencil</note>associates—three people in total—must carefully retreat. The dimensions of the circle are as follows: a piece of ground nine feet square is usually chosen, at the full extent of which parallel lines are drawn one within another, with various crosses and triangles drawn between them, close to which the first or outer circle is formed. Then, about half a foot inside that, a second circle is drawn; and within that, another square corresponding to the first, the center of which is the seal or spot where the master and associates are to be positioned. The empty spaces formed by the various lines and angles of the figure are filled with all the holy names of God.

<margin>A red pencil X and a vertical line mark this block</margin>

# THE OCCULT SCIENCES.

When the circle is drawn, the ground consecrated, and the Exorcist securely placed within the circle, he proceeds to call up or conjure the spirit by its proper name.

The forms of conjuration most commonly appear in the first instance under a ferocious and frightful guise, which is evidence that, whatever their nature, they are not of a kind to be trusted; but if the intention of the Magus be consciously divested of the desire to communicate with the abyss, an infernal spirit would not usually, according to the magical hypothesis, be able to disguise himself as an <term>elementary</term> or a mere <term>familiar</term>. All submundane spirits are variable and inclined toward deceit, but when properly subjected they are said to be frequent and obliging; they more or less require certain conciliatory offerings, such as fumigations, odors, incense, and other ingredients; the bloody sacrifices which are occasionally enjoined are rites of a detestable nature, and should never be attempted. The Exorcist, in all cases, must be greatly upon his guard, and when he has completed the exorcism, and made such inquiries as he wished to obtain from the spirit, he must carefully discharge him by some form like the following:—

> “Because you have diligently answered my demands, and been ready to come at my first call, I do here license you to depart unto your proper place, without injury or danger to man or beast; depart, I say, and be ever ready at my call, being duly exorcised and conjured by sacred rites and magic; I charge you to withdraw with quiet and peace; and peace be continued between me and you, in the name,” etc.

The magician must remain within the boundary of the circle until all signs of the spirit’s presence have passed away. Then he may venture to withdraw, repeating the Lord’s prayer; after which, concludes this ritual, he may take up the various utensils, and, having destroyed all traces of the circle, may return in safety to his proper home. According to one authority: “The prevalence of the black color in the requisites for the above ceremony shows that the elementals communicated with would be of the nature of the Earth, and of Saturn;"

# BLACK MAGIC.

## THE EVOCATION OF DEMONS.

An exceedingly large proportion of <insert>mystical pneumatology was concerned with the realm of demons of</insert> orthodox Christian theologians, and, despite the discreet silence of modern mystics, it is better to face this fact. Much that was commonly accepted in the West as White (*i.e.,* permissible) Magic was only a disguised goeticism, and many of the resplendent angels invoked with divine rites reveal their cloven hooves. It is not an exaggeration to say that a large majority of past psychological experiments were conducted to establish communication with demons, and that for unlawful purposes. The popular conceptions concerning the diabolical spheres, which have all been accredited by magic, may have been gross exaggerations of facts concerning rudimentary and perverse intelligences, but the willful viciousness of the communicants is substantially unaffected by that.

<margin>|</margin> It is universally recognized by all Christian mystics and magical hierophants that the <insert>demons are fallen angels;</insert> and they are in substantial agreement with orthodox theology as to the nature and cause of their fall. Some place it before the creation of the physical world, some on the second day of the creation. Their conceptions on the subject are quite as inadequate as anything offered by theology. It would be interesting to trace the demonological hypothesis from its extreme Oriental origin to its gruesome medieval elaborations, to establish by philological and historical facts the true nature of the primary conception of demons, and to distinguish between the demon of Socrates and the fiend of the Black Sabbath, but the inquiry is beyond

<summary>This page argues that much of historical "White Magic" was actually a disguised form of demon-summoning and explores the consensus among mystics that demons are fallen angels. It notes that while popular conceptions of hellish realms may be exaggerated, the malicious intent of those seeking to communicate with demons remains a historical reality.</summary>

<keywords>Black Magic, Evocation of Demons, pneumatology, goeticism, fallen angels, Christian mystics, medieval, Socrates, Black Sabbath, hierophants</keywords>

It is enough to state that the word “demon” did not anciently imply, by necessity, a spirit of an evil nature, and that the Doctrine of Devils passed over with the rest of a doubtful inheritance from the Jewish to the Christian systems. The classification of the angelic hierarchies was counterbalanced by a classification of the masters and hosts of the great abyss. Agrippa affirms that the lost angels are equal in number to those who retained their original status, and that they are also divided into nine formal hierarchies. Wierus, who was his disciple, has provided a tabulated statement of the entire Satanic monarchy, with the names and the surnames of seventy-two princes and a myriad of smaller devils. It appears from this table that the throne of Infernus has passed from the possession of the original and ancient serpent, Satan, and that Beelzebub reigns in his place, the entire arrangement being organized in the following manner:—

The hypotheses and the formal classifications of the grandmasters of medieval demonology—of <insert>Agrippa and Wierus, of Bodin, Delancre, and Delrio</insert>—have little value except from the archaeological standpoint and as otherwise unheard-of curiosities; that is to say, they have nothing in themselves to recommend them. As doctrinal extensions of alleged facts, they cannot be consistently revived along with modernized mysticism, which is concerned with the facts alone and not with fantastic explanations.

<margin>A red bracket in the margin marks the text from "Eliphas Lévi" to "Hermes or Enoch."</margin>
It may be concluded from the tenor of the previous remarks that the holy horror of Black Magic held by the modern apostles of mysticism is mainly well-founded; but, nevertheless, much nonsense has been written on the subject of the darker side of the transcendental art, and many exaggerated notions have been spread concerning the infernal branch of mystic practice. Sometimes these exaggerations take the form of distinct and, it would seem, deliberate perversions. <insert>Eliphas Lévi</insert> is the prophet of modern mysticism, one of the most accomplished mystics of all the Christian centuries, though probably no person who has entered into “that great magic chain which began with Hermes or Enoch and will only end with the world” ultimately had not only less belief in, but a more profound disdain for, all <term>pneumatic theorems</term>. Indeed, he has considerably blackened the Black Art through sensational fake citations which do not exist in the works from which he claims to quote.*

## BLACK MAGIC.

The “Grimorium Verum” and the “Grand Grimoire” share with the “Grimoire of Pope Honorius” the questionable honor of being the official textbooks of medieval black magic. Their contents are reprehensible enough, and there is no need to exaggerate their malice through distorted selections or by interpreting the recipes they contain in an unjustified way.

<margin>[Vertical line in margin marking the beginning of the paragraph]</margin>
The most noticeable feature of the Grimoires, and of the science they represent, is their complete futility—the immense expenditure of elaborate liturgical and ritual energy for the smallest possible result. Black magic offers its practitioner absolutely no power that the permitted branches of Magic are unable, by their own claims, to provide; for the most part, it is little more than an ignorant, stupid, and grossly superstitious perversion of the “white” art. Why should it be necessary to summon the devils of hell and invoke every intelligence of the abyss, from Bel and the Dragon to Astaroth and Lucifer, merely to obtain possession of hidden treasure? In reality, the hidden money bags of dead misers represent the very peak of ambition for the average black magician. Why should the wizard trade both body and soul, and bind himself with bloody parchments to the infernal hierarchy, simply to acquire the material riches that the less harmful sister art claims to distribute generously through the harmless and unselfish hands of Gnomes and Nature Spirits? *

* <note>A distinction should, however, be made between the mere sorcerer and the true Black Magician. The latter was ambitious for far different possessions than a miser’s money bags.</note>

It would appear that in medieval times, the doctrine of Nature Spirits—derived partly from the classical mythology of Rome, partly from the native religions of the Teutons, and partly from Arabian lore which reached Europe at an early period—became confused with the orthodox doctrine regarding fallen angels. More correctly, the indiscriminate condemnation of all branches of magic by triumphant Christianity resulted in the identification of elemental intelligences with the lesser hordes of devils, and this grotesque classification was accepted by

<summary>The author critiques famous medieval grimoires for their moral failures and practical futility, arguing that black magic's elaborate rituals for material gain are redundant compared to "white" magic. It also notes how nature spirits from various cultures were incorrectly classified as demons by early Christianity.</summary>

<keywords>Grimorium Verum, Grand Grimoire, Grimoire of Pope Honorius, black magic, white art, ritual magic, Bel and the Dragon, Astaroth, Lucifer, Gnomes, Nature Spirits, medieval demonology, Teutonic mythology, Arabian lore</keywords>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the relationship between medieval theology and the practice of black magic, specifically focusing on why practitioners turned to the "abyss" and the current state of such beliefs.</meta>

->THE OCCULT SCIENCES.<-

the black magicians, who expected to obtain their desires with greater ease from demons than from other classes of invisible beings. This opinion was fully supported by the theology of the period; the universal spread of the devil's influence, the practical omnipotence of Satan, and his extreme readiness to distribute the kingdoms of the earth—with all their pomps and vanities—to those who would fall down and worship him, were doctrines tirelessly taught by the Roman Catholic Church.

<margin><note>A red vertical line is drawn in the left margin next to this paragraph.</note></margin>
The orthodox views concerning demonology have fallen into well-deserved discredit; the system of infernal magic built upon their foundation has sunk with them into permanent oblivion. Rudimentary, regressive, and malevolent intelligences may indeed exist among the peoples of the limitless invisible world, and it may be possible to communicate with these; however, it would not be through the methods, with the alleged results, or with the involved penalties of the black rituals. If it were otherwise, reproducing them today—when all types of mystical experiments are gaining a certain belief and exciting curiosity mixed with reverence—would be nearly criminal. But, unlike the beliefs and the practices connected with lawful magic, they belong to an order of ideas which has completely passed away; they are the dead branch of the living tree of occultism, and it is only their historical interest, and the high place to which they are entitled among the curiosities of obsolete superstition, that justify their consideration in any comprehensive statement of the whole scope of esoteric science.

Many of the practices taught in the “Grimoires” are, however, relatively harmless; on the other hand, many of the experiments contained in legitimate rituals border closely on Black Magic—those which are concerned with animal sacrifices, a survival of Judaism and of Hellenic <term>theurgy</term>, being especially open to objection.

The filth, trickery, and nonsense of the “Grimoires” are the most noticeable features concerning them. Disgusting physical processes for the recovery of female purity, revolting recipes for obtaining invisibility by eating the boiled bones of dead black cats, directions for the

<summary>The author explains that medieval black magic was encouraged by the Church's emphasis on Satan's power, but argues that such infernal rituals are now obsolete superstitions. While some Grimoire practices are harmless or merely disgusting, others involving animal sacrifice are criticized as bordering on black magic.</summary>
<keywords>black magic, demonology, Grimoires, theurgy, occultism, Satan, superstition, esoteric science, Latin church</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the Grimoires, detailing the contradictory nature of black magic rituals which combined pious Christian observance with the attempted summoning of demons.</meta>

# BLACK MAGIC.

composition of aphrodisiac potions, and other waste from the gutters of superstition, combine with grotesque and usually impossible formulas for the summoning of "the grand Lucifuge," and written contracts for the sale of men's souls for small rewards to the inhabitants of damnation.

It is impossible to interpret the "Grimoires" in any serious way; diabolical practices are always preceded by long and solemn prayers that insist on the purity of the magician's intention and on his fixed resolve to belong irrevocably to God. The most sacred rites of the church are practiced as required preliminaries, and the authors are apparently innocent of any conscious profanation in the matter. When the supreme moment of summoning finally arrives—when the masses have been said, when the long fasts have ended, when the practitioner has divested himself of all impure desires and has kept his body from all sexual defilement for a prescribed time—when at length he determines to invoke the devil, it is generally evident that he intends to trick the fiends. He possesses the words that master them; he can torture them with his magic wand; he can bully them into complying with his demands; he treats them consistently as fools. When he sells them his soul, he deceives them with an obvious play on words; and the devil leaves satisfied, while his summoner chuckles at his hoax and completes his grotesque performance with a determination to devote himself to good deeds, to distribute the buried treasures he has unearthed with the fiend's help among Christ's poor, while his familiar spirit is dismissed with a paltry coin on the first day of the month. Such is Infernal Magic! Such is the art or science upon which some modern mystics direct their condemnations!

Should there be anyone foolish enough to want to revive this truly awful art, it would be wise to remind him that it can only be practiced by a believer in the Catholic Church.* The belief in that system must be both real and strong, and it is an absolutely vital condition.

"To be a Christian is difficult," said the impressed but hesitant pagan, and the heights of mystical holiness are also a laborious climb; however, the pinnacle of mastery is far more easily reached by those called to it than is the devilish state that the true Black Magic requires of its practitioners.

Abstinence from every kind of impurity must be observed for the period of an entire quarter of the moon; a pledge must be given to the "Grand Adonay," who is the master of all spirits, that the number of daily light meals shall not exceed two, and that they shall be preceded by prayer throughout the entire prescribed term. The practitioner, moreover, must undress as rarely, and sleep as little, as possible, but he must meditate continually on his undertaking and center all his hopes in the infinite goodness of the divine Adonay. The "Grimoires" are so full of deceptions and mystifications that it is frequently difficult to declare with any certainty their real meaning, but it would appear to be God whom they designate under his name Adonay, and not some master-fiend who is granted the title of Deity. The invocation of Lucifer must be distinguished from the worship of him, which we have not found in the rituals of black magic.

According to the "Grand Grimoire," the materials required for evocation are a stone made of red enamel, called ematille, which, it is said, can be purchased from a pharmacist; a young virgin goat, which must be crowned with vervain and decapitated on the third day of the moon; and a forked branch of a wild hazel which has never borne fruit, and which must be cut on the day of evocation, when the sun is just rising. A piece of wood must be fashioned to a size corresponding with one end of the fork of the genuine rod, and must be taken to a locksmith so that he may cap the two small branches with the steel blade with which the victim was slain, taking care that the ends are slightly pointed when they are fitted to the wood. When everything is carried out in this manner, the Magus may return home and attach the aforementioned cap with his own hands to the genuine rod. When these tasks have been completed, the practitioner may be certain that in the "Blasting Rod" (which is the name given to the instrument) he possesses a most priceless treasure.

The location for the summoning must be a lonely and isolated spot, and the time must be night. To that place, he must transport the rod, the skin of the kid, the stone called ematille, two vervain crowns, along with two candlesticks and an equal number of candles made of virgin wax, created by a virgin girl and duly blessed. He must also take a new steel and two new flints, with enough tinder to light a fire, as well as half a bottle of brandy, some consecrated incense and camphor, and four nails from the coffin of a deceased child. The great Kabbalistic circle must be formed with strips of the kid’s skin, fastened to the ground using the four nails. Then, with the stone called ematille, a triangle must be drawn within the circle, beginning at the eastern point. In the center of this figure, the practitioner must take his place; he must place the two candlesticks and the two vervain crowns on the right and left of the triangle. The candles should then be lit, and a brazier, which must be in front of the practitioner, must be piled with willow-wood charcoal and lit with the help of a small amount of the brandy and part of the camphor; the rest is to be saved for the periodic renewal of the fire according to the duration of the operation.

Many incantations are provided in the rituals, and these are to be used one after another until the spirit appears. The most powerful, which is also apparently the most nonsensical, may be cited as an example of them all.

In the *Grimorium Verum*, the infernal hierarchy is described in greater detail than in the *Grand Grimoire*. Lucifer, Beelzebuth, and Ashtaroth are said to reign respectively—the first over Europe and Asia, the second over Africa, and the third over America—seemingly with equal power.

Most modern defenders of Black Magic would be surprised to find what a ridiculous sequence of errors Black Magic was reduced to in the Middle Ages. However, such an advocate would claim that in ancient times it was not so, and that Black Magic, in its true sense, represents the corruption of Divine Magic to produce material and evil results. They would argue that too often the desire for worldly advancement and the satisfaction of revenge had led the medieval student away from the higher paths.

The solemn and ceremonial invocation of curses upon another person undoubtedly formed an important part of ancient <term>goetic art</term>. An instance from Plutarch's *Life of Marcus Crassus* may be quoted here. It occurred when Crassus was leaving Rome to march against the Parthians, despite the fact that they were allies of Rome at the time. "Ateius, one of the tribunes, went to meet Crassus. First, by the authority of his office, he commanded him to stop and protested against his enterprise. Then he ordered one of his officers to seize him, but the other tribunes intervened, and the officer let Crassus go. Ateius now ran ahead to the gate and placed a censer with fire on it there. At the approach of Crassus, he sprinkled incense upon it, offered libations, and spoke the most horrible curses, calling upon certain terrifying and strange gods. The Romans say these mysterious and ancient curses have such power that the

The following <term>goetic</term> invocation of Typhon Seth, the Egyptian Evil Deity, is taken from the Leyden Papyrus (No. 65, col. xv.). It is Greco-Egyptian and is of value not only as representing an ancient ceremonial for invoking a curse upon a person, but also as showing the great importance attached to the correct knowledge of certain names. This is corroborated again and again in the Egyptian “Ritual of the Dead,” and seems to prove that such an idea is no mere medieval superstition. It is, furthermore, especially to be noted that the magical names are *not* Egyptian. Some have thought them to have been adopted from Hebrew or Chaldee.

The rejection of much of the magical hypothesis of the Spirit World is the first course that enlightened reason seems to require of the modern student. For instance, the divine character of the Hebrew alphabet will be accepted by few persons today. The supposed correspondence of its ciphers

<meta>This page continues the philosophical analysis of mystical and magical doctrines. It specifically addresses the Kabbalistic belief in divine hierarchies and the linguistic nature of creation, while comparing magical demonology to Christian traditions and modern psychology. It concludes by arguing that while specific magical hypotheses may be outgrown, the underlying experimental "facts" of spiritual perception remain valid.</meta>

# BLACK MAGIC.

67

<margin>(</margin>...with the divine creative forces which combined for the production of the innumerable hierarchies of intelligence—just as the letters of an alphabet combine for the production of an indefinitely expandable language—is, however, an important article in the Kabbalist's creed. So too is the anagrammatic extraction of the names and offices of angels from scriptural verses selected at random. There is little in the mystical doctrine concerning demonology to distinguish its philosophical importance from the demonology of the Christian church. The hypothesis of elementary spirits is extremely suggestive and is an interesting contribution to folklore, but its classifications are by no means scientific in character. The eschatological doctrine derived by magic from Jewish Kabbalism is undoubtedly valuable as a hypothesis, though there is little to confirm it in the discoveries of modern psychology.

But when point after point of the prominent doctrines of magic is eliminated in this manner, the student may reasonably inquire whether the mystical basis and the temple have not both melted into thin air. Criticism which "pumps out with a ruthless ingenuity, atom by atom," must inevitably leave us with "emptiness." It should, however, be remembered that the secret which the mystics possessed was the opening of the inner eye for perception into the world called spiritual. Prophecies will become void and tongues will cease, but it is affirmed that love will not fail. In the same way, doctrines that were once authoritative and hypotheses once held to be adequate are outgrown in the progress of the mind and in the extension of the intellectual horizon. However, the facts which they seem to have been invented to explain cannot be made obsolete by time, and truths which can be verified by experiment are not left behind in evolution. Now, the experiments which were the foundation of magical doctrine and magical hypothesis are facts which are not to be evaporated by the severity of critical analysis; they are the synthesis of the experiments of the ages, and they can be verified by those who desire to do so.

The lucidity of the mystics exhibited what there is, naturally, good reason to believe: namely, the existence

of countless hierarchies of being, which, according to the fashion of the time, were duly classified and arranged, as has already been shown. In practical performance, the magician of the past was ahead of current psychological experimentation; but the new mysticism, while expanding the circle of experiment and seeking to surpass the Magi in the extent of its practical research, also endeavors to realize in its disciples that ideal life which the Magi envisioned. It should also, as it progresses, do its best to establish the foundations of a rational hypothesis regarding the unseen.

The ceremonial part of magic, with its dreadfully powerful formulas and its excess of strange ritual practices, was granted an absolute value in the past. It will be seen that an inherent power was supposed to reside in certain words and acts—a principle which is at the heart of all superstitious practice. Now, the actual and verifiable value of ceremonial magic is of two kinds. It produced an elevated state in the operator which developed the hidden faculties of his inner being; and the atmospheric conditions required for success in all classes of mystical experiments were produced by its perfumes and incense. As the human imagination is always open to the same kinds of impressions, and as modern psychology is equally dependent for success on atmospheric and other conditions, Ceremonial Magic should be as effective today as at any period in history.

The *Hexameron* of Torquemada; the *Tableau de l’inconstance des démons* by Delancre; the *Pseudomonarchia Dæmonorum* of Wierus; and the *Disquisitiones Magicæ* of Delrio are the most authoritative and exhaustive sources of knowledge on demonological doctrines and science. There is an entire collection of medieval Latin literature devoted to the processes for the exorcism of evil spirits. As the intervention of devils has always been the popular explanation for all supernatural phenomena, so in all languages supernatural literature has been largely demonological in character. In English, there are countless books which cover all branches of sorcery and witch-

<summary>This page analyzes the psychological mechanisms behind ceremonial magic, arguing that its rituals and atmospheres induce heightened states of consciousness. It also identifies the primary historical texts of demonology and notes how supernatural phenomena have historically been attributed to demonic intervention.</summary>

<keywords>ceremonial magic, psychology of ritual, hierarchies of being, Magi, demonology, exorcism, Torquemada, Delancre, Wierus, Delrio, supernatural phenomena</keywords>

# BLACK MAGIC.

<meta>Blue underlining under the names "Scot's", "Glanvill's", "Cotton Mather's", and "Richard Baxter's". A faint orange vertical pencil bracket appears in the left margin next to the first thirteen lines of text.</meta>

craft. One of the most notable is Scot's "Discovery of Witchcraft," dated 1651, with which, representing the opposing interest, may be compared Glanvill's *Sadducismus Triumphatus*, belonging to the same period, and Cotton Mather's "Wonders of the Invisible World." In 1859, a "Philosophy of Witchcraft" was attempted by J. Mitchell. The "Letters on Demonology" addressed to J. G. Lockhart by the author of "Waverley" <note>Sir Walter Scott</note> should not be confused with the "Discovery." There is also a "History of Witchcraft"; and many sermons, treatises, and accounts of physical pacts between devils and witches have contributed to the proper understanding and illumination of the general subject. Richard Baxter's "Certainty of the World of Spirits fully evinced," 1691, is a representative work of its kind. For the practical part, or the method of communicating with demons, the *Grimorium Verum* and the *Grand Grimoire* are said to be entirely sufficient.

# NECROMANCY.

## THE EVOCATION OF THE SOULS OF THE DEAD.

WE have seen in the section devoted to angelology that, after the nine choirs of celestial intelligences in the <term>pneumatic enumeration</term> of the Kabbalists, there is the order of beatified human souls who exercise a beneficent and energizing influence on the incarnate human race. Their chief is said to be Moses, and by their natures they are in opposition to the souls of the wicked, who are under the tyranny of Nahema, the demon of impurity. Such a classification is, to all appearance, very arbitrary and inadequate; the formal distinction between good and bad is not substantiated by present psychological experiments. But this and other doctrinal teachings of the old mystics do not belong to that portion of esoteric wisdom which alone is rational to revive. The student should always carefully separate the ascertainable facts of the science from the theoretical structure which covers them. The differentiation of the human soul into a multiplicity of separable parts (even the time-honored and reasonable distinction between the soul and the spirit) must be properly referred to the domain of speculation to which it properly belongs. As regards the subject of this chapter, a central, fundamental, and vital fact is this—that practical magic in its investigations of extra-mundane worlds has come into communication with "those whom the living call dead," and that the ritual of such communication has been bequeathed by magicians to posterity.

Many speculations concerning Necromancy are reasonable, many hypotheses are probable, and others are neither probable nor reasonable. Yet some writers appear to sup—

<summary>This page discusses the Kabbalistic hierarchy of souls, contrasting the beatified souls led by Moses with the wicked souls under the demon Nahema, while arguing for a focus on the practical communication with the dead over abstract speculation.</summary>
<keywords>Necromancy, Kabbalah, angelology, Nahema, Moses, pneumatic enumeration, soul and spirit, extra-mundane communication, esoteric wisdom</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on Necromancy, moving from the classification of souls to the authority of mystical traditions and the possibility of communicating with the dead.</meta>

<header>THE OCCULT SCIENCES.</header>

...suppose that if we accept the facts recorded by the mystics, we are bound to accept the solution which they offer.

On the other hand, it is not unreasonably argued that the men who have most investigated the psychological world have the greatest right to an opinion about it. They have, indeed, every right to an opinion, and their opinions must have a claim on our respect, but we need not consider them final. We hold that in magic there is no authority except that of the pioneer who has gone before. Now, the pioneer may be a beacon in the darkness, but he is not an infallible guide. The mystics have penetrated far beyond ourselves into the infinite realms of the soul, but they may not have measured the infinite, and thus we are by no means committed to any of their absolute doctrines. <note>The opposing view has been well stated by an esteemed collaborator as follows: "The value of psychological authority can only be measured by equal experimental knowledge."</note>

Generally speaking, the <term>pneumatic dogmas</term> of the Middle Ages were based on the lines of orthodox <term>pneumatology</term>; as we have seen, they admitted the distinction between pure and fallen angels and reproduced their spiritual histories point by point. The hierarchy of elementary spirits is foreign to Christian theology, and theology denounced them as demons; the psychological history of the human individual in its after-death condition is, on the other hand, substantially identical in both systems, and the higher the position of the mystic, the closer he gravitates in his opinions toward the accredited teachings of the great religion of the day.

Once more, an important point in that branch of <term>transcendental science</term> which refers to the souls of humanity consists in the possibility of establishing that the soul survives death and preserves its intelligence and its individual being in another order of existence, by the method of actual and conscious communication with departed men and women.

The hypotheses which have been founded on this fact are extremely numerous and are sometimes suggestive and beautiful. They all have similarities with each other as well as with the Christian scheme. When estimated at their true worth, they will deserve and repay study.

<keywords>mystics, magic, pneumatic dogmas, pneumatology, spirits, demons, afterlife, transcendental science, soul, psychological authority</keywords>

<header>NECROMANCY.</header>

<note>Vertical red line in the left margin alongside lines 4–6; red bracket in the right margin alongside lines 2–10</note>
and are rich in reflected lights. One of the explanatory hypotheses derived from the <term>Kabbalah</term> which most recommends itself to esoteric students today is concerned with the existence of a fluidic envelope of the spirit, which corresponds to St. Paul's conception of a spiritual organism, and is termed the <note>underlined in red</note><term>astral body</term>. An exceedingly clear exposition of this hypothesis and of the soul's eternal progress has been given to us by <term>Eliphas Lévi</term>, and it fairly represents the ancient doctrine of the <term>Hebraistic mystics</term>.

In its broadest scope, this hypothesis affirms the existence of a single substance diffused through all space, out of which the entire Cosmos, with all its sentient populations, was originally developed, and into which, at the close of the great and final cycle, it will all ultimately be resolved. It is the <note>underlined in red</note><term>Great Telesma</term> of <term>Hermes Trismegistus</term>, an all-encompassing and all-penetrating substance, the first matter of original creation, the created Light of Genesis. The polarization of this substance around a center produces living beings, and in man it forms the <note>underlined in red</note><term>Astral Body</term>, or <term>Plastic Mediator</term>. The spirit, or permanent principle of the individual, which the hypothesis apparently distinguishes from the cosmic substance and refers to a divine origin, departs at the moment of death clothed in the astral form. In the case of a virtuous person, this form evaporates like a pure incense, but it enchains the wicked mind.

The service of mystical doctrine to the spiritual aspirations of man has a definite value of its own. The <note>underlined in red</note>ascent of the ladder of being, the cycles and incarnations of the soul, are delightful poetic conceptions which nourish the loftier energies of the imagination. The legendary romances of the soul deserve to be incorporated in a volume devoted to the subject of <term>pneumatic speculations</term>; they are not confined to mysticism; they exist among all peoples, both primitive and civilized; they throw great light upon <term>comparative mythology</term>; but it must always be remembered that ordinary psychological theories are as much in the field of speculation as are the North American traditions of "Travels into the Land of Souls."

<meta>This page continues the exploration of mystical practices, transitioning from the theoretical nature of the "astral body" and "cosmic substance" to the practical application of summoning the dead, specifically focusing on the definitions and implications of Necromancy.</meta>

# THE OCCULT SCIENCES.

<meta>Pencil brackets in the left margin alongside the first and second paragraphs.</meta>

The ceremonial summoning of the departed is usually termed Necromancy. This word, however, by its etymology, means the art of predicting future events through communications established with the dead; it is divination by the inhabitants of the grave. The science of the mystics has expanded this original meaning, while popular superstition has corrupted it. According to the interpretation of the mystics, necromancy is the summoning of the souls of deceased humanity, for whatever purpose it is undertaken; as it is understood by the common people, it is synonymous with "Black" Magic, featuring obscene rituals and dark, repulsive practices. Some self-proclaimed mystics, however, have occasionally endorsed this second view, which can only be excused by ignorance. Eliphas Lévi—after personally disturbing the eternal rest of Apollonius through a deliberate ceremonial summoning—indiscriminately denounced the entire art of necromancy as the "blackest of the sciences of the abyss" while attempting to equate modern Spiritualism with the hellish schemes of sorcery.

In fact, the summoning of the souls of the departed is one of the most important branches of practical mysticism; it is one of the ultimate tests by which the *mystic gospel* may be said to stand or fall. If it is possible—after following a specific way of life for a set period, intended to elevate the intellect, sharpen spiritual perceptions, and develop what might be called a new sense in humans—to enter into actual and genuine communication with beings who have departed from our plane of existence; if we can see them *as they were*; if we can, to some extent, know them *as they are*; and if, at that time, we are in conscious possession of our physical senses, then the *mystic gospel* must be the truth itself.

There are important questions connected with legitimate *necromancy* that cannot be discussed here. Even if the physical facts are admitted, the problem of identity remains to be addressed; to prove the reality of summoning spirits is not to explain all spiritual mysteries. In an introductory textbook, we believe it is enough to indicate by what means the mystics claim to place their

<summary>This page defines necromancy as the summoning of the dead for divination or communication, distinguishing between its high mystical purpose and its popular association with black magic. The author asserts that the success of such evocations serves as the ultimate test for the validity of mystical philosophy.</summary>

...disciples in communication with the invisible planes of existence. In the summoning of the souls of the dead, one procedure is, at least, relatively simple, and a comprehensive account—stated to be based on an ancient secret theory—has been provided by a French writer in a recent work on the occult sciences.

<margin>X</margin>
> "Prompted by a feeling of profound tenderness," says P. Christian, "it occasionally happens that a bereaved person will dedicate the room where a beloved being died to perpetual mourning. Like a holy place, the chamber is shut and sealed, to be revisited only on the anniversaries of the joys that are gone and of the separation which is final on earth.
> "Nevertheless, the soul can occasionally be attracted by the devotion of the heart; though unseen, it is present at the sacrifice of loving tears. If it is the hour of dusk, and if the departed spirit is called upon by an exalted act of faith and affection made in the name of the Almighty, the brilliance of its immortal essence may break forth in splendor for a moment in the midst of that restful twilight in which nature rests after the setting of the sun.
> "The affection which unites us from beyond the grave to those we mourn must be entirely pure in character. They are to be considered exclusively as the transfigured inhabitants of a higher sphere, who in their new form of existence pray to the Eternal and Almighty Being to make us worthy of one day reaching their own blessedness. An examination of conscience is another equally indispensable condition. If we have wronged our neighbors, the injury must be repaired; if we have enemies, we must forgive them from the very depths of our hearts; if we have neglected the soul-elevating duty of adoration and prayer to God, we must cultivate regularity in worship, in accordance with the rites we have received from our ancestors. These preliminary steps are the best evidence that the summoning of the dead is not sorcery, and that it is not an unholy pact with the malicious spirits in the depths of the invisible world. It is through these preparations alone that

## NECROMANCY.

which he must unfailingly fulfill. Lastly, it will indicate the time when the evocation may be renewed.

> “When it has disappeared, the operator must turn to the east, rekindle the fire on the altar, and make a final offering of incense. Then he must remove the crown and the garlands, take up his candle, and withdraw with his face toward the west until he is out of the oratory. His last duty is to burn the final remains of the flowers and leaves. Their ashes, joined with those which have been collected during the time of retreat, must be mixed with myrtle seed and secretly buried in a field at a depth that will secure them from being disturbed by the plowshare.”

The conditions of necromantic <term>evocation</term>, as can be seen, are somewhat elaborate; they are designed for the legitimate exaltation of the intellectual faculties and for the direction of the force of will. However, there is nothing that endangers one's reason or clouds the brain, and they may be fulfilled by any person who has a little money at their command for the purchase of the required instruments and who occasionally has the privilege of possessing his soul in solitude. The <term>theurgic</term> doctrine has made provision in the event of a failure; it recommends the renewal of the experiment, with additional precautions, on the next anniversary, and affirms that the third time will never fail to yield a result, unless the operator is oppressed by an ingrained vice which has become like a second nature to him.

It must be clear from the ceremony described above that there is nothing repellent to the most cultivated spiritual sense in the rites of lawful necromancy. It is otherwise, however, with the summons of the infernal art—with the unholy necromantic practices of Black Magic, which violate the sanctity of the tomb and endeavor to establish a corrupt communion with the souls of evil men.

The rituals of infernal summons are the outcome of certain psychological doctrines which prevailed during the Middle Ages and are, to some extent, remnants of a very remote antiquity. Persons who committed suicide, or who perished by a violent death, being separated from their bodies before the natural time,

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the darker aspects of necromancy, specifically focusing on "earth-bound" souls and the transition from classical to modern literature on the subject.</meta>

# THE OCCULT SCIENCES.

...were supposed to be more or less bound to the place where the catastrophe happened, until the “radical moisture” <note>In alchemy and early medicine, the vital fluid or fundamental life-force of a body.</note> of their corpse was entirely consumed.

Such earth-bound souls were easily attracted and exploited by sorcerers and black magicians, and many repulsive and objectionable recipes for their evocation still exist. The skeleton or corpse was fumigated with mixtures of blood, milk, eggs, and other substances, and the deceased person was invoked with the usual incantations of Black Magic, generally for an evil purpose. These and other abuses led the Romans to classify Necromancy alongside treason, secret poisoning, and other crimes.

In a Greco-Egyptian papyrus in the British Museum, there is a curious necromantic process described for making a magical ring to bind a person in any manner desired. This ring, the papyrus goes on to say, is to be taken to the grave of a person who died prematurely, and buried there four fingers deep in the ground, with these words:

> “O departed spirit, whoever you are, I deliver to you such a person, so that he may not do such a thing. Then, having covered it up, depart. And you will do it best during the waning of the moon.”

The bibliography of Necromancy is as extensive as the literature of the supernatural itself. For the ritual, it is unnecessary to go further than the directions we have already provided. For the practice of black necromancy, the student is referred to the final section of Ebenezer Sibly’s “Occult Sciences.” In general literature, some information may be found in Howitt’s “History of the Supernatural;” in the “Universal History of Apparitions,” dated 1770; and in Jung Stilling’s “Theory of Pneumatology,” translated in 1884. There is also some curious material to be found in Dr. F. G. Lee’s “Glimpses of the Supernatural.” The recent publication by the authorities of the British Museum of a facsimile of the Papyrus of Ani (“The Book of the Dead”)—a manuscript which scholars date to a period fourteen centuries before Christ—will enable modern students to determine the nature of this celebrated work, which was intended for the use and protection of the dead in the world beyond the grave.

# PART II.

## ALCHEMY.

<margin>0 1</margin>
ALCHEMY is known, and misunderstood, by the whole world as the supposed science of transmuting metals—which the ignorance of an ignorant era labeled as "base"—into the resplendent perfection of gold and silver. Outside the circle of Hermetic students, the possibility of such a conversion is generally mocked today. If anyone, by chance, comes across the evidence that exists for its reality in the buried scientific literature of the distant past, they may be overwhelmed by its extent and solidity, but can hardly be shaken in their prejudice.

<margin>0 2</margin>
We intend in the present essay to define in a clear manner the exact theory of metallic transmutation that was practiced by the alchemists, to state the further possibilities that may be developed from that theory, and to reveal the connection that exists between this branch of Hermetic art and the branch known as Magic. As we are forced to include the largest available amount of compressed information in the shortest possible space, we must ask our readers for a certain rational confidence in those statements that a lack of space prevents us from verifying.

<margin>y</margin>
The philosophical writings which, in the fourth century, were produced under the name of Hermes Trismegistus, were the source of alchemical inspiration during the following Christian centuries. The first distinct and undoubted reference to alchemy came after the circulation of these books, which are as mysterious in content and as important in mystical history as they are doubtful <margin>0 3</margin>

<summary>This page introduces the study of alchemy, defining it as the transmutation of base metals into gold and silver while establishing its historical origins in the 4th-century writings of Hermes Trismegistus.</summary>

<keywords>Alchemy, Hermetic art, Transmutation, Hermes Trismegistus, Magic, metallic conversion, occult history</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the historical origins of alchemy, specifically citing Zosimus the Panopolite (noted here as the Pomopolite) and Geber, before transitioning into the two-fold goal of the alchemical process: metallic transmutation and human transfiguration.</meta>

<header>THE OCCULT SCIENCES.</header>

<page-num>84</page-num>

<margin>✓</margin> and suspicious in their origin. The reference in question is contained in a manuscript treatise by Zosimus the Pomopolite, which is preserved in the library at the Louvre. The first of the practical alchemists—Geber, the Arabian <margin>✓</margin> physician—is usually attributed to the eighth century of our era. From his writings, it would appear that the art was already mature, and he appeals to the authority of a line of earlier adepts and to a literature of apparent antiquity from which he drew extensively. From the <margin>04</margin> eighth to the sixteenth century, the doctrines and practices of alchemy continued to be spread in the West; and, with Germany as its center, it diffused a great light of mysticism until the eve of the French Revolution.

<margin>✓</margin> The declared object of physical alchemy was an investigation of natural secrets that would produce a practical method for the conversion of certain substances, generally metallic, into gold and silver. This was accomplished, for the most part, by means of an elixir which—in harmony with a theory to which much Hermetic importance has sometimes been attached—could be applied to the body <margin>05</margin> of the alchemist after a certain occult adaptation, as well as to a metallic body, with a strictly analogous result. In the mineral kingdom, the completed process was called transmutation; in its application to humanity, it was known as the conversion or trans- <margin>06 [pencil bracket and blue mark in margin]</margin> figuration of the individual by means of alchemy. The result, in the first case, was gold; or if the process was stopped at a certain stage, silver was the product instead. In the second case, a complete renewal of all the vital forces and the development of physical capacities to an exceptionally advanced degree transferred the successful magus to an advanced stage of evolution, and endowed him with powers and abilities that could be used in both a material and spiritual direction.

While the uninitiated students of the Hermetic mystery exhausted their wealth and squandered their entire lives, ransacking every kingdom of nature and <margin>02</margin> experimenting with every conceivable subject from dung to eggshells, so as to finally accomplish the extraction of the fundamental matter of the elixir, the adepts, who appear to have received the secret by oral...

<keywords>Zosimus the Pomopolite, Geber, physical alchemy, transmutation, elixir, Hermetic theory, magus, transfiguration</keywords>

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of alchemical theory, explaining how practitioners followed an arcane tradition to achieve success in transmutation, while introducing the philosophical analogy between the perfection of humanity and the perfection of gold.</meta>

<page-num>85</page-num>
<header>ALCHEMY.</header>

transmission, pursued their experiments in accordance with an arcane theory which was a precious inheritance from the past. The philosophers who were participants in this theory could operate with ease and ensure success. *For them, it was the work of a child or a woman.* The student who was outside the select circle of the hierophants was, however, in the hands of God; the <margin>08</margin> divine science of the Hermetists might be communicated by one of those inexplicable flashes of spontaneous intuition which the God-encompassed minds of the philosophers—who rejected chance and believed in an ever-present Providence—interpreted as direct revelation. A perfect master of science might also initiate a stranger after proper tests and progressive study.

*The Hermetic theory was at once philosophical and practical.* Its philosophical section is, for the most part, *exposed in the literature of alchemy;* <margin>09</margin> its practical portion is preserved in symbolic language and in pictorial symbols which are capable of such diverse interpretations that their true meaning seems invariably to escape the student. By the terms of the philosophical theory, it is evident that the adepts regarded the animal creation as many successive steps through which Nature laboriously ascended to the creation of her most perfect achievement: Man. In every stage of production, Man was the end in view. That which the human individual was to the rest of the animal kingdoms, *gold was to the world of minerals*; it was therefore affirmed, in the allegorical language of alchemy, that Nature always intended to produce gold; the existence of the inferior metals was due to arrested development at various stages of the process. Put less crudely, through the successive steps of the entire mineral kingdom, Nature worked upward toward gold. The foundation of the precious metal is thus to be found in its inferiors, just as there is also a certain common nature between man and the animals which are below him. It was the object of alchemy to take up the work of Nature where it had been arrested by circumstances, to develop the *latent perfections in lead, mercury, and antimony*, and in a thousand other subjects, and to produce, following the lines of her observed operations, the metallic perfection which was her aim.

Alchemists believe they are simply completing Nature's unfinished work by refining inferior metals into their most perfect state.</summary>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the plausibility of alchemical transmutation, moving from the historical evidence of success to a more detailed examination of the philosophical and practical principles behind the art.</meta>

<page-num>86</page-num>
<header>THE OCCULT SCIENCES.</header>

Perhaps there is little in this hypothesis to recommend it to those who are familiar with the facts of modern science, yet the evidence that exists for the performance of transmutations in the past is abundant and good for its kind. If the composite character of metals can be accepted as a possibility—and the trend of scientific experiment is, we believe, moving in this direction—then it is conceivable that the lifelong labors of so many generations of adepts may have occasionally resulted in discovering the constituent elements of gold and in the actual composition of the metal. That success, if it were in reality achieved, was occasionally entirely accidental, is made evident by the fact that the operator was sometimes unable to repeat the experiment which had realized his consuming ambition. The crudity of the explanatory hypothesis does not significantly undermine the evidence; there are many certain facts in modern physics which, even today, are very inadequately explained.

The philosophical theory of alchemy, which we have thus briefly presented, deserves more extended consideration. As the belief in the transmutation of metals has by no means passed away; as the study of practical alchemy is said to be actually reviving with the general resurrection of mysticism; as Figuier admits that the romantic conversion is possible and may even be ultimately performed; and as, as late as the middle of this century, Dr. Christopher Girtanner, who has been described as an eminent professor of Göttingen, actually affirmed in the *Annales de Chimia* that it was destined to be generally known and practiced, that every chemist and artist would be able to manufacture gold, and that kitchen utensils by the close of the present age would be made of the precious metals; it seems desirable to provide the reader with a comprehensive account of the principles which, on the authority of the alchemists themselves, were involved in the art of transmutation. The substance of the following pages is derived from the masonic alchemy of Baron Tschoudi, which is based on the authority of Paracelsus; wherever it seems obscure or imperfect, we have endeavored to supplement it with insights from the writings of other adepts.

# ALCHEMY.

<meta>Pencil markings (circles and brackets) appear in the margins around the first and second paragraphs.</meta>

The first study of an alchemical philosopher is research into the operations of Nature, whose end is God, just as He is also her sole beginning. Nature is philosophically divisible into four chief regions: the dry, the moist, the warm, and the cold, which are the four elementary qualities from which all that exists must be derived. Nature is divided into male and female. She is compared to <term>mercury</term>. She is never visible, though she acts visibly, being a volatile essence that performs its function in bodies and is informed by the universal spirit. She represents the divine breath, the central fire, which is called the <term>sulfur of matter</term> by the philosophers, and is identical with that <term>mercury of the sages</term> which manifests itself through the gentle heat of Nature.

The investigators of Nature should be, like herself, simple, truthful, patient, and persevering. What they desire to perform should be in accordance with her, whom they should follow in every point. In seeking to achieve something more excellent than she has performed in a particular subject, they should consider how it is improved, which is invariably by its like; for example, if you would develop the intrinsic virtue of a metal beyond its natural point, you must grasp the metallic nature itself and know how to distinguish between the male and female in Nature.

The searcher must know how to obtain the seed of metals, which is their <term>elixir</term> or superior <term>quintessence</term>, the most finished and perfect decoction and digestion of the thing itself. This seed, or germ, is produced by the four elements through the will of the <term>Supreme Being</term> and the <term>Imagination of Nature</term>.

The true and <term>primeval matter</term> of metals has a double essence, being an aerial warmth and moisture and a dry heat. But the student must beware how he interprets these terms in their literal sense. There are many circles within the circle of alchemical philosophy. For example, the "air" of the philosophers is said to be a water coagulated by fire, which produces a universal solvent, and even by the "center of the earth," where the elements are supposed to deposit their metallic seed, the philosophers appear to have referred in an arcane manner to

<summary>This page defines the alchemical philosopher's path as the study of Nature’s divine operations and elemental qualities. It explains that the seeker must understand the "seed" of metals and cautions against literal interpretations of alchemical terms like "air" or "the center of the earth."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the philosophical and allegorical foundations of alchemy, specifically focusing on the "living" nature of alchemical substances and the natural formation of metals within the earth.</meta>

...a mysterious storehouse of chaotic forces. But the allegory and the intentional confusion are by no means at an end here. The disciple of the <term>Hermetic art</term> is required to direct his attention to what is termed by the French adepts the <term>*point de la nature*</term>, and this he should not seek in the common metals which are absolutely dead, while those which are known to the hierophants are absolutely living and possessed of a vital spirit. Now, the <term>living gold</term> of the philosophers is explained by another authority to be that “<term>fixed grain</term>” which animates the <term>mercury</term> of the sages; but as the <term>sophic sulphur</term>, or <term>Red Magisterium</term>, is also a life-giving gold, the confusion of <term>alchemical nomenclature</term> is of an exceedingly embarrassing kind to the uninitiated.

Metals were supposed to be produced in the depths of the earth in the following manner. When the circulation of the <term>philosophical elements</term> has deposited the seed, it is returned towards the surface in a sublimated state. The seed of every metal is originally one, in which the possibility of their conversion is founded; they are differentiated by local influences acting within the center. Before all other study, it is affirmed to be absolutely necessary that an amateur should understand the formation of metals in the depths of the earth. Without this, and the faithful imitation of Nature, he will never achieve anything successful. Nature composes all metals of <term>mercury</term>, which is, however, a living and feminine principle, and not the substance known under that name to ordinary chemistry; and of sulphur, which is a living male. In a matrix of saline water, these substances are combined in a vaporous condition, and a species of <term>vitriol</term> results, which by the circulation of the elements is again converted into vapor, is combined with sulphur, is transformed into a glutinous mass, and after other complicated processes—which it would be perfectly pointless to list—a metal is evolved, which is pure or impure according to the location of its production.

These explanations, it will be seen, offer no instructions for the discovery of the all-important seed. It is evident throughout that the described processes refer to

<meta>This page continues the exploration of alchemical literature, discussing the deliberate obscurity used by adepts to hide their processes and the debate over whether alchemy was a genuine secret science or a widespread fraudulent practice.</meta>

# ALCHEMY.

...the laboratory practices of the <term>adepts</term>, and not to cosmic evolution. Simplified and pruned as it is in this brief presentation, it will appear to the ordinary scientific reader as a raving chaos of unintelligible extravagance, out of which it would seem impossible to develop any form or harmony, even if a key were provided to the exact meaning of every figure of speech. When it is added that the most illustrious <term>adepts</term> were accustomed—in addition to allegorical language, insoluble <term>cryptograms</term>, and symbols which admitted innumerable interpretations—to fill their pages with false recipes, useless fictions, and numberless errors, so as to further disguise from the ignorant the truth which they pretended to reveal, it is evident that the recovery of the practical process for <term>metallic transmutation</term> is a hopeless and insane quest; for when no reliance can be placed upon any statement, while deception abounds everywhere, progress of any kind is impossible.

That alchemical <term>typology</term> is <note>underlined</note>nonsense and a hoax<note>end underline</note> from beginning to end cannot, however, be affirmed by any person who is acquainted with the facts of the case. In the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries, when the spread of <term>Hermetic ideas</term> and the increasing facilities of printing made <term>alchemy</term> a profitable business venture for booksellers, a literature which largely consisted of pure imposture appears to have sprung into existence; but in earlier centuries, <note>underlined</note>there was no such incentive for dishonesty.<note>end underline</note> The writings of the alchemists remained in the obscurity of manuscripts, and it is absurd to suppose that a laborious terminology was invented, and innumerable books were written, merely to gratify a purposeless passion for unprofitable and aimless deception. It is more probable that a conventional language was devised to enable the participants in a common pursuit to communicate without difficulty about matters which it was dangerous to refer to in any open and understandable manner. At the same time, it must be admitted that the entire problem is almost inscrutable, and it is difficult to propose any theory concerning it that can be supported.

It may be stated, by way of a conclusion to this por-

When the <term>adepts</term> move from the natural principles of <term>alchemy</term> to an account of the actual process, they are less crude in their methods and less contradictory in their statements. The operations described have the character of serious experiments and could be reproduced if the required materials could be obtained.

The profound study of <term>Hermetic allegory</term> has suggested to a few minds an unexpected solution that is exceedingly evocative and curious. We have seen that the <term>mystics</term> regarded the successive steps of creation as all leading up to man; and, although the analogy may have nothing to recommend it, the <term>occult doctrine of correspondence</term> led them to regard the evolution of minerals as proceeding according to a parallel method. More recently, we have seen that every earthly substance, whether animate or otherwise, possesses a spark of <term>elementary gold</term> at the center of its nature. It is evident, therefore, that on one hand, the <term>gold of the philosophers</term> is not a metal; on the other hand, man is a being who possesses within himself the seeds of a perfection he has never realized, and he therefore corresponds to those metals that the <term>Hermetic theory</term> supposes to be capable of development. It has *consequently been proposed* that the <term>conversion of lead into gold</term> was only the stated objective of alchemy, and that it was actually a search for a process to develop the latent possibilities in the subject, MAN; that the quest was pursued successfully; and that it led to magnificent results in the physical and spiritual orders, as well as to an acquaintance with facts

Without claiming that this hypothesis turns the literary chaos of the philosophers into a perfect order, it can be affirmed that it significantly clarifies their writings, and it is remarkable how contradictions, absurdities, and difficulties seem to vanish wherever it is applied. <margin>5</margin>

At the same time, it is equally and abundantly clear that those alchemists whose lives we are somewhat acquainted with were, in the vast majority of cases, clearly physical chemists in search of a physical secret and seeking material wealth.

<margin>6</margin>
It may be said that since actions speak louder than words, this new interpretation, however plausible, is probably false. Yet, beyond the light it sheds on a large section of alchemical literature, there are other considerations in its favor that cannot be easily dismissed. First, there is the fundamental and truly important fact that those Hermetic books of Alexandrian Platonism, theurgy, and Egyptian tradition attributed to Trismegistus—which were the ultimate, constantly quoted, and infallible authority for all Western <margin>}</margin> alchemists—appear completely lack any clear connection with metallic transmutation; they are devoted to transcendental cosmology and a system of spiritual philosophy. If the alchemists were workers in metals, why did they appeal to Hermes? It is not enough to answer that they believed the Alexandrian symbolist was describing the processes of their metallic science in a secret way. There is nothing to support <margin>8</margin> the claim, for the *Golden Treatise of Hermes Trismegistus*, which alone is concerned with physical transmutation, is a work from the fifteenth century—long after the establishment of alchemical doctrines in the West, long after Hermes had been cited as an authority, and in all probability, it was a product of that very opinion. Thus, the fact remains that there is nothing in early Hermetic <margin>9</margin> literature to give weight to such an interpretation.

In the second place, it is fairly certain that the

correspondence with superior intelligent beings, and the hope that they might in the end be enabled to create an intellectual union with the *Summa totius Perfectionis* <note>The Sum of all Perfection</note>, was the divine dream of the mystics. This they endeavoured, and this they aspired to accomplish by virtue of a theory of development which Hermes applied in his explanation of the material cosmos. They deemed this theory equally applicable to humanity as the *minutum mundum* <note>the small world, or microcosm</note>—the cosmos within the cosmos, the small world within the great world, and intellectually the centre of it. It was also appropriated by the alchemists because they believed that the development of a particle of matter must proceed along the same lines and according to the same basis as that of the grand totality. With such an interpretation, we can understand the appeal to Hermes by both physical and spiritual mystics, the identity of symbols, and the parallel allegories between the two schools of thought.

At this point we must pause; it would be quite out of place in an elementary treatise to attempt any description of mystical methods in the development of the interior Sol <note>Sun</note> and of its complement, the interior Luna <note>Moon</note>. We believe we have established the points which we undertook to demonstrate at the beginning. We have shown that the ordinary notions concerning the Hermetic mystery and alchemy are inadequate to the scope of the subject; we have endeavoured to explain the concepts which are at the base of the theory of transmutation, to indicate the possibilities which the mystics beheld behind it, and to reveal, as was promised, the connection that exists between this branch of transcendental experiment and that which is known as magic. For, from the mystical standpoint, magic was the modification of man by alchemy, or the spiritual side of the *magnum opus* <note>Great Work</note>.

Books on the subject of alchemy in the English language are rare and expensive. The following are recommended:— “A Suggestive Enquiry into the Hermetic Mystery;”* “Remarks on Alchemy and the Alchemists,”

---

* Of all modern works, this is the most important to the student. Issued in 1850, it was suppressed almost on the day of publication, as the authoress and her advisers considered that it was too suggestive to be circulated with prudence.
