## THE ELIXIR OF LIFE.

Just as the goals of the <term>alchemists</term> have been interpreted in a romantic spiritual sense, so too has that other department of <term>thaumaturgic physics</term>, intimately connected with alchemy—alchemy, indeed, under another aspect—<margin><note>In the original, a right-side bracket marks the text from "nected with alchemy" to "body of man."</note></margin>which is concerned with the conversion, reconstruction, and complete <term>transmutation</term> of the present human body. The entire <term>transcendentalism</term> of the West seems to have been emphatically based on the fall of humanity from a primordial condition of grace, strength, perfection, beauty, and physical immortality; the divine dream of its practitioners was to re-establish the harmony which once existed between man and his <term>fontal source</term>, so as to rescue the individual, at least, from the miserable ruin of the race and to restore him to his original condition.

This, indeed, was unmistakably their clear and declared object. A certain **method of life** and a certain **medical regimen** were the means by which it was deemed possible to secure this resplendent rehabilitation. To achieve immortality by a medicine which had the quality of **renovating wasted tissues**, of **eliminating the germs of** disease, and arresting the progress of decay, was a grand upward step, a substantial realization of the dream. The possibility was universally admitted; the process was hoped for, longed for, toiled for, and often died for. Some claimed to have accomplished it, and many pretended to possess the secret; a few, carried away beyond sober reason by search, expectation, and desire, genuinely believed themselves to have attained the grand way, the true path, and may have been encouraged in their sublime self-deception by the actual discovery of powerful healing secrets which are unknown to modern science. It is at any rate reasonably certain that the <term>Elixir of Life</term>, the <term>Universal Medicine</term>, and the **renewal of youth** are concepts which

<summary>This page explores the alchemical goal of physical immortality, framing the pursuit of the Elixir of Life as a method to restore humanity to its original state of perfection and health. It describes the historical belief in medical regimens and secret medicines capable of arresting decay and renewing the human body.</summary>
<keywords>Elixir of Life, alchemists, thaumaturgic physics, transmutation, transcendentalism, immortality, medical regimen, Universal Medicine</keywords>

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Elixir of Life, moving from the philosophical goals of Western transcendentalism to the literal and physical pursuit of longevity as documented in historical and mystical texts.</meta>

->THE OCCULT SCIENCES.<-

<meta>Blue pencil underlining under "but to whom we must always recur for quotation"; vertical red pencil mark in the left margin next to the final paragraph of the main body.</meta>

were understood by the mystics in a literal sense, and no modern interpretation must be accepted which violates that understanding. But it also becomes evident through the study of the great transcendentalists that the double aspect of <term>alchemy</term> must be extended to the <term>life-elixir</term>; it was actual, it was physical, it was truly sought—there was neither pretense, nor allegory, nor subterfuge about it. However, it also included another side, a larger scope, a deeper search, and a higher meaning.<note>The following mystical commentary has been offered on this point: "The hidden understanding of the Elixir of Life is in the alchemical *Solve et Coagula*, when spirit is turned into matter, and vice versa. When a man knows his own spirit, he cannot die—that is, he cannot lose consciousness while in the flesh. He can put aside his earthly dwelling if he wishes, especially if he has allowed it to become feeble. He then disappears. If he has maintained his physical health perfectly, he can move to another country, appearing to die in a given place. But to do so, he must have friends with the necessary knowledge."</note>

<term>Eliphas Levi</term>, always open to the most adverse criticism, but to whom we must always return for a quotation, says: "To imprison a soul forever in a mummified human body—such would be the horrible solution of pretended immortality in the same body and on the same earth." That may be true enough, but impossible, unworthy, and gross though it may be, it was still sought by <term>transcendental science</term> in the West. Only the apostles of transcendental science would have cried out against the distorted presentation of their cherished idea; they would have said that their great elixir renews vitality, and that it gives immortality to youth rather than to a mummy.

When we turn from these higher considerations to the renewal of physical youth, which the <term>mystics</term> desired to achieve, we are brought into the presence of much that appears barbarous, absurd, and belonging to the awkward, clumsy, and stumbling infancy of medical science.

The entire subject of the life-elixir, the grand <term>palingenesis</term>, the <term>universal medicine</term>, and the perfect way of youth-renewal, has been exhaustively discussed in an extremely curious book published in Paris in 1716, entitled: *A History of those Persons who have lived more than a century, and of those who have Renewed their Youth.*

<summary>This page explores the dual nature of the Elixir of Life, emphasizing that it was sought as both a literal physical medicine for youth and a mystical tool for spiritual consciousness. It references Eliphas Levi's critiques of physical immortality and introduces historical literature regarding centenarians and youth renewal.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, life-elixir, Eliphas Levi, transcendental science, palingenesis, universal medicine, Solve et Coagula, mystics, longevity</keywords>

# THE ELIXIR OF LIFE.

*with the Secret of Rejuvenescence, drawn out by Arnold de Villeneuve, by M. de Longueville-Harcourt.* The fifteenth chapter discusses the possibility of renewal, with the curious illustrations that follow.

> "To renew youth is to enter once more into that happy season which gives to the human frame the pleasures and strength of the morning. Here it is to no purpose that we should speak of that problem so much discussed by the Wise—whether the art can be carried to such a pitch of excellence that old age itself should be made young. We know that Paracelsus has boasted of the metamorphic resources of his <term>Mercury of Life</term>, which not merely rejuvenates men but converts metals into gold; he who promised to others the years of the sibyls, or at least the 300 winters of Nestor, himself died at the age of thirty-seven. Let us turn rather to Nature, so admirable in her achievements, and do not think her capable only of destroying what she has produced at the moment she has begotten them. Is it possible that she will refuse to man, for whom all was created, what she grants to the stags, the eagles, and the serpents, who annually cast aside the mournful accompaniments of old age and assume the most brilliant and gracious qualities of the most joyous youth? Art, it is true, has not yet arrived at that peak of perfection from which it can renew our youth; but that which was unachieved in the past may be accomplished in the future—a marvel which may be more confidently expected because, in isolated cases, it has actually already taken place, as the facts of history make evident. By observing and following the manner in which nature performs such wonders, we may certainly hope to achieve this desirable transformation; and the first condition is a pleasant temperament, such as that which was possessed by Moses, of whom it is written that for one hundred and twenty years his sight never failed him.
> "The stag, eagle, and sparrow-hawk renew their youth. Aldrovandus has written on the rejuvenation of the eagle. Among the birds of the air, we are told by Pliny that the raven and the phoenix live, each of

> them, six hundred years. No one denies that the stag is renewed by feeding on vipers and serpents, while the apes of Caucasus, whose diet is pepper, provide a sovereign remedy for the lion, who grows young by devouring their flesh. Those who have written about the elephant maintain that its normal life is extended through three centuries, while the horse—which alone in creation participates in the natures of man, the lion, the ox, the sheep, the mule, the stag, the wolf, the fox, the serpent, and the hare, deriving three qualities from each—has occasionally survived with undiminished vigor for a hundred years. The serpent, who is instrumental in the rejuvenation of the stag, renews its own youth by shedding its scales; from all these considerations, it follows that it is not beyond belief that a similar miracle may be found in the superior order of the same creations from which man himself is derived, for man is surely not in a worse condition than the beasts whom he rules.”

From these considerations, the writer moves to an account of those persons, male and female, who are supposed to have renewed their youth. Unfortunately, the evidence is chiefly confined to the mythical periods of antiquity. Eson, the father of Jason, was wrapped by Medea in a quantity of warm herbs and aromatics, saturated with certain liquids that extracted the potent juices of these plants and restored the youth of the patient. A fountain is mentioned by Herodotus which restored strength to the elderly. The spring of Lucaga in America is credited by Peter Chieza with the same marvelous property. A similar fountain is located on an island of the Greek Archipelago, according to Andræus Baccius, and another in the neighborhood of Argos was called the Fountain of Canathus. Torquemada and Peter the Martyr, Aulus Gellius and Pliny, Lovrichius, and William Postel are cited in support of the reality of renewal; and when the historical evidence has been at length exhausted, M. de Longueville-Harcourt supplies, what is promised in his title, the “<term>arcanum</term>” of Arnold de Villeneuve for the operation of the great work of renewal.

# THE ELIXIR OF LIFE.

> "This marvelous process for the rehabilitation of Nature," says the author already cited, "is not to be found in the folio edition of the illustrious Arnold de Villeneuve; it is preserved in an ancient Latin manuscript, which came into the hands of a certain M. du Poirier, chief physician of the general hospital at Tours, who lent it to the Abbé de Vallemont, of Pomaine," by whom it was communicated to the author, M. de Longueville-Harcourt. <margin>99</margin> The rejuvenating process of Arnold de Villeneuve is supplemented by a recipe for the Universal Medicine, which is generally identified with the Elixir, but appears to be of lesser virtue. This special elaboration is the achievement of M. de Comiers, who had a doctrine that diet and sweating were certain remedies for everything. It was claimed to be the universal medicine in a liquid form, and was guaranteed to free or protect the partaker from every disease. The dose was from five to six drops in wine or broth, according to the nature of the illness."

According to Bernard Trévisan, a distinguished French adept, the reduction of the "philosophical stone" into mercurial water results in the alchemical elixir of life, which is the Hermetic <term>aurum potabile</term>. Its potent virtues are equal to the cure of every variety of disease, and it prolongs life beyond the ordinary limits. When the perfect elixir has been elaborated to the Red, it is said to transmute copper, lead, iron, and all metals into a purer gold than that which is found in the mines, while the same elixir elaborated to the White produces an excellent quality of wholly unalloyed silver.

Several formal recipes for the composition of the Universal Medicine are given in alchemical literature. That in <term>Albertus Parvus</term> requires eight pounds of sugar of mercury as its foundation. Those who partake of one dose night and morning will find their life prolonged and their health insured; while gout, sciatica, vertigo, and every internal complaint will be entirely and speedily eradicated.

At the solemn moment of death, the illustrious Benedictine, Trithemius, who is counted among the greatest of the Christian and Catholic hierophants, formulated a

> portion; and, immediately, the Soul, descending from the pyroplastic sphere, shall restore, by a marvelous embrace, its dead and deserted body. This is that world-renowned medicine about which so many have written, but which, nevertheless, so few have known.”

In the *Doctrine and Ritual of Transcendental Magic*, Eliphas Lévi informs his students that he has come into possession of Cagliostro’s great secret of rejuvenation, but his reasons for withholding it from publication will be readily understood. If it is necessary to pose as the protector of dangerous knowledge, one should at least do so consistently; but with true French fickleness, and without a word of warning—much less of explanation or defense of his change of position—he provides the recipe in his next book. By its nature, he makes evident the pure charlatanry of his previous scruples, which are entirely in character with the transcendental devices that abound in his earlier work.

By means of this preposterous regimen, which is too complicated to permit reproduction here, the impostor—who was not entirely an impostor—pretended to have extended his life over a period of several centuries. On the authority of Lévi, we learn that the apostles of the marvelous are convinced that the "Great Copt" <note>A title used by the occultist Alessandro Cagliostro.</note> is now located in America, where he is the supreme and invisible pontiff of the grand chaos of the spirit rappers <note>A reference to practitioners of Spiritualism.</note>.

Aristeus, the philosophical alchemist, is supposed to have delivered to his disciples what he terms the golden key of the Grand Work, which will render all metals diaphanous <note>Transparent or translucent.</note> and man himself immortal. The process appears to consist of the esoteric treatment of air, but whether this refers to the ordinary atmosphere or to something more concealed and recondite, we have no means of judging. It is congealed and distilled until it develops a divine sparkle and subsequently becomes liquefied. It is then subjected to heat and is reinforced by another atmosphere. After these and other treatments, the elixir, or solar marvel of all the sages, should reward the alchemical worker.

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "golden key" of the Grand Work mentioned by Aristeus and transitions into a critical evaluation of the practical feasibility of Hermetic secrets and the literature surrounding the Universal Medicine.</meta>

### THE OCCULT SCIENCES.

A secret may possibly be contained in this truly philosophical explanation, but the explanation itself is a secret, and an exceedingly great and monumental mystery. It is the concentrated center of the incomprehensible. However, we are informed that the principles bequeathed to the chosen followers of Aristeus have been studied by skillful practitioners of the *magnum opus* <note>The "Great Work" of alchemy, referring to the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. Their reflection has led to the brilliant conclusion that anyone who composes a mixture with the true balm of mercury will not work in vain. When this mixture is treated according to the methods of alchemical preparation and united with their master's elixir, it will perform every possible wonder that can be expected from such a supreme achievement.

Regardless of this, it is certain that following the methods of the Hermetists, no practical work can even be attempted in the physical realm because the necessary materials are missing. No investigation, no matter how diligent, can clarify secrets that have never been revealed; an exhaustive study leaves the entire subject exactly where the beginner first found it—in a chaos of clouds and darkness. It is highly probable that some of the minor recipes found in Hermetic books may contain medical secrets unknown to modern science, which could be recovered through careful experiment. Amid such a vast amount of material—mystic therapeutics, mystic chemistry, and mystic herbalism—there may indeed be something of value; however, it will not be Raymond Lully’s great Elixir, nor the true Metallic Medicine. These, if they ever existed, appear to be beyond recovery.

The literature regarding the Universal Medicine, as distinct from general alchemical literature, is not extensive. The work we have already cited is perhaps the most complete of its kind; however, another work that is extremely curious and should not be overlooked by the inquisitive is Salmon’s *Polygraphia*, which contains much information on the philosophers' Grand Elixir. Some of our information can be found in *Les Admirables Secrets d’ Albert le Grand* <note>"The Admirable Secrets of Albert the Great," a famous book of folk magic and medicine.</note>; there are also notes on potable gold in the *Dictionnaire Infernal* ("Infernal Dictionary") and in Migne’s encyclopedic *Dictionnaire des Sciences Occultes* ("Dictionary of Occult Sciences").

## CRYSTALLOMANCY.

ALTHOUGH it properly belongs to divination—an elementary branch of occultism—the Science of the Crystal is serious enough in character and important enough in its results to require a separate discussion.

It was discovered in the past that staring fixedly at a transparent and radiating object dazzled and troubled the eyes, eventually producing a condition in many subjects that we call hypnotic.

This artificial hypnotic state was achieved in countless ways, but by none more successfully than using substances like mirrors, crystals, and precious stones such as the <term>beryl</term>. Mirrors were more frequently used in ceremonial evocations; the crystal, on the other hand, was considered the perfect instrument for bringing forth supernatural revelations through visions. It was practiced in two ways, both of which were preceded by a formal consecration, or "charging" <note>A ritual process of dedicating or empowering an object for magical use.</note> of the powerful stone. Those who lacked the personal faculty of <term>clairvoyant lucidity</term> <note>A state of clear-sightedness or psychic vision often associated with hypnotic trances.</note> could operate through the mediation of a young girl or boy born in lawful marriage. Their physical purity and spiritual innocence—which in children more closely resembles the ideal of unfallen humanity—were believed to be intimately connected to success in the practice of all lawful magic. Furthermore, the predominance of intuition over rational perception during early youth was another distinct advantage.

The subject's imagination was influenced by the recitation of the charge, and perhaps further elevated by a minor ceremony. He might be draped, for example, in a mantle of violet silk and crowned with a garland of lilies. Incense would be burnt in his presence, or penetrating and delicious per-

<summary>This page introduces crystallomancy as a branch of occultism that uses transparent objects to induce hypnotic states and visions. It explains the ritual preparation of crystals and the traditional preference for using young, "innocent" children as mediums for these visions.</summary>
<keywords>crystallomancy, crystal gazing, hypnosis, beryl, divination, clairvoyance, ritual magic, scrying, ceremonial magic</keywords>

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page continues the detailed description of the ritual preparations for crystallomancy, moving from the physical adornment of the subject to the sensory environment and the resulting hypnotic experience, before discussing the practical tools of the craft.</meta>

->THE OCCULT SCIENCES.<-

scents, such as lilac and magnolia, might be spread around; his hands, feet, and forehead might be anointed with <term>chrism</term> <note>Consecrated oil used in religious or magical rituals.</note>, and an aromatic drink might be given to him. When these preparations were over, he was placed in a dim light in front of the magic crystal, and his eyes were fixed upon its surface. Gradually, for a suitable subject, a mist would gather in the depths of the globe; the inner faculties of the mind would expand like a flower, and the external senses would be at least partially suspended—except for the voice of the operator, with whom he was in <term>magnetic rapport</term> <note>A state of psychological or spiritual connection between a hypnotist and a subject.</note>. The crystal itself would vanish from the sight of the entranced person, the mist would melt or part, and he would be captivated by the appearance of a radiant being—the indwelling spirit of the stone—or some other vision he was influenced to see by the words or the will of the <term>Magus</term>.

This form of divination is undoubtedly one of the most innocent, pleasing, and successful methods of minor magical practice, and it is one which can be reproduced by a practitioner today with considerable ease. The true crystal has been credited with magnetic properties that assist the development of inner sight, but for success it is essential that the stone be of considerable size and shaped as either a perfect sphere or an egg. A good specimen is therefore expensive, but excellent imitations in glass may be easily obtained, which are said to be used by occult students with genuine results, and can be purchased for the cost of a few shillings. In the past, the favorite medium was the pale, water-green <term>beryl</term>; some practitioners preferred a clouded crystal, and every variety of precious stone has, at times, been pressed into service.

Evidence for the complete reliability of many recorded results obtained by the crystal is abundant; events occurring at a distance have been seen by the seer in his trance and later verified in detail. By virtue of what law such occurrences are possible is beyond the scope of our current investigation; we are concerned with the facts alone. Explanations may be ingenious and plausible, and many have indeed been—

# CRYSTALLOMANCY. 105

devised, but at the present stage of psychological experiment, the hypothesis seems decidedly premature.

<term>Crystallomancy</term> in its second and more advanced form was practiced without the intervention of a medium, when the <term>Magus</term> believed himself to possess the required faculty of <term>clairvoyance</term>.

In modern times, the rules for obtaining a vision have been exceedingly simplified. A practical student of the subject has reduced them to the following points:

> “Keep the crystal clean. Do not be too liberal in allowing strangers to handle it, unless you are going to look for them, in which case they ought to hold it in their hands for a few minutes. Hold the crystal between your fingers and thumb, or on a table if it is flat at the end. If the crystal appears hazy or dull, it is a true sign that you will see; the crystal will subsequently clear, and the forms will manifest. If it is required to see events which are taking place at a distance—say, in Australia—look lengthwise through the crystal; if you can see at all, you are likely to accomplish your object.”

But there are <term>rituals</term> contained in old manuscripts and books of magic which insist upon a far more elaborate ceremonial.

> “Those who desire to establish communication with good spirits in the crystal,” says one of these older authorities, “must lead a religious life, and keep themselves unspotted from the world. The operator must make himself clean and pure, using frequent ablutions and prayers for at least three days before he attempts the practice, and the moon must be waxing. If he chooses, he may have one or two wise and discreet persons as companions and assistants, but he and they must equally conform to the methods and rules of the art. The operator must be firm, strong in faith, great in confidence, and he must be careful that no portion of the ceremonies is omitted if he desires to achieve success, for the accomplishment of his design depends on the exactitude with which the entire ritual is performed. The <term>invocant</term> may perform the practice at any time of the year, provided the two <term>luminaries</term> are in a fortunate aspect, in conjunction with fortunate planets; when the sun is in

In order to carry out his work, the <term>operator</term> must have a small room in a secluded part of the house, such as an attic or a basement kitchen. It must be clean and neat, but with no lavish ornaments to distract his attention. The floor must be well scrubbed and perfectly level, so as to receive the lines of the various circles and characters which are to be traced upon it. The room must be free from intruders and from the rush of business, and it should be locked when not in use. Every preparation belonging to the art must be made during the <term>moon’s increase</term>. The <term>operator</term> must be provided with a small table covered with a white linen cloth; a chair should be placed in the room, along with the materials required for a fire, which will be necessary to burn the incense appropriate to the planet that governs the hour of practice. A torch and two wax candles, placed in gilded or brass candlesticks—highly polished and engraved—must also be provided, together with a pair of compasses and several minor accessories, such as twine, a knife, a pair of scissors, etc. The <term>magic sword</term> must be made of pure steel; it must be supplemented by a <term>wand</term> of hazel wood, of a year’s growth and a yard in length, engraved with appropriate sacred characters. Every instrument, large or small, must be entirely new and must be consecrated before use.

<margin>Vertical red line mark in margin.</margin>
The most important addition to the practice is the <term>crystal</term>, which must be about four inches in diameter, or at least the size of a large orange. It must be properly ground and polished so as to be free from specks or spots; it should be enclosed in a frame of ivory, ebony, or boxwood, which should also be highly polished. Sacred names must be written around it in raised letters of gold; the pedestal to which the frame is fixed may be made of any suitable wood, properly polished. The crystal, like the other instruments, must be consecrated before being used and should be kept in a new box or drawer under lock and key. The names to be engraved on the frame are, at the North, TETRAGRAMMATON; at the East, EMANUEL; at the South, AGLA; and at the West, ADONAY. The

# CRYSTALLOMANCY.

In consecrating all the instruments and other accessories of the art, the <term>invocant</term> must repeat the forms of consecration while placing his hands upon the different articles, and his face must be turned toward the East.

Once the consecrations are finished, he may then arrange the table with the crystal on it, along with a candlestick containing a wax candle on each side of the circle. The circle should be seven feet in diameter and must enclose a mystic square, whose angles at the top point must, in each case, touch the circumference. Both figures must be appropriately inscribed with sacred names and mystical characters and symbols. When the operator enters the circle with his companions, if there are any, it must be during the day and hour of Mercury, while the moon is increasing, and the operations must be prefaced by an earnest invocation of VASSAGO, who is the spirit of the crystal.

If the conjurations are repeated often, if the operator is patient and constant in his perseverance, and not disheartened or dismayed by any boredom or delay, the spirit—as the ritual claims—will at last appear. At that point, he must be bound with the <term>Bond of Spirits</term>, after which he may be conversed with freely. “That this is a true experiment, and that the spirit has been bound to the fellowship and service of a magic artist before, is very certain,” says the same authority; “but as all aerial spirits are very powerful, it will be well for the operator not to leave the limits of the circle until a few minutes after the apparition has been formally given permission to depart.”

It seems clear that the intelligences which manifest to the <term>Magus</term> through the graceful medium of the crystal are members of an elementary hierarchy, and the intelligent reader will discern that the force which controls them is the will of the operator, rendered magnetic by the discipline of the ritual, and expanded as well as directed by the elaborate, if strange, ceremony. He will probably conclude that the so-called divine names, which are frequently distorted versions of dead languages, or are simply chaotic, are

impotent symbols in themselves until they are vivified by the intelligent and measured desperation of the operator, and that the entire *modus operandi* is calculated to work upon the internal faculties of the man, rather than upon the fears of the spirit. If the will and the imagination of the operator are sufficiently strong, and at the same time sufficiently controlled to dispense with ritual formalities, an effect may be produced without them. Paracelsus, relying upon the force of the concealed *magnes* <note>A term for the magnetic or attractive power inherent in humans</note> in the arcane man, denounces the ceremonial of magic with the violence of a medieval hierophant. At the same time, if the rites are observed at all, they must be observed *in toto* <note>entirely</note>, for the smallest deviation will react on the will of the operator; as Eliphas Lévi indicates, it will produce apprehension and embarrassment which may frustrate the entire process.

Among the many persons who in recent years have conducted experiments with the crystal, one of the most successful was the late Frederick Hockley, who devoted his life to the collection and transcription of works on the secret sciences, and who was also a practical student of several branches of magic. He was not himself a seer in the ordinary sense of the term, and his dealings with the spirits of the crystal were conducted through the mediation of clairvoyants. When his extraordinary library was dispersed, a long series of communications obtained through the crystal—extending to several volumes written in his own hand—became a center of interest for collectors of occult manuscripts. The success obtained by Mr. Hockley in this, as in other departments of the magic arts, had considerable evidential value, as it was achieved by a private gentleman who never posed as a mystic, who pursued his experiments in secret for his personal satisfaction only, who never published the results of his researches, and who sought neither notoriety nor financial gain.

<meta>This page transitions from a discussion of the operator's internal state and the history of Frederick Hockley to a focused exploration of the Will as the central mechanism of magic, specifically through the lens of Joseph Glanvil's writings.</meta>

# THE COMPOSITION OF TALISMANS.

<note>Vertical red mark in the right margin adjacent to the first five lines of the paragraph.</note>
The English Platonist, Joseph Glanvil—author of *Sadducismus Triumphatus*, a powerful investigation into witchcraft, of a "Key to the Grand Mysteries in Relation to Sin and Evil," and of other tracts and treatises—was an important mystic of his time, but he is now known only by one anecdote and one profound sentence. The anecdote is that of the Scholar Gipsy, who abandoned his Oxford studies to lead a nomadic life and to discover a secret known by the Romani people, through which they could strangely influence the wills of others. Who among us is unfamiliar with the enduring beauty of Matthew Arnold’s elegy based on this anecdote? As transcendentalists, we should be grateful to the gifted poet who has indirectly preserved the memory of the deceased mystic. But Glanvil is also immortalized by a sentence that Poe unearthed and placed as the central idea of one of his wildest stories. “And the Will lies within it, which does not die. Who knows the mysteries of the Will and its strength? For God is but a great Will pervading all things by the nature of its intent. Man does not surrender himself to the angels or to death completely, except through the weakness of his own feeble will.” Both the anecdote and the sentence are taken from *The Vanity of Dogmatizing*, a title that could respectfully be recommended to the attention of many people today. This single sentence is, in essence, the summary of magic; for the seat of the soul's power <note>Blue checkmark in the right margin.</note> is said to reside in the Will of man. This Will is the great magical agent, the instrument for developing all inner faculties, the life of conscious action, and the sole underlying principle of mystical rites and ceremonies.

As the will of a strong man can influence a weaker

<summary>This page introduces the philosopher Joseph Glanvil and his influence on literature, specifically highlighting his assertion that the human Will is the fundamental agent and principle behind all magic and mysticism.</summary>
<keywords>Joseph Glanvil, Will, magic, Sadducismus Triumphatus, Matthew Arnold, Edgar Allan Poe, mysticism, The Vanity of Dogmatizing, Scholar Gipsy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the human Will as the primary magical agent, moving from Joseph Glanvil’s theories to the specific application of the Will in the creation of talismans.</meta>

<warning>The left margin contains vertical ink and pencil lines marking specific passages.</warning>

mind—just as it can command the magnetic patient to sleep and the clairvoyant to see—so it was believed in the past that it could endow an inert substance with an occult force. This force was transferred from the innermost individuality of the operator through a great mental projection, and this was Talismanic Magic. The principle was degraded by the ignorance and superstition of the past; the power was separated from its connection with the operating mind and was instead attributed to the methods and the material of the talisman, or to the mindless observation of specific times and seasons. The vitality of the belief departed, and the vivid realization of man’s absolute power over all things, animate and inanimate, was replaced by a submissive worship of occult forces. These forces exercised a destructive and unreasoning tyranny over both ordinary men and the magus.

At its best, talismanic magic seems to rank as one of the curiosities of esoteric science that is primarily of archaeological interest. We may recognize the principle it involves as a legitimate part of mysticism. This principle concerns the transfer of human magnetism to inert objects, which is paralleled in a lower kingdom of nature by the transfer of a similar and equally mysterious power from the lodestone to iron. Beyond this, and beyond the role played by the talisman in the ceremonies of evocation, it is a branch of mysticism that has little to justify its revival today. It encourages intellectual weakness and directs the student’s attention to the trivialities of transcendental art. The talisman has been concisely defined as an astrological symbol engraved upon a sympathetic stone in correspondence with the constellation or star represented by that symbol. Its name is derived from a Greek word that signifies a symbol, image, or figure. It is a sign that stands as the symbolic equivalent of a force or influence. The emblem must be engraved upon the material of the talisman by an operator who is, at the very least, philosophically acquainted with the nature of the influence it signifies. He must be able to concentrate his entire attention and his undivided will upon the work while his mental powers are isolated from all distractions.

<summary>This page defines talismanic magic as the projection of an operator's mental will and magnetism into an object, though it notes that the practice often declined into superstition. It describes the talisman as a symbolic tool that requires intense concentration and astrological correspondence to function.</summary>

# THE COMPOSITION OF TALISMANS.

The doctrines and practice of talismanic magic, as it flourished amidst the Christianity of the West, are derived from the Kabbalah, which in turn was indebted to Chaldea, Egypt, and Babylon; for if the talisman is younger than religion, it is as old as superstition and as wide in the sphere of its influence.

The seal of Solomon is the most famous of all talismans, and nothing was believed to be impossible for those who possessed it, as it had power over all spirits, which is equivalent to saying that it represented a strong psychic force. The five-pointed star of the microcosm, which is the Star of Bethlehem, the star of the Magi, and the mystic sign of humanity—just as the double triangle of Solomon was the symbol of the universe, the macrocosm, or the great world, man being *minutum mundum*—this emblem was of equal repute and, in the human sphere, of even greater power than the seal of the mythic monarch of transcendental Judaism. With the single point, or horn, in the ascendant, it represented White Magic; reversed, it was the emblem of sorcery, witchcraft, and the grotesque extravagances of the "Sabbath."

Occasionally, talismanic influence has been accredited with a broader range. Five varieties are enumerated by Elihu Rich:—1. The astrological, having the characters of the heavenly signs or constellations. This branch was developed by Paracelsus, who refers talismanic virtue to the seven genii of the seven planets. 2. The magical, engraved with symbolical figures, and the names of unknown angels. The mystical designs which are found upon Gnostic gems may be referred to this branch. 3. The mixed, engraved with celestial signs and barbaric words. 4. The *sigilla planetarum*, composed of Hebrew numeral letters, or their equivalents in Roman and Arabic ciphers. 5. Hebrew names and characters. This classification is, however, exceedingly arbitrary. The majority of talismans are sufficiently composite in design to embrace all of these varieties. Within the historical period, they were all formed under appropriate planetary influences, and to

It also outlines Elihu Rich's five-part classification of talismans, including astrological and magical varieties.</summary>

this extent, if no further, was of a distinctly astronomical character. A more accurate distinction, though one originating in superstition, may be based on the difference between artificial talismans created through the science of the Magus and substances which were credited with a natural talismanic virtue, as in the case of precious stones.

The virtues attached to the talisman were usually of a practical character, and an important position was assigned to them in the mysteries of the healing art. Just as in modern mesmeric therapeutics, the health-giving power of the physician is supposed to be transmitted to a patient at a distance by means of magnetized water or another suitable medium, so it would appear that the power of the Magus had entered into the talisman by the penetrating force of will, and could exercise a similar influence over a sick person when it had been created for a healing purpose. But the talisman would be more potent than the magnetic water of the unilluminated mesmerist. The science of the Magus enabled him to combine the force of his own will and intelligence with the most favorable influences of the entire sidereal world. The "genius" of the dominant planet of the moment—which, in other words, was the specific influence and destiny which it was astrologically supposed to radiate—entered into the act of creation; the talisman was imbued with its favorable and benevolent power, which was superior to that of the Magus (whose participation was practically forgotten from the moment the creation was over), and the consecrated object became the talisman of that planet under which it was consecrated. The alleged value of planetary influences will be appreciated in a subsequent portion of this book; here it is sufficient to observe that the belief in intervention from the astral world greatly assisted talismanic healing through its effect on the imagination, which by its natural therapeutics can accomplish what is impossible for medicine, and is the chief agent in all so-called miraculous cures—whether magnetic, magical, or talismanic.

# THE COMPOSITION OF TALISMANS.

The power of the seven planets which were known to the astronomy of the <gloss>circled</gloss> ancients is never stronger or more evident than when it acts through the intervention of the seven metals of alchemy, each of which corresponds to one of these celestial bodies. The metals which are assigned to each of the various planets are said to have been determined by the sublime insight of the Kabbalists. <margin>vertical line in red ink</margin> However this may be, it is certain that, from a magical perspective, gold was the metal of the Sun and of the first day of the week; silver of the Moon and Monday; iron of Mars and Tuesday; mercury (quicksilver) of Mercury and Wednesday; pewter of Jupiter and Thursday; copper or brass of Venus and Friday; and lead of Saturn and Saturday.

From Christian’s *Histoire de la Magie*, from the curiosities of the "Little Albert," and similar author-<margin>red checkmark</margin>ities, we may gather a clear understanding of the most important astral talismans. It should, however, be noted that, as in other ceremonial matters, there is little agreement on this subject among writers on magic. A variety of pentacles are attributed to each of the planets in the so-called "Keys of Solomon," which differ entirely from those described by Paracelsus, while among modern authors, Eliphas Lévi has developed a system of his own.

I. The <gloss>underlined</gloss> **TALISMAN OF THE SUN** must be composed of <gloss>underlined</gloss> pure and fine gold, fashioned into a circular plate and well-polished on both sides. A serpentine circle, enclosed by a pentagram, must be engraved on the front side with a diamond-pointed engraving tool. The back must bear a human head in the center of the six-pointed star of Solomon, which shall itself be surrounded by the name of the solar intelligence <gloss>circled</gloss> **PI-RHÉ**, written in the characters of the Magi. This talisman is supposed to ensure for its bearer the goodwill of influential persons. It is a preservative against death by heart disease, fainting, aneurysm, and epidemic diseases. It must be composed on a Sunday during the passage of the moon through the first ten degrees of Leo, and when that

### III. The **TALISMAN OF MARS**

The **TALISMAN OF MARS** must be made of a well-polished circular plate of the finest iron, having the dimensions of an ordinary medal. The symbol of a sword in the center of a pentagram must be engraved on the obverse side. A lion’s head surrounded by a six-pointed star must appear on the reverse side, with the letters of the name **EROTOSI**, the planetary genius of Mars, above the outer angles. This talisman serves as a protection against all conspiracies of enemies. It averts the chance of death in brawls and battles, in epidemics and fevers, and from corroding ulcers. It also neutralizes the danger of a violent death as a punishment for a crime when it is foretold in a birth horoscope.

This talisman must be created on a Tuesday, during the passage of the moon through the first ten degrees of Aries or Sagittarius, and when, moreover, it is in a favorable aspect with Saturn and Mars. This vessel must be ground into powder and buried in a secluded place once the operation is completed. Finally, the talisman must be sewn into a pouch of red silk and fastened to the chest with ribbons of the same material, folded and knotted in the shape of a cross.

IV. The **TALISMAN OF MERCURY** must be formed from a circular plate of <gloss>solidified quicksilver</gloss>, or according to another account, from an amalgam of silver, mercury, and pewter, with the dimensions of an ordinary medal and well-polished on both sides. A winged <gloss>caduceus</gloss>, with two serpents entwined around it, must be engraved in the center of a pentagram on the obverse side. This talisman must be composed on a Wednesday, when the moon is passing through the first ten degrees of Gemini or Scorpio and is in a favorable aspect with Saturn and Mercury. The consecration consists of exposing it to the smoke of a perfume composed of benzoin, mace, and storax, burned with the dried stalks of the lily, the narcissus, fumitory, and marjoram, placed in a clay chafing dish that has never been used for any other purpose. After the task is completed, this dish must be reduced to powder and buried in an undisturbed place. The talisman of Mercury is considered a defense in all types of commerce and business industry. If buried underground in a place of business, it will attract customers and prosperity. It protects all who wear it from epilepsy and madness. It averts death by <gloss>murder and poison</gloss>; it is a safeguard against treasonous schemes; and it brings about prophetic dreams when it is worn on the head while sleeping. It is fastened to the chest by a ribbon of purple silk, folded and tied in the form of a cross, and the talisman itself is enclosed in a pouch of the same material.

This talisman must be composed on a Thursday, during the passage of the moon through the first ten degrees of Libra, and when it is also in a favorable aspect with Saturn and Jupiter. The consecration consists of exposing it to the smoke of a perfume composed of incense, ambergris, balm, grain of Paradise, saffron, and mace (which is the second coat of the nutmeg). These must be burned with wood from the oak, poplar, fig tree, and pomegranate, and placed in a new earthen dish, which must be ground into powder and buried in a quiet spot at the end of the ceremony. The talisman must be wrapped in a pouch of sky-blue silk, suspended on the breast by a ribbon of the same material, folded and fastened in the form of a cross.

The talisman of Jupiter is held to attract the benevolence and sympathy of everyone to the wearer. It averts anxieties, favors honorable enterprises, and increases well-being in proportion to one's social condition. It is a protection against unforeseen accidents and the perils of a violent death when such is threatened by Saturn in the birth horoscope. It also preserves one from death by diseases of the liver, by inflammation of the lungs, and by that cruel affection of the spinal marrow which is termed *tabes dorsalis* in medicine.

VI. The **TALISMAN OF VENUS** must be formed of a circular plate of purified and well-polished copper. It must be of the ordinary dimensions of a medal, perfectly polished on both its sides. The talisman of Venus is credited with extraordinary power in cementing the bonds of love and harmony between husbands and wives. It averts from those who wear it the spite and plots of hatred. Its last and most singular quality is its power to change the hostility of an enemy into a love and devotion that will be proof against every temptation; this rests on the sole condition that such a person should be persuaded to drink a liquid in which the talisman has been dipped.

The talisman of Saturn was affirmed to be a safeguard against death by apoplexy and cancer, bone decay, consumption, dropsy, paralysis, and decline; it was also a preservative against the possibility of being buried alive while in a trance, and against the danger of violent death by secret crime, poison, or ambush. If the leader of an army in wartime were to bury the talisman of Saturn in a place that was feared might fall into the hands of the enemy, the boundary established by the presence of the talisman could not be crossed by the opposing army, which would quickly withdraw in discouragement or in the face of a determined assault.

The names of the presiding planetary intelligences given in the preceding pages are claimed to be Egyptian in origin. In the Teraphim, which were the talismans of medieval Kabbalistic rabbis, these names were replaced by Michael, Gabriel, Samael, Raphael, Zachariel, Anael, and Oriphiel.

Talismanic theories and practice are almost exclusively confined to the operations of White Magic; but we learn, on the authority of Christian, that the seven celestial talismans also had their infernal counterparts; there was both the white and the black talisman. It was possible for a corrupt Magus to infuse the poison of his corrupted will into the metal, and to combine it with the diabolical influence of the seven demons who stood in eternal opposition to the seven planetary angels. To the angel of Saturn was opposed the demon Nabam; to that of Jupiter, the demon Acham; to the angel of Mars, the demon Nambroth; to the Genius of Venus, the demon Lilith or Naemah; to that of Mercury, the demon Astaroth or Tharthae; to the angel of the Moon, the demon Satan.

This is especially true for the talisman called the *Almadel*, which is described in the earliest known work on magic printed in English. Through the use of this instrument, Solomon—the father of Jewish mysticism (in the sense that all kabbalistic arts, from the heights of theurgic experiment to the lowest science of the abyss, are freely attributed to him)—is said to have gained his great and sublime wisdom from the chief angels who govern the four altitudes of the world. "For you should observe," says this curious ritual, "that there are four altitudes in the world, which represent the four corners of the world—East, West, North, and South—these being subdivided twelve times, three to every part." The angels of each of these altitudes have their particular virtues and powers.

The mysteries of talismanic magic have occupied the scholarship of most writers on the practical branches of mysticism. Paracelsus is the foremost authority; but there is a wealth of information in the *Occult Philosophy* of Cornelius Agrippa. *The Magus* by Francis Barrett is an accessible book that summarizes previous literature. *Secrets Merveilleux de la Magie Naturelle et Cabalistique du Petit Albert* also contains talismanic secrets.

# DIVINATION.

The circle of each of the physical sciences is surrounded by a border of light, easy, and pleasing experiments which are themselves extremely minor, but are like so many gateways to deep knowledge; they should be valued for where they lead, rather than for what they are. The depths of esoteric science also have their light, imaginative borderland where the beginner may amuse himself, and where the empty interest of the modern *socialite*—who describes herself as "*highly occult*"—finds enough to support a superficial and sentimental interest in the mysteries of psychic force. Such are the methods of divination that abound in old books and in the folklore of all European peoples. They are introductory and minor experiments with the powers of the inner man; if the conditions necessary for these powers to manifest in any form are met, they are successful, they cause wonder, and they encourage belief. If the ignorance and stupidity of the practitioner, or the pointlessness of a specific method, causes a failed result, the whole science is condemned. Yet physics, as much as psychology—though each in its own way—depends on specific conditions for success. The friction of silk and sealing wax may produce a basic phenomenon of magnetism, but the friction of cloth and clay will produce no such result, and this is no fault of the science of magnetism.

<note>Pencil mark and vertical line in the right margin next to the following lines</note>
It should be clearly established from the start that all forms of divination are simply methods for exercising the intuitive faculties; they are intended to cause that temporary suspension of the external senses known as the hypnotic state. In some varieties, the phenomena of a complete trance are brought out in a suitable

<summary>The author introduces divination as a preliminary branch of esoteric science that functions by exercising a person's intuitive faculties and inducing a hypnotic state. He argues that the success of divination depends on specific conditions, much like experiments in physical sciences like magnetism.</summary>
<keywords>divination, esoteric science, psychic force, folklore, physics, psychology, magnetism, hypnotic condition, trance, intuition</keywords>

Those which are exclusively concerned with the performance of a hollow rite and produce no impression on the inner nature are either worthless frauds or foolish, superstitious practices.

A great deal of futility is mixed with divination; it is essentially trivial in character; in itself, it can produce no valuable results. The student of discernment and sense will not waste his time and his powers on the small wonders of magic art; he will instead gain entrance into the larger field of achievement.

The word "divination" is occasionally used in a broad philosophical sense which gives it a different character. Its significance is extended until it includes the sublime dream of astrology, spiritual visions, and the gift of inspired prophecy. According to the common meaning of the term, "to divine" is to guess what we do not know, says Lévi, but its true significance is indescribable in its sublimity. To divine (*divinare*) is to exercise divinity. To be a diviner in the full force of the term is, therefore, to be divine, and something even more mysterious. In a popular and elementary work, we have judged it advisable to use the word "divination" in its popular and narrower sense. The art in itself predates any historical period and is therefore supposed to have descended from primitive tradition. "In the first ages of the earth," says the Reverend Henry Thompson, "some means of communication between God and man, with which we are now unfamiliar, existed, as appears from the history of Cain and Abel; and the same circumstance teaches us that these means were connected with sacrifice, an extensive source of divination in later ages. After the corruption of mankind had banished those real signs of divine interest with which they had been originally favored, they nonetheless tried to obtain counsel and information through the same external observances; but, finding them no longer effective, they invented a multitude of superstitious ceremonies, which, in the progress of religious corruption and under the influence of idolatry,"

Evolutionists who are also mystics, and mystics who are rational men, will reject definitive opinions on the origin of religious beliefs and practices of the prehistoric period. It is enough for our purpose to know that divination flourished in Egypt, Chaldea, and Assyria, among the Babylonians and Ethiopians, and that in the most distant regions of the globe we find a striking similarity, extending even to details, among its practiced methods.

Over one hundred recorded forms of divination are known to Western magic. Those which are in use in the East, and among uncivilized nations, are probably innumerable. In selecting the following varieties for the information and use of the student, it is fair to note that they are gathered from several sources—from Latin, French, and English writers—and are reproduced with only slight adaptation of the original wording.

### AEROMANCY.

This is the art which (sometimes under the alternative name, Meteoromancy) is concerned with the prediction of things to come by the observation of atmospheric variations and the different phenomena of the air, particularly those of thunder, lightning, and fiery meteors. It is by virtue of this divination that the appearance of a comet has been supposed to portend the death of a great man. In his massive work upon magic, François de la Torre-Blanca, who follows Psellus, asserts that the true aeromantic art is the prediction of the future by the summoning of spirits in the air, or the pictorial representation of things to come, by the aid of demons, in a cloud, as in a magic lantern. Under this theory, it would simply be a branch of ceremonial magic; on the other hand, it may be assumed that the observation of powerful atmospheric phenomena—of sunset displays, auroral lights, of peaceful midnight splendors, of stars and storms and lightning—the mere prolonged contemplation of "the magical, measureless distance," would profoundly excite the imaginations of sensitive persons, and transform the

Heaven is thus the mirror of the human soul, and when we think that we are reading the stars, it is ourselves we are reading."

### ALECTROMANCY.

From the standpoint of magical folklore, the rooster is a bird of much interest and many virtues. It is endowed with the remarkable power of putting the infernal hosts to flight, and according to <insert>underlined</insert> Peter Delancre <margin>vertical orange line in margin</margin> in his *Tableau de l’inconstance des Démons*, the devil—who is the lion of hell—will vanish the moment he hears the voice of this <insert>underlined</insert> domestic fowl. It is also popularly supposed that the natural lion, who is the king of beasts, can be subdued and put to flight by it. The entire phantasmagoria of the Black Sabbath <insert>underlined</insert> disappears when the rooster crows; to prevent him from sounding his clarion during the night hours and routing the whole assembly, the sorcerer, instructed by the devil, anointed the bird's head with olive oil and twined a collar of vine leaves around its neck. But while his voice was hostile to the midnight mysteries of the wizard world, his flesh was supposed to possess valuable virtues in sorcery. In particular, an ancient and venerable method of divination was practiced through the use of this bird of the gods and of Aesculapius. The *<term>modus operandi</term>* is as follows: A circle must be traced on the ground in a spot free from observation, and it must be divided into twenty-four equal spaces inscribed with the letters of the alphabet, rejecting J and U, which are usually represented in ancient writings by their counterparts, I and V. <margin>pencil squiggle in margin</margin> A grain of wheat

Then the diviner, while still holding the rooster, should repeat, “*O Deus Creator omnium, qui firmamentum pulchritudine stellarum formasti, constituens eas in signa et tempora, infunde virtutem tuam operibus nostris, ut per opus in eis consequamur effectum*.” Subsequently, while placing the bird within the circle, he must repeat these two verses from the Psalms: “*Domine dilexi decorem domûs tuae et locum habitationis tuae. Domine Deus virtutum, converte nos, ostende faciem suam, et salvi erimus*.”

All that remains for the operator, after these preliminaries, is to note very carefully which letters the bird picks up the grains from, which should be invariably replaced by others until his meal is finished. From the collection of letters thus obtained, the skill of the anagrammatist can generally extract an oracle which will give information on the desired subject. It will be seen that this form of divination is entirely a matter of chance; instead of appealing to the inner faculties of a clairvoyant subject, it makes use of an undiscriminating instrument, and as such, the entire rite is a pure and absolute superstition.

The whole was well mixed, and the seekers, who should be several in number, each received an equal share. Subsequently, each of them unrolled the strips that had fallen to his lot and extracted from the collection of words and phrases the information of which he

### ALPHITOMANCY.

This form of divination is sometimes considered important and is credited with great antiquity, but it is superstitious and worthless from a transcendental perspective. When several persons were accused or suspected of a certain crime, and when it was desired to discover the true culprit, each of them was forced to swallow a piece of hard bread. Those who could do so without difficulty were dismissed without a stain upon their character. Those who nearly choked in the act were held to be guilty. When the test was successfully passed by everyone, it may be concluded that the esoteric investigators looked further afield for the criminal. The pellet of bread was called the <term>corsned</term>, or morsel of execration, and it is the origin of the common oath,

> "If I deceive you, may my next mouthful of food choke me."

The practical method was as follows: pure barley flour, unmixed with yeast, was kneaded with salt and milk, baked in the ashes while wrapped in greasy paper, and finally rubbed with vervain leaves. The mixture became exceedingly hard, and as beginning to choke while swallowing an unchewed lump could only be avoided by a miracle, many guilty persons were naturally discovered by its means. In the vicinity of Lavinium, there is said to have been a sacred wood where a version of this idiotic process was practiced as a test of virginity. A dragon—otherwise a serpent—was kept captive by the priests of the place, and on certain occasions, young women were commissioned to feed him. Their eyes were bandaged, and they were led into the grotto carrying cakes of honey and barley flour; those whose offerings were refused by the discriminating reptile were supposed to have lost their honor.

## AMNIOMANCY.

Divination was practiced by the Greeks using the caul of a newborn child. The superstition was extremely simple and quite foolish, consisting merely of an inspection of the color that the membrane happened to show. A fortunate future was predicted for the owner of a red-tinted caul, while bad omens were drawn from a dark or bluish appearance.

## ANTHROPOMANCY.

From gross superstition, divination occasionally moved toward foul and revolting practices. Predictions were made by examining the internal organs of disemboweled men and women; under the name of anthropomancy, this ritual is very old. It is mentioned in Herodotus, who informs us that Menelaus, when held in Egypt by opposing winds, sacrificed local children to satisfy his cruel curiosity and sought information about his destiny from signs in their stomachs. Fortunately, the nature of the signs and the method of their interpretation have not been recorded. Similar but unconfirmed stories are told of Julian the Apostate, who, in his necromantic rituals and nighttime sacrifices, is said to have sacrificed many children in order to consult their intestines. When he stayed at Carra, in Mesopotamia, during his last expedition, it is claimed that he retired with some accomplices into the Temple of the Moon; when their ungodly activities were over, they left the temple locked and sealed, with a guard stationed around it to keep everyone away until their return. Julian died, however, in the war, and when the temple was opened during the reign of the Emperor Jovian, a woman was found hanging by her hair with her hands outstretched, her stomach cut open, and the liver torn out. However, the memory of the Emperor Julian has been slandered by his rivals, and the reports of his enemies should be received with extreme caution.

<summary>This page describes Amniomancy, the Greek practice of divining a child's future from their birth caul, and Anthropomancy, the ancient and controversial practice of human sacrifice for divination, specifically citing accounts involving Menelaus and Julian the Apostate.</summary>
<keywords>Amniomancy, Anthropomancy, divination, caul, human sacrifice, Herodotus, Menelaus, Julian the Apostate, Mesopotamia, Emperor Jovian</keywords>

## ARITHMOMANCY.

The mystical properties of numbers were developed by Pythagoras and Plato, and the science of esoteric mathematics passed from the Greeks to the Kabbalists, who transmitted it to Western mystics; Louis Claude de Saint-Martin was one of the last and most distinguished of its initiates. The powers, virtues, and mysteries attributed to numbers by the ancients are similar to those already described as the properties of the Hebrew occult alphabet.

> “In evaluating these doctrines,” says Elihu Rich, “one must remember that all movement, proportion, time, and, in short, every concept of quantity and harmony, can be represented by numbers. Therefore, whatever qualities are attributed to these concepts may also be expressed by numbers as the signs of occult virtues and laws. Philosophers know that the movements of nature are rhythmical; physicians have observed this in the cyclical nature of diseases; and the designation of the seventh day as a Sabbath has added a religious obligation to this natural law. The *three*, the *ten*, and the *twelve* are also numbers of well-known importance, and *one* is the most divine of all, as it expresses the unity of God and the inclusion of all things in perfect harmony. The use of numbers in divination has taken many curious forms. It is enough to mention here Gematria, the first division of the Cabbala, which teaches how to calculate the numerical value of the letters in specific words and to draw conclusions from the relationship between the sum of one text and another. This method converts the Bible into a book written entirely with numbers, and some strange results are obtained—probably about as close to the truth as Rabbinical astrology. Some curious properties of perfect, amicable, and other numbers have been explained by the English Platonist Thomas Taylor in his *Theoretic Arithmetic*. The most valuable ancient remains connected with this subject are found in the *Chaldean Oracles of Zoroaster*.”

It was the custom of the Greeks to examine the number and value of the letters in the names of two

The Chaldeans divided their alphabet into three parts, which they attributed to the seven planets, and extracted their omens accordingly. It is evident that such calculations must produce totally opposite results in different languages, and unless nationality acts as a determining element in destiny, it is difficult to look upon these numerical arts as anything but ingenious curiosities.

### ASTRAGALOMANCY

This was a form of divination using a pair of ordinary dice, which might be thrown together or singly. If one wishes to obtain information on any subject by this method, including the mysteries of the future, the question must be written upon paper which has been passed through the smoke of a fire of juniper wood. The writing must be placed face downward on the table, and the dice must then be thrown. The result of each cast must be converted into its alphabetical equivalent and carefully noted. If both dice are used, the process must be continued until the numbers from 2 to 12 have all been obtained, at which point the intuition of the operator must be concentrated on the collection of letters. If the order in which they are produced results in an intelligible message, he will have cause to be gratified. If no sense can be extracted, he must seek the required revelation in another rite. The alphabetical equivalent of the numbers has been given as follows:— 1 = A; 2 = E; 3 = I or Y; 4 = O; 5 = U; 6 = B; 7 = C, K, or Q; 8 = D or T; 9 = F, S, X, or Z; 10 = G or J; 11 = L, M, or N; 12 = R. As most of the numbers may signify any of three letters, the calculation will be somewhat difficult. The letter H is omitted, as it is as unnecessary to successful divination as it is to the speech of the Cockney. The laws of destiny are independent of those of spelling, says one pithy writer. Whatever may be thought of the mystic notary art, it is unlikely that common numerical divination, more especially when

### AXINOMANCY.

This method was employed for the discovery of hidden treasures and the detection of criminals. In the first case, the method is of a uniquely foolish kind. A round agate must be carefully balanced on the edge of a red-hot hatchet. If the balance is maintained for a certain time, there is no treasure in the neighborhood. If it falls, note the direction in which the ball rolls, and perform the operation three times in total. If the direction taken by the agate is the same in each case, it will not be in vain to dig; should it vary, it would be best to conduct your experiments in another place. The second method would be an insult to the intelligence of the Sandwich Islanders <note>A historical name for the people of the Hawaiian Islands.</note>. To detect a thief or other criminal, the blade of the hatchet must be buried vertically in the earth, with the handle protruding in an upright position. The rest of the performance consists of a circular dance around the hatchet until the soil is churned up and the hatchet falls flat on the ground. The direction taken by the handle will indicate that part of the physical world in which search should be made for the criminal. The operator would do well to remember that he has before him a magical, measureless distance; that he has eternity, if necessary, for the quest, and that as Macdonald, the poet, remarks, “There is plenty of room for meeting in the universe.”

### BELOMANCY.

Under this name, divination with arrows was long practiced during ancient military expeditions. A number of darts were selected, and favorable and unfavorable answers to the given question were written randomly upon them; they were then shuffled like a deck of cards, and one of them was drawn at random. The reply which chance thus provided was considered the voice of destiny and the will of the gods. The operation is supposed to

# DIVINATION.

have been practiced as far back as the time of the Chaldeans. It is a point to be noticed that the more ancient the method, the more gross is the superstition it involves—a fact that may be recommended to the consideration of some over-zealous believers in the wisdom of the distant past. There was another method of <term>Belomantic</term> divination, which consisted of casting certain arrows into the air; the course of the inquirer, whether an individual or an armed host, was directed by the tilt of the weapons.

### BIBLIOMANCY.

<margin>A vertical pencil line appears in the margin alongside this paragraph.</margin>
Occasionally, the forms of divination exceeded the bounds of superstition and passed into the realm of frantic madness. There was a quick method for dealing with sorcerers that was probably the most potent discoverer of <insert>witchcraft that any ingenuity could devise.</insert> A large Bible was placed on one side of a pair of weighing scales. The person suspected of magical practices was set on the opposite side. If he outweighed the Bible, he was innocent; otherwise, he was held guilty. In the days of this mystical weighing and measuring, the scales may truly be said to have fallen from the eyes of a bewitched generation, revealing "sorcery and enchantment everywhere."

Bibliomancy, however, included a more harmless practice, and one of an exceedingly simple character. This was the opening of the Bible with a golden pin and drawing an omen from the first passage that presented itself. Books like the Scriptures, the "Following of Christ," and similar works abound in suggestive and pertinent passages which all men may apply to themselves, and the method was consequently very popular. <insert>St. Augustine denounces the practice in worldly affairs, but declares that he had recourse to it in all cases of spiritual difficulty.</insert> The appeal to chance is, however, essentially superstitious.

### CAPNOMANCY.

This was a method of divination by smoke, and it may be judged that it frequently must have ended in the very thing from which it first originated. The concentration of an impressionable mind upon fantastic wreaths of vapor might, however, produce a hypnotic condition as successfully as any other method. The shapes which imagination gave to the rising fumes were interpreted in an oracular manner, and omens were drawn from the direction in which they drifted away, the latter practice being entirely superstitious. Two methods were followed. In the first, seeds of jasmine or poppy were cast on burning coals, and the form of the fumes was examined; in the other, the smoke was inhaled by the operator and was supposed to bestow a prophetic gift. A powerful effect would undoubtedly be produced by the smoke of strong narcotics, but the visions which originate from poppy heads are not to be counted among the infallible truths of the <term>magian art</term>.

### CEROSCOPY.

Divination with wax has long been replaced by divination using tea leaves and coffee grounds, but it has the advantage of being older than these methods and is further removed from the commonplace. Wax of the purest quality was melted in a brass vase and stirred into a liquid of uniform consistency. It was then poured into another vase filled with cold water, but slowly, and in such a manner that it hardened into tiny discs upon the water's surface. A variety of figures were thus presented to the eye of the seer and interpreted according to the impulse of his intuitive powers. This is an exceedingly harmless practice, which might produce clairvoyant results in a suitable subject.

### CLEIDOMANCY.

The key which shuts and opens is a favorite symbol with all mystics and is founded on a natural analogy; this analogy has a quality that is rare, being perfect in its own way. The suggestiveness of the symbol reacted on the material instrument, and divina-

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Cleidomancy, detailing specific rituals and beliefs associated with using a key and a Bible for divination. <page-num>133</page-num>
->DIVINATION.<-

Divination by means of a key was long practiced in various ways. Each method, it is said, should be practiced when the sun or moon is in Virgo. One method is for discovering the name of a suspected person, or in any case where a name is required. This name should be written on a key; the key should then be tied to a Bible, and both should be suspended from the ring finger of a young virgin, who must repeat in an undertone the words, "*Exurge Domine, adjuva nos et redime nos propter nomen sanctum tuum.*" The practitioner, meanwhile, must repeat the suspected name along with a few sentences of appropriate prayer. If any noticeable oscillation is visible in the book and key, it may be concluded that the name has been correctly divined, and action may be taken accordingly. On the other hand, if they remain stationary, it is certain that the inquiry has followed a wrong path. Occasionally, more elaborate forms of prayer are used, such as litanies and the seven penitential psalms; in such cases, the guilty person was said to develop a miraculous impression of the mystic key upon his body. He was also liable to lose one of his eyes, a possibility which gave rise to the proverb, "*Ex oculo quoque excusso hodie fur cognoscitur.*" The second method was the formal placement of the key upon a specific page of a book. When the Bible was used for this purpose, it was opened at the fiftieth Psalm <note>Even today, divination with the Bible is said to be commonly used among the uneducated classes of the dissenting religious groups.</note>. The volume was then closed with the key inside and tightly secured with a cord—which was, if possible, a woman's garter—but in such a way that the ring of the key stuck out. The book was then suspended from a nail in the wall or from the finger of the inquirer; it was then considered certain that it would spin as completely as the situation allowed at the mention of the name of the person who had committed the deed under investigation. By a third, but less common method, two persons suspended the volume between them, holding the ring of the key with their two forefingers.

Cleidomancy is said to be still performed in Russia

A variety of these entertaining but foolish practices was divination by means of a sieve suspended from a thread, or by shears which were hung from the finger. The choice of instruments might be varied indefinitely, and the use of a consecrated pulley would probably be a useful improvement, which may be recommended to the modern magician. If divinatory rites, however superstitious and grotesque, possess the power ascribed to them in the remote ages of faith, we may confidently look forward to the time when they will be used in our Courts of Justice, when the godless skill of the common detective has perished with the reign of reason, and the unbounded resources of magic will enable an enlightened humanity to prove and disprove anything. Then may the enemies of the initiates look forward to an evil time. Sacred and beautiful science of the Magi, the last word for humanity, the first and final revelation, the harmony of the whole universe, the universal synthesis, the science of Barrett and of Solomon,

> “ The day comes, your day,
> In which all things bloom again! ”

### DACTYLOMANCY.

Who is not aware of the virtues of the ring in magic? Equally powerful for binding and loosening, it is more important than the ring used in weddings. Truly, says Chispa, there is more to marriage than the wedding ring, but there is nothing that is greater, worthier, or more wondrous than the great, worthy, wondrous, and altogether mystical ring of the occult art. The ring of Fastrada, the ring of Gyges, the ring of Solomon—they are better than the bells of Fairyland, better than the cap of Fortunatus. The chain of Mesmer was a magnetic ring; the séance circle is a living, throbbing, universal ring of humanity; the magic circle is truly a ring also. You who would unearth treasures, secure the magic ring!
