# DIVINATION.

<meta>The text contains hand-drawn red ink underlining beneath specific phrases identifying planetary rings.</meta>

You who would be adepts of the “divine science,” learn its many-sided mysteries! You who would fascinate a woman, wear it invisibly on your person, and you shall invisibly enter into the <term>penetralia</term> <note>the innermost parts or secrets</note> of a woman’s affection; be sure she will then receive you as an unseen god! You who would divine truly, divine only by the ring! Create it under a favorable constellation, set it with mystic characters, and suspend it by a thread or a hair over a glass; it will oscillate with the speed of a pendulum, and once for YES and twice for NO—just as you choose to decide—it will respond to your questions with delightful, musical vibrations echoing against the sides of the glass. Inscribe the letters of the alphabet on the edge of a circular table, and the intelligent instrument, passing over certain letters, will compose a definite answer for you and reveal the mysteries you seek with the precision of the spirit-rapper’s table. It is best to perform the operation with religious observances: the diviner should be clothed in white linen with his head shaven; he should carry a branch of <term>vervain</term> in his hand—a beneficial plant provided by Nature to drive away evil spirits—and the ring should be solemnly consecrated with appeals and vows.

The power is greatly increased by properly aligning the rings under the influence of the planetary spirits. The <note>Underlined in red ink</note>ring of Saturn should be made of lead, set with onyx or garnet, which should be engraved with a stone encircled by a serpent. The <note>Underlined in red ink</note>ring of Jupiter should be made of brass, set with a topaz, sapphire, or amethyst, and engraved with the image of an eagle holding a pentagram in its beak. The <note>Underlined in red ink</note>ring of Mars should be made of iron, set with ruby, red jasper, or hematite, and engraved with the symbol of a serpent devouring the point of a sword. The <note>Underlined in red ink</note>ring of the Sun should be made of gold, set with <term>hyacinthus</term> <note>a precious stone, likely zircon or sapphire</note> or chrysolite, and engraved with a crowned and lion-headed serpent. The <note>Underlined in red ink</note>ring of Venus should be made of copper, set with an emerald, and engraved with the Indian <term>lingam</term>. The <note>Underlined in red ink</note>ring of Mercury should be composed of an alloy of brass, lead, and fixed quicksilver, set with cornelian or <term>alectorine</term> <note>a stone traditionally said to be found in a rooster's gizzard</note>, and engraved with the symbol of the <term>caduceus</term>, which is a scepter interlaced

by two serpents. The ring of the Moon should be of silver, set with crystal or moonstone, and engraved with the image of a sphere intersected by two crescents.

The use of constellated rings involves a more elaborate ceremony. The day and time of the constellation regulate the use of the rings. Should it take place on a Tuesday, the ring of Mars should be selected, and the inquiry conducted during the first, eighth, fifteenth, or twenty-second hours, which are consecrated to the Genius <note>a presiding spirit or tutelary deity</note> of that planet. The circular table already mentioned must be engraved with the signs of the Zodiac and of the seven planets. A number of discs made of the metal appropriate to the period, and bearing the symbols of three alphabets, should be thrown randomly on the table; a prayer should be addressed to the Genius, the ring should be suspended by a thread above the table, and the priest, or the pythoness <note>a female seer or priestess</note>, should set fire to the thread with a torch consecrated to Hecate, the goddess of enchantment. The ring will then fall upon the table, and the letters over which it rolls, and those by which it pauses, must be carefully noted. This operation should be repeated seven times, and then the intuition of the diviner must gather the meaning of the oracles from the collection of letters obtained. Now, the faculty of intuition, which is possessed to an extraordinary degree by people of certain temperaments, can undoubtedly be as successfully exercised on a random heap of letters as on the grounds of coffee, and herein lies the extent of the value inherent in divination with rings.

### GASTROMANCY.

This is a method of divination which induces clairvoyance in suitable subjects and might lead to very interesting results. Several circular glass vessels filled with clear water are placed between a number of lighted candles. The intelligences of the spiritual world are invoked in an undertone, and a virgin boy or girl is then introduced and directed to concentrate their gaze on the surface of the vessels, at which point the answer of the oracle becomes visible to their magnetized faculties in fantastic figures produced by the refraction of light within the vessels.

There is another <term>gastromantic</term> method that corresponds more closely with the meaning of the term—divination from the belly. This was either the practice of <term>ventriloquism</term> or the result of some form of possession.

### HYDROMANCY.

The art of <term>divination</term> by water is simply a branch of <term>clairvoyance</term> that has been successfully practiced in all ages and nations, although, as the demonologist Delrio truly remarks, *nulla fœcundior imposturis* <note>Latin: "none more fertile in deceptions"</note>. Several varieties are distinguished by authorities on this interesting subject:

1. When, at the conclusion of invocations and other magical ceremonies, the names of people or other required information are seen written upside down in the water. 2. When a ring is suspended by a thread above a glass of water, and a vision begins after the sides of the vessel have been struck a certain number of times during its swinging motion. 3. When three small pebbles are thrown one after another at short intervals into clear, still water, and the circles that form on the surface—and the ways they intersect—are interpreted as <term>omens</term>. 4. When the countless movements and disturbances of the sea waves are carefully examined and clairvoyant conclusions are drawn. 5. When the color of the water and the figures seen within it are interpreted. The traditions of the ancients attributed special qualities for this form of <term>hydromancy</term> to certain streams and springs. 7. When water was poured into a glass or crystal basin and a little oil was added, which was supposed to increase the seer's visionary power. 8. When the twists, turns, and sounds made by rivers and torrents as they plunged into ravines or churned in whirlpools were interpreted as <term>fatidic</term> <note>prophetic or oracular</note> utterances. This method was very popular among the early Germanic peoples. There are also some minor types of hydromantic divination that are more clearly superstitious. One, which is native to Italy, was used to discover thefts.

The names of suspected people were written upon pebbles and thrown into water. The name of the guilty person remained indelible. Another method involved filling a glass or cup with water and pronouncing mystic words over it. On certain occasions, the water was supposed to bubble and pour over the brim, though we are unable to state under what specific circumstances. Finally, the ancient Germans are said to have used a barbaric and foolish perversion of the art of <term>hydromancy</term>. When they doubted the faithfulness of their wives and wished to determine if a newborn child was legitimately or otherwise conceived, they threw it into the water. If the infant floated, the mother was considered innocent; if it sank, the child was deemed illegitimate.

“Hydromancy,” says Elihu Rich, “is, in principle, the same thing as <term>divination</term> by the crystal or mirror, and in ancient times, a natural rock basin kept constantly full by a running stream was a favorite medium. The double meaning of the word *reflection* should be considered here, and how, gazing down into clear water, the mind is inclined toward self-withdrawal and contemplation, deeply tinged with melancholy. Rocky pools and gloomy lakes appear in all stories of <term>witchcraft</term>—witness the Craic-pol-nain in the Highland woods of Laynchork; the Devil’s Glen in County Wicklow, Ireland; the Swedish Blokula; the witch mountains of Italy; and the Babiagora, between Hungary and Poland. Similar locations in the glens of Germany were marked, as Tacitus mentions, by salt springs; there was an additional good reason for this, which would take us too far from the present subject to explain.

“It was actually only another form of <term>divination</term> by the dark water pool that attracted so much public attention... when Mr. Lane, in his work on ‘Modern Egypt,’ testified to its success as practiced in Egypt and Hindostan. Having resolved to witness the performance of this species of <term>sorcery</term>, that gentleman watched as the magician began his operations by writing forms of invocation to his familiar spirits on six slips of paper; a chafing dish containing live charcoal was then procured, and a boy was summoned who had not yet reached the age of

puberty. Mr. Lane inquired who were the persons that could see in the fluid mirror, and was told that they were a boy not yet arrived at puberty, a virgin, a Black female slave, and a pregnant woman. To prevent any collusion between the sorcerer and the boy, Mr. Lane sent his servant to bring the first boy he met. When all was prepared, the sorcerer threw some incense and one of the strips of paper into the chafing-dish; he then took hold of the boy’s right hand and drew a square with some mystical marks on the palm. In the center of the square, he poured a little ink, which formed the magic mirror, and desired the boy to look steadily into it without raising his head. In this mirror, the boy declared that he saw, successively, a man sweeping, seven men with flags, an army pitching its tents, and the various officers of state attending to the Sultan. The rest must be told by Mr. Lane himself.

> “The sorcerer now addressed himself to me and asked me if I wished the boy to see any person who was absent or dead. I named Lord Nelson, of whom the boy had evidently never heard; for it was with much difficulty that he pronounced the name after several trials. The magician desired the boy to say to the Sultan, ‘My master salutes you, and desires you to bring Lord Nelson; bring him before my eyes that I may see him speedily.’ The boy then said so, and almost immediately added, ‘A messenger has gone and brought back a man dressed in a black (or rather, a dark blue) suit of European clothes; the man has lost his left arm.’ He then paused for a moment or two, and looking more intently and closely into the ink, said: ‘No, he has not lost his left arm, but it is placed to his breast.’ This correction made his distinction more striking than it had been without it; since Lord Nelson generally had his empty sleeve attached to the breast of his coat; but it was the right arm that he had lost. Without saying that I suspected the boy had made a mistake, I asked the magician whether the objects appeared in the ink as if actually before the eyes, or as if in a glass, which makes the right appear left. He answered that they appeared as in a mirror. This rendered the boy’s description faultless.”

> Though completely puzzled, I was somewhat disappointed with the magician’s performances, for they fell short of what he had accomplished, in many instances, in the presence of certain of my friends and countrymen. On one of these occasions, an Englishman present ridiculed the performance and said that nothing would satisfy him but a correct description of the appearance of his own father, of whom he was sure no one in the company had any knowledge. The boy, accordingly, after calling by name for the person mentioned, described a man in a <term>Frank dress</term>, with his hand placed on his head, wearing spectacles, and with one foot on the ground and the other raised behind him, as if he were stepping down from a seat. The description was exactly true in every respect; the peculiar position of the hand was caused by a nearly constant headache, and that of the foot or leg by a stiff knee, caused by a fall from a horse while hunting. On another occasion, Shakespeare was described with the most minute exactness, both as to his person and dress; and I might add several other cases in which the same magician has excited astonishment in the sober minds of several Englishmen of my acquaintance.”
>
> “It may be worth adding,” says Elihu Rich, “that in a case of <term>hydromancy</term> known to the writer, the boy could see better without the medium than with it, though he could also see reflected images in a vessel of water. This fact may be admitted to prove that such images are reflected to the eye of the seer from his own mind and brain; how the brain becomes enchanted in this way, or the eye disposed for vision, is another question; certainly there is no proof that the remembered image in the mind of the inquirer is transferred to the seer, as evidence can be shown to the contrary.”

supreme gods. The questioning of a magnet that had been washed in spring water was supposed to evoke an understandable response from that substance in the faint tones of a small child. The amethyst also possesses lithomantic properties; the events in the future are supposed to be revealed in dreams to those who are in the habit of wearing this stone on their person. A mystic variety of the *ophite stone* is mentioned in the so-called Orphic hymns under the name of "the true and vocal *sideritis*." Its appearance is rough and hard; it is heavy and black in color, and it is entirely twisted with wrinkled veins. It was given by Apollo to *Helenus*, who, for the space of ten days, abstained from the marriage bed, from the bath, and from animal food. "Then, washing this intelligent stone in a living fountain, he cherished it like a babe in soft clothing; and having honored it as a god, he at length gave it breath through his hymn of mighty virtue. Having lighted lamps in his own purified house, he fondled the divine stone in his hands, carrying it about as a mother carries her infant; and you," says the oracular writer, "if you would hear the voices of the gods, should likewise provoke a similar miracle; for when you have diligently wiped and cradled the stone in your arms, it will suddenly utter the cry of a newborn child seeking milk from its nurse's breast. Beware, however, of fear; for if you drop the stone upon the ground, you will provoke the anger of the immortals. Ask boldly of future things, and it will reply. Place it near your eyes after it has been washed, look steadily at it, and you will perceive it divinely breathing. This was how *Helenus*, trusting in this fearful stone, learned that his country would be overthrown by the *Atridae*."

The possession of a similar treasure is attributed to a physician named *Eusebius*, who obtained it through an impressive intervention of celestial phenomena. "One night, motivated by an unaccountable impulse, he wandered out from the city of *Emesa* to the summit of a mountain honored by a temple of *Minerva*. There, as he sat down, fatigued by his walk, he saw a globe of fire falling from the sky and a lion standing by it. The

The lion disappeared, the fire was extinguished, and Eusebius ran and picked up a *baetulum*. He asked it which god it belonged to, and it readily answered, to Gennæus, a deity worshiped by the Heliopolitans under the form of a lion in the temple of Jupiter. During this night, Eusebius said he traveled no less than 210 stadia (more than 26 miles). He never became perfectly master of the *baetulum*, but was obliged very humbly to request its responses. It was of a handsome, globular shape, white, and a palm in diameter, though sometimes it appeared larger and sometimes smaller; occasionally, also, it was of a purple color. Characters could be read on it, impressed in the color called *tingaribinus*. Its answer seemed as if proceeding from a shrill pipe, and Eusebius himself interpreted the sounds. While some authorities consider the animating spirit of this mysterious stone to be divine, others attribute it to an elementary influence. It is said to have been frequently found on Mount Lebanon, and a parallel has been established between it and the black stone of Elagabalus, as well as the monument anointed by Jacob at Bethel.

## PYROMANCY.

The purest, most powerful, and most exalted elementary intelligences were those who inhabited the fire; sacred and consecrated flame was the most perfect method of mystic purgation; a bright and clear fire was no less effective in driving off evil spirits than in scaring wild beasts from a hunter's camp at night. Lights have been used in all religious ceremonies from time immemorial; it would be strange, indeed, if they were wholly unconnected with the mysteries of the divinatory art. Pyromancy, however, is comparatively obscure, just as it is also essentially superstitious. A few handfuls of powdered resin were cast into a fire; if a bright flame leaped up, a good omen was drawn from it; a slow and smoky combustion predicted misfortune. When a victim was burned, the future was occasionally predicted by observing the color of the flame. Occasionally, also, a sick person would be set in front of a large fire; if the shadow cast by the body was straight

and at right angles to the furnace, his speedy cure might be confidently expected; if it fell upon the ground at an oblique angle, it was a certain sign of death. The observation of lighted torches was also practiced in antiquity. Three torches were arranged in a triangle, all of them being made of fine wax. The flickering of the flame from right to left foretold approaching migrations; the maneuvers of secret enemies were signified by a spiral curl; a fitful rising and falling presaged dangerous changes of fortune. A dominant brilliance in a single torch was the sign of unforeseen fortune; sparkling and crackling were considered a warning to be prudent and a threat of setbacks and treachery. The formation of an extremely brilliant point at the end of a wick announced permanently increasing success; the sudden extinction of one or more of the torches was considered especially disastrous for the subjects of the consultation as well as for the inquirer himself.

### MYOMANCY.

<margin>74</margin>
When Halton II, nicknamed Bonose, the usurper of the archbishopric of Mayence, refused around the year 1074 to give food to the poor during a famine, and persisted so far in his sin that he is credited with burning a house full of insistent bread-seekers, he is said to have been visited by a severe judgment from heaven. He fell sick in a castle situated on a small island in the Rhine, where he was devoured by a multitude of rats. A similar fate is traditionally recorded of a Polish king. Besides being the ministers of divine retribution, common domestic vermin are otherwise of a certain importance in supernatural history. It is a good omen to encounter an albino rat, just as it is also a sign of destruction when these intelligent animals completely desert a ship. Mice, on the other hand, are of evil omen, and the harmony of suspicious circumstances during the consultation of an oracle would be permanently disrupted by their cry. Divination by rats and mice apparently originated from these superstitions, but the actual nature of the practice has not been preserved.

### ONOMANCY.

The evolution of Shakespearian criticism has been developed to such a degree that his writings are sometimes regarded by people of sufficiently advanced vision as a complete course of initiation into the mysteries of occult philosophy and into the general history of the soul. This is one of those profound hypotheses which, in a well-regulated world, would of course have been true, but it is an extravagance within the limits of three-dimensional space. A true Kabbalistic Magus would never have written that proverbial question, “What’s in a name?” <margin>(</margin> It is a doctrine inherent in all Kabbalistic Magic that nothing happens by chance, that the name which is inherited by a child is inherited by virtue of eternal law, and that even his given name, though apparently arbitrary and depending on parental taste and whim or other considerations, is not a product of chance but must also be attributed to the workings of a secret principle. From there, it is a step to affirm that the name is in itself the expression of a future destiny, or that the events of the future can be discerned in the letters of a name; thus arises Onomancy, which is the art of divination by names and was in high favor throughout the pagan world. The followers of the Pythagorean philosophy used to maintain that the talents, activities, and good fortune of men were in conformity with their destiny, their genius, and their name. The esoteric significance of the name Hippolytus proclaimed that its bearer would be torn to pieces by horses; it was equally predetermined that Agamemnon would for years be encamped outside the walls of Troy, and that Priam would be ransomed from slavery. It does not matter that there was more than one Hippolytus and that they were by no means identical in their destinies. The family name also had its influence, and a second given name would also act upon the life of its bearer. Now, the science of onomantic divination had two leading rules. An even number of vowels in a man’s name signified something wrong on the left side; an uneven number indicated a similar imperfection on the right.

The causation, of course, is not clear; but God is all-knowing. It was no less emphatically certain that a name with more letters would give an advantage over a rival whose ancestors had less copiously supplied him with alphabetic resources. It was not by sword or shield that Achilles triumphed over Hector; it was by the superior length of his name. The misfortunes of modern warfare and competition of every kind may be definitely attributed to the neglect of this ancient wisdom. Happy is the modern bookmaker who comes across this volume, metaphorically or otherwise, and is compelled by his genius to glance at this page. A permanent fortune will be secured for him through the possession of these mystic tips. It will be no less suitable for ladies choosing between two suitors, as the only sure guide to fortune in the lottery of the marriage market. Old times have changed, old manners are gone, but there is no reason to suppose that the power has departed from a divination that was successfully practiced in the past. What is more excellent than the authority of Cælius Rhodigunis? <note>Likely referring to the 15th-century author Lodovicus Caelius Rhodiginus.</note> Has he not informed us that Theodotus the Goth practiced a peculiar and original Onomancy on the recommendation of a Jew, who was doubtless a king-initiate? “The diviner advised the Prince, who was on the eve of a war with Rome, to shut up thirty hogs in three different sties, having previously given some of them Roman and others Gothic names. On an appointed day, when the sties were opened, all the Romans were found alive, but with half their bristles fallen off; on the other hand, all the Goths were dead. From this omen, the onomantist predicted that the Gothic army would be utterly destroyed by the Romans, who would, at the same time, lose half their own force.”

It appears on the authority of the French archaeologist, Count de Gebelin, that this method of divination was found capable of unheard-of developments by the “divine” Cagliostro, who foretold to Masonic *illuminés* in solemn conclave the most important events of the French Revolution by the names, birthplaces, and other natal circumstances of the chief participants therein. ### ONYCHOMANCY.

Divination by the fingernails would appear to be a trivial invention, were it not for the fact that for some unexplained reason it has been dignified with the title "the observation of the angel Uriel." "Upon the nails of the right hand of a pure boy or a young virgin, or upon the palm of the hand, some olive oil is placed—or, better yet, walnut oil mixed with tallow or blacking." Tallow was occasionally the only ingredient, and wax was sometimes used as a substitute. Once the nails had been anointed in this way, they were turned toward the sun and studied intently by the seer. The goals of the inquiry determined the time of the observation. The search for money or items buried in the earth had to be conducted while facing East; it was thus a morning activity, as the full blaze of sunlight had to fall directly upon the medium's prepared nails. The occult investigation of crimes and consultations prompted by love were conducted while facing South (which also served for investigating murder), while facing West was for robbery. It was required that the child repeat the seventy-two verses of the Psalms which the Hebrew <term>Kabbalists</term> collected for the <term>Urim</term> and <term>Thummim</term>, and which are preserved in a treatise titled *De Verbo Mirifico* and in Reuchlin’s Rabbinical collection. "In each of these verses, the venerable four-letter name <note>The Tetragrammaton, YHWH.</note> appears, along with the three-letter names of the seventy-two angels; these are related to the mystical name <term>Schema-hamphores</term>, which was hidden in the folds of the lining of the high priest's vestment." The hypnotic state can be produced in such a variety of ways that even the anointed nail should not be strictly excluded from the category of possible instruments. There is, however, a sense of eternal appropriateness that forbids an anticlimax in either action or speech, and this has definitely relegated such methods to the limbo of absurdity.

## OOMANCY.

The domestic egg is saved from being completely ordinary by its symbolic value, its suggestion of hidden possibilities, and its representation of a world in miniature. Divination by eggs may therefore be allowed to pass, if only because of its unique charm. The ancients used to discern in the outer shape and in the inner structure of the egg the most impenetrable secrets of the future—an art that is supposed to have originated with the divine Orpheus. If a pregnant woman is anxious to find out the sex of her future child, she must carry an egg in her bosom at the proper temperature. When, under the influence of her natural warmth, a chick is finally hatched, its sex will determine whether the infant will be male or female. Divination, however, has recently been more commonly practiced with the whites of eggs, and this method was favored by the famous Mademoiselle Lenormand.

> “Take a glass of water, break a fresh egg slowly into it, and from the shapes assumed by the white, let the predictions of the future be gathered by the intuitive skill of the seer.”

This is by no means a modern method; it is referred to in the following terms by a *Grimoire* of some antiquity.

> “The ritual of the egg is to determine what will happen to a person who is then present in the company. An egg from a young black hen must be broken and the germ extracted from it. A large glass, very clear and clean, must stand ready, being filled with pure water. The germ must be placed in it, and afterward the vessel must be exposed to the midday sun of summer, with many prayers and incantations, the water also being stirred vigorously with the finger to move the said germ. When this has been done enough, it is allowed to sit still for a moment and is gazed at without being touched. Something relating to the seeker and the object of the divinatory inquiry will then be visible in the water.”

In addition to the methods of divination that we have listed above, there are many of a minor character, and many which are limited to certain locations and

...more properly belong to folklore. A brief reference to a few of the former class will complete this exhaustive notice.

**GYROMANCY** was a kind of divination practiced by walking around <note>perambulating</note> a circle inscribed with the alphabet. When fatigue was followed by dizziness and dizziness by utter collapse—when the operator dropped to the ground—the letter upon which he fell was the first in the response of this oracle. If, after an appropriate pause, he was sufficiently recovered to begin again, he might obtain a second letter in the same manner, and so on until he produced an intelligible sentence, or until death or madness intervened. This is an interesting instance of mystical practice which may be recommended to modern agility in advanced <term>thaumaturgics</term> and to the distant followers of the Eastern howling dervish. **HIPPOMANCY** was a Celtic divination by the neighing of white horses, set apart for the purpose and fed at the public expense in consecrated forests where the trees were their only shelter. They were made to walk behind a sacred chariot; the priest and king followed and observed their every movement, drawing omens from them with an absolute and unfailing confidence founded on the conviction that the animals participated in the great <term>arcanum</term> of the gods. The Saxons also drew omens from the movements of a sacred horse, fed and housed in a temple, from which it was led before war was declared against an enemy. If it stepped forward with its right hoof first, that was a favorable omen; in the other case, they abandoned their enterprise. **ICHTHYOMANCY**, like all divinations of incredible coarseness, is of ancient origin and may be recommended to the Wisdom Religion and the apologists of a plundered <note>spoliated</note> past. It was performed by inspecting the entrails of fish. An Apollonian fountain of prophetic fish was preserved at Myra <note>Mirea</note> and was consulted by the great Apuleius, who was initiated by Ceres into the many-layered mysteries of the rose.

**KEPHALONOMANCY** is an interesting practice involving various ceremonies with the boiled head of a donkey.

The donkey is a sacred, prophetic, divining, mystical, and essentially divine animal. Because of its long ears <note>auricular elongations</note>, it is related to Baphomet and to universal

[Kephalono]mancy. This is a very interesting, harmless, and respectable practice which Delrio attributes to the medieval Jews. However, it seems to have been practiced by the ancients, who placed the head upon live coals, recited superstitious prayers, pronounced the names of suspected persons about whom they desired information, and listened for a cracking and convulsive movement of the jaws of the decapitated beast. The name which was pronounced at this crucial moment by the intelligent operator was that of the guilty person.

**LAMPODOMANCY** was a form of divination by observing the shape, color, and various movements assumed by the flame of a lamp.

The art of divination by pearls was named **MARGARITOMANCY**. The precious stone was placed by a fire and covered with a glass vessel. The inquiry was conducted to recover stolen goods; it involved repeating the names of suspected persons in a loud voice. When the name of the guilty party was pronounced by the speaker, the pearl was supposed to leap up to the top of the glass, which it occasionally shattered with its force. This is another entertaining and simple device which can easily be tested by the owner of the stone in question; in the event of its success, it would be a good instance of divining power and the production of wonderful effects without apparent causes.

By means of a simple ceremony, **PARTHENOMANCY** guaranteed to determine the presence or absence of virginity. The circumference of the girl’s neck was measured with a thread, and on the repetition of this operation after a reasonable period, an unfavorable conclusion was drawn from an increase in its size.

**SCIAMANCY** was performed by summoning the shades of the dead. It differed from <term>necromancy</term> and <term>psychomancy</term> as it was neither the soul nor the body of the deceased person which responded to the appeal of the <term>Magus</term>, but a kind of mysterious likeness, most probably an <term>astral shell</term>.

**SPODANOMANCY** has a definite advantage over most forms of divination as the devil is occasionally supposed to interfere personally on its behalf. “It is performed,” says Grand Orient, “by scattering ashes thickly in some place exposed to the air, and writing therein with the tip of the finger any question...

We have treated the methods of divination at such length that it will be almost unnecessary to refer to sources where more extensive information can be found. *Les Devins, ou Commentaire des Principales Sortes de Devinations* (The Diviners, or Commentary on the Main Types of Divination) by Caspar Peucer, dated 1584, is a quarto of nearly 700 pages and may be considered to cover the subject exhaustively. Works in the English language are few and unimportant.

-># THE DIVINING ROD.<-

Although essentially a method of divination—and naturally, as such, to be included in the section bearing that name—the interest that has for so long been attracted to the magnetic wand, its extraordinary history, and the very large number of witnesses who have testified to its genuine power, require it to be treated separately, as with the magic crystal.

Our readers have already become familiar with the mystical properties of the rod in necromantic and black magic. However, the ordinary divining rod—which is known by reputation to everyone and is still used in remote country places—is an instrument of natural magic rather than pneumatic art. This is essentially the same as saying that if its strange properties are indeed established facts, they are unobserved phenomena of ordinary science and belong, like the lodestone, to the field of magnetism. They have, however, been explained in various ways, for the subject has been treated seriously in large volumes and has generated its own literature; sometimes the mystery was solved with the help of a "corpuscular hypothesis," and sometimes by subsurface electricity. But the agency of the devil was, of course, the primary explanation, as the omnipresent court of hell was the dominant reality of that age.

The divining rod was and is a forked branch of wild hazel, beech, or apple tree. It works best if it is a shoot of one year’s growth; and, on the whole, to minimize variations in results under seemingly identical conditions, it is safer to cut the rod with some solemnity. The instructions given in the "Great Grimoire" and the "Red Dragon" are similar to those detailed for the *verendum* <note>a ceremonial rod or wand</note> of black magic. The wild hazel is recommended, the branch to

It also specifies that the rod should ideally be a year-old forked branch of hazel, beech, or apple wood cut with ritual solemnity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for harvesting and consecrating the divining rod, transitioning into its practical application and historical shift from general divination to water dowsing.</meta>

be grasped in the left hand and cut with three blows of a knife held in the opposite hand. This must be performed at the moment of sunrise, and the following recitation should be made:

> “I gather you in the name of Eloim, Mutrathon, Adonay, and Semiphoras, so that by their power you may possess the virtues of the rod of Moses and Jacob for the discovery of all that I desire to know.”

The experiments should always be accompanied by a short invocation, with the rod being held lightly in both hands by the two ends which form the fork:

> “I command you in the name of Eloim, Mutrathon, Adonay, and Semiphoras to reveal to me—”

—here name the special object of your experiment.

As a rule, the rod is used independently of magical ceremonies, and independently of prayers or formulas, as a natural curiosity which produces remarkable practical results. In this case, the power of turning it is a gift which, like mediumship, belongs to exceptional persons, and seemingly to them alone. The operation is extremely simple; it consists of holding one of the upper ends of the rod in each hand and walking leisurely over the ground. When you approach the object of your search, the rod turns by itself in the hand, and the indication is considered to be infallible.

The instrument, as will be seen later on, was originally used for the purposes of direct divination—for obtaining an omen or presage, for discovering a thief, or for unearthing buried treasure. In Europe, and especially in England, these applications of its powers became obsolete upon the discovery that, as numerous as they may have been in many directions (not even excepting alchemy), the divining rod had a wider range of sympathy with the ancient element of water than with any other material substance, or with any human culprit, whether robber or witch; henceforth, it was generally utilized for the indication of subterranean springs. The testimony already referred to relates exclusively to this aspect of its occult virtues.

The discovery in question is attributed to Jacques Aymar, who gained notoriety at Lyons in 1672 by the performance of a variety of wonders which included

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the divining rod's applications, transitioning from its primary use in locating water to its historical role in criminal investigation and the controversy surrounding its most famous practitioner.</meta>

# THE DIVINING ROD.

the detection of assassins, of sorcery, of thieves, of underground wells, and of obliterated boundaries. It is curious to read in a book of the seventeenth century that the sensitive person who mastered the rod was always subject to the same kinds of facial contortions, violent nervous quivering, and a pulse elevated to fever point, which are characteristic of thought-readers and mediums today. It is also notable that, since murders were common in those days of relative violence, whenever the psychological bloodhound was led by his rod or otherwise to the scene of a murder, he always experienced a painful sensation in the heart, accompanied by oppression and occasionally by sickness.

Before the fame of Aymar, this instrument of divination, as distinct from its use in the practice of magical evocation, lacked any special fame. His wonders brought him to the attention of the Prince de Condé, but his actions when he was under the patronage of this nobleman in Paris were of a questionable nature, and it is alleged that he admitted to being an impostor—that the rod had no power of its own, and that he had resorted to the practice of divining for the reasons that most commonly influence a needy rogue in his tricks. Despite these highly probable admissions, the fame of the divining rod grew, and its practitioners multiplied. Boys and women became proficient in this as a form of detective skill. Ponderous books written in its praise, bolstered into folios by large collections of true and circumstantial accounts; books exposing the shallow and fraudulent pretense; books that explained it through purely natural considerations; books that insisted on its miraculous character; books that blamed the devil—an entire literature arose. A pursuit that had originated in folklore became fashionable for a time—for a century—and then it returned to where it began: to the peasantry who created folklore, and among whom it has been a living reality.

The divining rod, upon an impartial survey of its history, seems to have been used—as is naturally most probable—in both good and bad faith, and in each case

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the divining rod, shifting from historical accounts of fraud to a psychological explanation involving animal magnetism and clairvoyance.</meta>

# THE OCCULT SCIENCES.

Much the same results were obtained regardless, for trickery is strictly imitative, and in the production of fraudulent effects, it is seldom designed to surpass the original and genuine model. Completely apart from mysticism, and independent of magic and mediumship, the divining rod can be explained by facts in animal magnetism which are now generally accepted. Psychometry—which is a part of clairvoyance just as clairvoyance is a higher form of mesmerism—has many authenticated results and offers some similarities to the wonders of the filbert wand. What the disk is to the eye in hypnotism, the rod was to the hand in rhabdomancy. A mineral applied to the forehead of a sensitive person awakens an instinct, or an inner sense of sight, or some other potential faculty into temporary life, and the subject *sees—sees*, it is claimed, the past history of the object almost from the dawn of evolution. Now, there seems to be a connection between the physical eye and the eye of the mind, but the activity of the mind’s eye can sometimes be developed by sensations from the sense of touch. The discoveries attributed to the divining rod are often a form of clairvoyance in which the operator, at the right moment, sees what he is required to discover; the instrument is merely a pretext for the development of internal vision, acting much like a crystal does for the eye, and producing some hypnotic or abnormal condition. Although one type of wood may be said to be better than another, the rod itself can possess no hidden power. The effect, whatever it may be, acts possibly through the rod, but it is the individual who is affected, as is evident from his nervous excitement.

Experiments with the divining wand undoubtedly have an element of interest and, in one sense, a practical utility, but they belong to the curiosities of the secret sciences and have no value from the standpoint of true mysticism. An earnest seeker who, in the language of mystical allegory, is in search of *Diana Unveiled* and the *Way of the Light*, should abstain from the trivialities of the practice; he is seeking his soul; let him leave the pursuit of minor marvels to those who are easily astonished.

<summary>The author argues that the divining rod's effects are not due to the wood itself but are a form of clairvoyance or animal magnetism triggered by the operator's heightened sensitivity. Ultimately, such practices are dismissed as minor curiosities that distract from the true spiritual goals of mysticism.</summary>

The element of interest to which we have just referred—distinguished for the moment from its practical utility—is the fact that the search for water is still pursued using this old method of magic. It is one of the few survivals of the antiquated faith in the "sympathy and antipathy" of things. Although the citation of specific cases is generally beyond the scope of the present work, the two following testimonies to the persistence and success of this divinatory method at the dawn of the twentieth century will show that even in the byways of mystic art, there is occasionally a real and recoverable power and truth. The accounts in each case are drawn from the newspapers that commemorated the occurrence within a short period of its date.

"The question as to the magical or the 'scientific' value of the 'divining rod,'" says the *Manchester Examiner* of February 17, 1886, "has just been reopened by the success which has attended its use at the Flelton <note>Likely a misspelling of Fletton, a district in Peterborough.</note> Wagon Works of the Midland Railway Company regarding the discovery of a permanent supply of water. The required daily volume of water was about five hundred or six hundred gallons, and the well on the premises only yielded half that quantity. It was necessary, therefore, to supplement the supply either by sinking other wells or by constructing an expensive system of piping from Peterborough. The former plan was preferred, and two new wells were sunk to no purpose. The services of a gentleman of the district, who bore the reputation of being skilled in the art of discovering water by means of the 'divining rod,' were then called in. This wizard or expert employed for his purpose a forked hazel twig, holding one prong of the fork in each hand, with the point directed toward the sky. After walking about the premises for some time, the point of the fork suddenly began to bend down—purely, as the best evidence suggests, of its own accord—and to turn toward the earth. The wielder of the wand declared that a plentiful supply of water would be found in the spot indicated. The same phenomenon was

It should be added to these accounts that modern Spiritualism has discovered a new application for the divining rod, which is used as a means of communication by invisible intelligences. It is placed in the hands of the medium, or of a participant at a séance, and is held over the letters of the alphabet arranged in a circle or line. After preliminary swayings, it is said to indicate various letters with considerable speed, leaping from point to point, and by this simple process, the wisdom of the world beyond is conveyed to those questioning the wand in intelligible words and sentences.

While in magic the rod was used chiefly either for the control of visible spirits—achieved through the power of specific words and ceremonies that formulated the magician's will—or for the discovery of hidden springs of water, it should not be forgotten that it was also used for the purposes of direct divination. As far back as the time of Hosea, we know that the people sought counsel from their wooden idols, and that their staffs gave them answers. In the days of Greek augury, it was the custom to set up two staffs, over which mystic verses and incantations were whispered in an undertone, until the staffs, influenced by the <term>daimons</term>, were suddenly cast to the ground. The direction of the fall determined the nature of the prophecy. The rods of the Hebrews were peeled on one side, and an omen was drawn from the manner

...in which they fell. In the days of the <term>witch fever</term>, it was devoted to a more serious and, at the same time, more abhorrent purpose. It had long been used to discover fugitive thieves; it was supposed to have the power to detect secret spell-makers and casters of the <term>evil eye</term>. Then it developed a more unerring and terrifying power, for it was claimed that the V-shaped rod obtained from a shrub now called the <term>witch-hazel</term> would infallibly turn in the direction where a witch was to be found. Believing in the truth of the adage that "a thief should be set to catch a thief" if you wish to track him, they used what the ignorance of the time would have called <term>witchcraft</term> to expose the witches. On being led into the presence of a group of women, the professional <term>witch-hunter</term> would balance his rod; the direction in which its forked portion happened to turn—or was secretly made to turn—indicated the guilty person, who was persecuted or perished accordingly.

The actual nature of the connection between the rod and the water it indicates has not been made clear by the many writers who have devoted their attention to the subject and shared the results of their research. Whether it is impossible to use it to find precious stones and precious metals has not been discussed by theorists, nor has it been attempted in experiment. But though the mystic rod may be influenced by nothing denser than water, and though the attention of the <term>alchemists</term> was concentrated on the creation of gold and silver so exclusively that they had no leisure to develop a process for discovering them in their natural form in mines, the byways of <term>magical art</term> have not abandoned the needy seeker. This account may be appropriately concluded by providing him with an exceedingly curious method for the detection of concealed treasures, which is related to the subject now under notice because it involves the use of a <term>vervain rod</term>, or branch.

### ASTROLOGY.

One of the most ingenious and plausible defenses of the science of astrology has been made by Eliphas Lévi, in a passage brief enough to be quoted.

> “Nothing is indifferent in Nature; a pebble more or less upon a road may crush or profoundly alter the fortunes of the greatest men and even of the greatest empires; much more, then, the position of a particular star cannot be indifferent to the destinies of the child who is being born, and who enters by the fact of his birth into the universal harmony of the <term>sidereal world</term>. The stars are bound together by attractions which balance them and cause them to perform their revolutions with regularity in space; the network of light extends from sphere to sphere, and there is no point on any planet to which one of these indestructible threads is not attached. The precise place and moment of birth should therefore be calculated by the true <term>astrological adept</term>; then, after an exact computation of the <term>starry influences</term>, it remains for him to reckon the chances of condition—that is, the opportunities or obstacles which the child must one day meet with in his state of life, in his relatives, in the disposition he inherits, and, consequently, in his natural aptitude for the fulfillment of his destinies. Human liberty and enterprise must also be taken into account, should the child come to be truly a man and to extricate himself by a bold will from blind influences and from the chain of fatality. It will be seen that we do not allow too much to astrology, but what we leave to it is incontestable: it is the <term>scientific and magical calculus of probabilities</term>.”

It may be at once admitted that there is nothing indifferent in Nature; it may be admitted that a stone upon a road may modify the destiny of any person, and,

<summary>This page introduces a defense of astrology by Eliphas Lévi, who argues that the positions of stars at birth influence a child's destiny through a universal harmony of light and attraction. Lévi describes astrology as a calculation of probabilities that must also account for human will and environmental factors.</summary>

<keywords>Astrology, Eliphas Lévi, sidereal world, astrological adept, starry influences, calculus of probabilities, destiny, celestial influence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the validity of astrology, transitioning from Eliphas Lévi’s theoretical defense of planetary influences to a critique of how the science is practically applied and its distinction from mysticism.</meta>

indirectly, of any people. It may also, and no less, be allowed that the aspect of the starry universe at the moment of birth—provided other considerations are granted—cannot be a matter of indifference to the child. All of this is suggestive; all of this is, at any rate, practically reasonable. However, while a stone upon the road—should it produce an effect upon a person passing by—will produce an effect that can be calculated, the influence of the stars on a <term>nativity</term> is one that cannot be measured by ordinary methods. It is assessed by <term>astrology</term> in a completely *empirical* manner, so far as can be judged from a standpoint of outside impartiality. It is, in any case, quite certain that the *<term>calculus of probabilities</term>* described in the paragraph above is impossible to carry through and has never been attempted by <term>astrology</term>.

In discussing the claims of this—the least occult of the secret sciences, which is nonetheless so venerable for its antiquity, so majestic in its associations, and so sublime in its general theory and objective—one would like to be as lenient as possible and to find a way to believe what everyone must wish were true: that day unto day utters speech, and night unto night shows knowledge, and that it is possible to translate the magical language of the stars into intelligible prophecies of the unfulfilled. The distant <term>music of the spheres</term> has found an interpreter; the splendid pageantry of “worlds revolving unceasingly” assumes a new significance. Through all the depths and heights of the “magical, measureless distance,” we behold intelligence everywhere; the planets are prophets, the stars are seers, and mind rules the universe. One dreams of <term>Chaldean lore</term>, of the clear vision and the patient study of the wise workers of old. <term>Astrology</term>, it is clear, if we regard the eternal fitness of things, ought to be a true instrument of knowledge.

But those who are mystics must remember that <term>astrology</term>, though it passes for a secret science, is scarcely a branch of <term>mysticism</term>. If it is capable of producing truly genuine results, it must be considered to some extent as standing alone among the sciences called magical, because while it cannot be classified as unimportant, its best results

can contribute nothing to the science of the soul. However

<margin>Vertical line on left margin alongside final sentence; check mark on right margin next to names</margin>
Astrology proceeds by methods that are said to be
the result of many ages of accumulated experimentation. The
aspect of a certain planet at the moment of birth is
supposed to have a definite effect upon the life of the
"native," because it has been observed in the past that
it actually has that effect, and it is in this way that the science
has been built up. Its assumptions are therefore of a
strictly *à posteriori* kind; it asks no faith of the student,
and each person is free to test its value by their own
experiments, after which they may reasonably judge it. It
is therefore useless to condemn astrology until it has been
honestly weighed and found wanting. It is impossible
in an elementary handbook to provide our readers with
the means of examining the truth of astrological claims.
If they wish to pursue the subject, they must have re-
course to the voluminous authorities that exist. They
must study Placidus de Titus and Junctin de Florence,
Ptolemy, Lilly, and Partridge.

<margin>Red vertical line and pencil bracket on left margin; mark resembling an 'M' and two vertical slashes // on right margin</margin>
The writers who, under the names of "Raphael" and
"Zadkiel," issue "textbooks" and "guides" to the
science, now share whatever honor or profit
is to be derived from the teaching of astrology in the
England of our own day. Miss Rosa Baughan has,
in a recent book, made astrology the basis of another
<insert>red underline</insert>"occult" science, namely, that of palmistry.

Avoiding the responsibility of deciding between the
rival systems of Placidus and Ptolemy, or of horary and
genethliacal astrology, we proceed to give a summarized
version of the theory on which all systems of astrology
must rest.

<note>Vertical ink line in the left margin next to the first four lines.</note>Signs of the same nature are found in the sky; for instance, the Ram, the Lion, and the Archer. These three form the <gloss>trine aspect</gloss> because they <gloss>divide the heavens into three parts</gloss> and are separated from one another by three other constellations. Such an aspect is good and favorable. When those that divide the sky by six meet at the hour of the operation—such as the Ram with the Twins or the Bull with the Crab—the <gloss>sextile aspect</gloss> is formed, which is of a <gloss>moderate quality</gloss>. When those which divide the sphere <gloss>into four</gloss>—such as the Ram with the Crab, the Bull with the Lion, or the Twins with the Virgin—meet at the hour of operation, they form the <gloss>quartile aspect</gloss>, which is distinctly a <gloss>bad one</gloss>. When those which belong to opposite parts of the sky—such as the Ram with the Balance, the Bull with the Scorpion, and the Twins with the Archer, etc.—are found similarly placed, the <gloss>contrary aspect</gloss> is formed, which is <gloss>harmful and malevolent</gloss>. <note>Vertical ink line in the right margin marking the following section on conjunction and opposition.</note>The others are in conjunction when two planets are united in the same sign and in the same house; they are in opposition when they are situated at two opposite points.

Each sign of the Zodiac occupies a place which is called the <gloss>Celestial House or the House of the Sun</gloss>, and the twelve houses consequently <gloss>divide the Zodiac into twelve parts</gloss>; each house occupies thirty degrees, and each was represented by the astrologers of old by numerical symbols within a square or circular figure divided twelve times.

The <gloss>first house</gloss> is that of <gloss>Aries</gloss>, which is called the Orient Angle in the language of stargazers. This is the <gloss>house of life</gloss>, because those who are born under its dominion enjoy a considerable length of years. The <gloss>second house</gloss> is that of <gloss>Taurus</gloss>, which is called the Inferior Gate. It is the <gloss>house of wealth</gloss> and of the way to fortune. The <gloss>third house</gloss> is that of <gloss>Gemini</gloss>, and it is called the Abode of the Brothers. It is the house of inheritance and of solid patrimony. The <gloss>fourth house</gloss> is that of Cancer, which is called the Foundation of Heaven, the Earth’s Angle, and the Abode of Parents. It is the house of treasure and of the profits of patrimony. The <gloss>fifth house</gloss> is that of Leo, and it is the abode of children; it is also that of legacies and donations. The

# ASTROLOGY.
<meta>Handwritten markings throughout: Blue and red pencil underlining of astrological terms; a red bracket in the left margin next to the description of the ninth house; a red tick mark next to the start of the second paragraph; a faint blue horizontal strike-through line through the lower half of the third paragraph.</meta>

The sixth house is the house of Virgo; it is called the Love of Mars; it is the house of sadness, disease, and setbacks. The seventh is that of Libra, which is called the Western Angle; it is the house of marriage and weddings. The eighth house is that of Scorpio, which is called the Superior Door; it is the abode of terror, alarms, and death. The ninth house is that of Sagittarius; it is called the Love of the Sun; and piety, religion, travels, and philosophy are contained within it. The tenth house is that of Capricornus, which is called the Center of Heaven, and crowns, honors, and responsible offices are attributed to it. The eleventh house is identified with Aquarius, which is called the Love of Jove; it is the house of friends, favors, and fortune. The twelfth house is that of Pisces, which is called the Love of Saturn. It is the most baleful and fatal of all; it is the house of poisonings, of misery, of envy, of ill temper, and of violent death.

The Ram and the Scorpion are the houses influenced by Mars; the Bull and the Balance are attributed to Venus; the Twins and the Virgin to Mercury; the Archer and the Fishes to Jupiter; the Goat and the Water-bearer to Saturn; the Lion to the Sun; and the Crab to the Moon.

The position of the planets in relation to the constellations must be carefully noted, and as the rapid revolution of the earth causes changes every moment in the arrangement of the stars, the exact moment of birth should be confirmed by the midwife if an accurate horoscope is to be ensured. When Mars is in agreement with Aries at the moment of birth, it grants longevity, courage, and pride; if it is in agreement with Taurus, wealth and courage result. Broadly speaking, the influence of Mars increases that of the constellation with which it aligns, adding strength and value. Saturn, who inflicts penalties, miseries, and disease, increases the evil and nullifies the good influences; Venus, on the contrary, increases those which are good while it weakens the power of those which are evil. Mercury increases or weakens influences according to the nature of its conjunctions. In agreement with Pisces, its good effect is diminished; with Capricornus, it is extended. The

# THE OCCULT SCIENCES.

The Moon adds melancholy to fortunate constellations, and sadness or insanity to others. Jupiter, giving honors and riches, increases all favorable influences and almost destroys bad ones. The Sun in the <term>ascendant</term> gives the favor of princes, and its effect upon influences is akin to that of Jupiter; in the <term>descendant</term> it foreshadows reverses. Gemini, Libra, and Virgo give beauty in a superlative degree; Scorpio, Capricornus, and Pisces give beauty in a middle degree; the others more or less of ugliness. Virgo, Libra, Aquarius, and Gemini endow the <term>native</term> with a pleasant and melodious voice; Cancer, Scorpio, and Pisces give one that is ordinary or disagreeable, while the others are devoid of influence in this respect. <margin>pencil bracket along the right edge</margin> If the planets and constellations are found in the East at the time of the <term>horoscope</term>, their influence will be experienced at the opening of the life or the enterprise; if they are seen in the <term>zenith</term> the effect will be postponed until the middle, and until the latter end if they appear in the West.

These general notions may be supplemented by an account of the properties of the seven planets, <margin>v-shaped mark</margin> compiled by Elihu Rich from a number of ancient authorities. Saturn is cold and dry, melancholy, earthy, malevolent, solitary, and when “ill-governed” produces the most malignant qualities; the Buddhists represent him as crowned and of a black color. Astrologers consider him the “greater infortune.” Jupiter, the “greater fortune,” is the author of temperance, modesty, sobriety, and justice; he rules the lungs and blood, and the last month in gestation; he is represented in a golden color, riding upon a lion. The colors under him are sea-green, blue, and purple. Mars, the “lesser infortune,” is choleric and fierce, the author of quarrels, dissensions, strife, war, and battle; his colors are red and white; he is depicted riding on a peacock, with a crown on his head; fierce animals, blood-colored stones, etc., are under his influence, and he is the cause of all fevers. The Moon is feminine, neither fortunate nor unfortunate in herself, but having an influence in accordance with the aspect of other planets; her metal is silver; her colors white, pale green, and pale yellow; when well dignified

she [the Moon] gives a timid, imaginative, engaging disposition, and a fondness for change and traveling. She is represented crowned and riding on an elephant. Venus, the “lesser fortune,” is the source of joy and sociability, the queen of pleasure, and the mistress of refinement; her colors are white and light blue. The Buddhists depict her crowned, riding upon a bull; when poorly positioned, she disposes one to lust and immorality. Mercury is the source of the sharpest wit, ingenuity, and invention; when well-positioned, he produces a subtle imagination and a retentive memory; otherwise, his tendency is toward all kinds of trickery, empty boasting, and gossip. His metal is quicksilver, his angel is Raphael, and his colors are black and azure. He rides on a buffalo and is painted dark blue. He is masculine or feminine according to his alignment with other planets. The Sun, if well-positioned, is always equal to one of the fortunes; in some respects, his influence is equal to that of Jupiter, but generosity is his predominant characteristic. The diamond, the ruby, the carbuncle, pure gold, and all yellow metals are under his rule; he is represented riding on a horse.

In addition to the direct influence which is exercised by the celestial universe on the destinies and dispositions of men, there is an influence of an indirect nature on life and fortune through the control which is assigned to the planets and the stars of the twelve constellations over the various parts of the human body. The head, which is the seat of intelligence, is assigned to the care of the Sun, which is the root and source of the influences diffused from the entire planetary system; the Moon governs the right arm; Venus has dominion over the left; Jupiter presides over the stomach; the energies of the sexual organs are directed by the powerful spirit of the red-colored Mars; Mercury dominates the right foot, which is said to be the seat of speed; the left foot is assigned to Saturn. # THE OCCULT SCIENCES.

The spiritual hypotheses of angelology have assigned the government of empires and cities to the ministers of Heaven, but the science of the stars has discovered that the responsibility of the spiritual hierarchies is shared by the great constellations. It has been decided by German adepts in the mysteries of celestial influences that Frankfurt is ruled by the Ram, Wurtzburg by the Bull, Nuremberg by the Twins—which should therefore be the sign of the Rosicrucian Fraternity; Magdeburg by the Crab, Ulm by the Lion, Heidelberg by the Virgin, Vienna by the Balance (which today has a certain appropriateness, as it is there that the peace of Europe is held in an oscillating equilibrium); Munich by the Scorpion, Stuttgart by the Archer, Augsburg by the Goat, Ingolstadt by the Water-bearer, and Ratisbonne by the Fishes.

An authority more venerable than German astrologers, the mythical messenger Hermes, has placed the seven openings in the human head under the special direction of the seven planets, and has assigned Saturn and Jupiter to the ears, Mars and Venus to the nostrils, the Sun and Moon to the eyes, and Mercury to the mouth. <insert>Leon the Israelite</insert>, in his Kabbalistic “<insert>Philosophy of Love</insert>,” explains that the sun and moon are appropriately assigned to the eyes, as those luminaries are the eyes of the superior world; while Mercury—the messenger, the revealer, the true Hermes, the Lord of the Logos—has a natural rule over speech.

The list is by no means exhausted. Saturn has power over life, changes, buildings, and the circle of the sciences; Jupiter over honor, ambition, wealth, and clean habits; Mars over war, prisons, marriages, and animosities; the Sun over hopes, happiness, profit, and inheritances; Venus over friendship and love; Mercury over diseases, losses, debts, commerce, and the sources of fear that exist in the human soul; and the Moon over sores and wounds, dreams and thefts. Such is the division laid down by Albertus Magnus—namely, in the “Admirable Secrets,” which,

The planets also have precedence over the seven days of the week, a fact which is too commonly known to need more than a passing reference; but as the emblems which astrology has assigned to the planets it was acquainted with are continually recurring in Hermetic science, and especially in physical alchemy, it will be as well to reproduce them here for the benefit of beginning inquirers.

There is a simplified form of astrology which, like most simplifications in occultism, is vague and unsatisfying in its results. This is the method of foretelling the destiny of an individual from the position of the constellations at his birth. It considerably limits the varieties of human disposition and reduces the scope of fortune within exceedingly narrow bounds. Astrological calculations have the interest of curiosity alone, and well-balanced minds will be disinclined to review the observations which follow in anything but an antiquarian light.

### *The Signs in the Heavens, and the Prophetic Significance Involved in Them.*

1. THE RAM.—The power or influence of Aries is hot and dry. It rules in the heavens from the 21st of March to the 23rd of April. Those who are born under its influence are quick-tempered, prompt, lively, eloquent, and inclined to study, but also violent, untruthful, and inconstant. They are seldom faithful to their word and quickly forget their promises. The native receives from the constellation a dry, lean, and slender body; he is strong and large-boned, has piercing eyes, sandy or red hair, and a swarthy complexion. His chief dangers will be from horses, and he will be fond of fishing and hunting.

The woman who is born under Aries will be vivacious, inquisitive, and pretty. She will be fond of gossip,

She will marry soon and will be the mother of many children. The presence of Jupiter or Venus in this sign at the hour of birth will impart a better influence to it; Saturn or Mars will intensify its malignant qualities. Aries rules the head and face and produces smallpox, epilepsy, apoplexy, headaches, hypochondriasis, baldness, ringworm, etc. The color which corresponds to it is white mixed with red.

2. **THE BULL.**<insert>underlined in red ink</insert>—The influence of Taurus predominates from the 22nd of April to the 21st of May. It is cold, dry, earthy, melancholy, and feminine; it imparts to the native a broad brow, thick lips, and a rather brutal and melancholy temperament, slow to anger but violent and difficult to appease once enraged. From these observations, it is plain that it also imparts the lower kind of courage as well as physical strength. The person born under its influence will make enemies but will be able to prevent them from injuring him. He will enjoy a certain kind of happiness and will travel to far countries; his life will be long, and he will be subject to few diseases.

The woman who is born under this constellation is endowed with strength and courage. She will have great energy but will be violent and easily carried away. At the same time, she will be able to submit to her duty and will prove an obedient wife. Much judgment and much good sense will characterize her, but she will be somewhat over-talkative. She will be a widow more than once; she will bear children to whom she will leave fortunes. The color corresponding to Taurus is white mixed with citron.

3. **THE TWINS.**—The power or influence of Gemini is hot and moist, like a fat, rich soil. The native will be fair and tall, with a straight body and a sanguine complexion, rather dark than clear. The arms will be long, the hands frequently short and fleshy, the hair and eyes generally a dark hazel, the looks wanton, and the understanding skillful in business. This constellation is rich in

He will be lively, subtle, and pleasure-loving, and will have some taste for the arts.

The woman who is born under Gemini will be affectionate and beautiful, with a gentle and simple heart, but somewhat neglectful of domestic duties. The fine arts, more especially music and drawing, will exercise a great fascination over her mind.

The diseases which belong to the sign are those of the arms, shoulders, and hands, as well as frenzies, fevers, blood poisoning, disorders of the brain, and insanity. The color corresponding to Gemini is white mixed with red. The constellation governs from the 22nd of May to the 21st of June.

4. **THE CRAB.**—This sign stirs up squabbles; it is cold and moist, like a marshy and boggy land. It rules in heaven from the 22nd of June to the 21st of July. The native who is born under its influence is fair and pale, of a short and small stature, with the upper part of the body being large in proportion to the lower, a round face, brown hair, and gray eyes; the disposition will be <term>phlegmatic</term> and heavy, the constitution weak, and the voice faint. Men under its influence will be sensual; their lives will be checkered with lawsuits and quarrels, from which, however, they may often emerge successfully. Their chief dangers will be at sea. Moreover, this horoscope commonly gives a tendency toward gluttony, though occasionally prudence, wit, and a touch of modesty.

Women who are born under this constellation will be inclined to have many children; they will be quite good-looking, active, and quick-tempered, but soon calmed. They will have no tendency toward excessive obesity. They will

5. **THE LION.**—The dominion of Leo lasts from the 22nd of July to the 21st of August. It is fiery, hot, dry, masculine, <term>choleric</term>, barren, but commanding. It gives to the native a large masculine body, with broad shoulders, an austere countenance, hair tending toward yellow, piercing eyes, a sprightly look, a strong voice, and an oval face of a ruddy or <term>sanguine</term> complexion. The character which it will impart is courageous and resolute, brave, audacious, magnanimous, proud, eloquent, and self-sufficient; the mind is aspiring, the heart generous, and the disposition open and courteous. Such a person will love joking and will be frequently surrounded by danger, but his children will be his consolation and happiness. He will be continually giving himself over to passion, and will just as continually repent for his excesses. Honors and dignities will be showered on him, but, in the first instance, he will have sought them for a long time.

The woman who is born under this constellation will be vivacious, hasty, and bold. She will hold grudges; she will talk much, and her speech will frequently be bitter. For the rest, she will be good-looking, though with a large head. She must beware of boiling water and of fire. She will be subject to pains in the stomach and will have few children. This constellation produces all the passions and affections of the heart, as also convulsions, violent fevers, plagues, and pains in the back. The color is red or green.

Diseases of the intestines are attributed to the sign of the Virgin. Black speckled with blue are the colors which correspond to its influence.

7. **THE BALANCE** rules in the celestial sphere from the 22nd of September to the 21st of October. The sign is volatile in character; it is <term>sanguine</term>, hot, moist, and masculine. Men who are born under this sign have a tall and well-proportioned body, a handsome face, and a complexion which in youth is ruddy, and in old age florid and pimply; the eyes are gray, and the hair yellow or flaxen. According to one account—for accounts differ—the disposition is friendly and courteous, and the character is distinguished for rectitude; according to another, it is commonly quarrelsome and pleasure-loving. Success may be expected chiefly in maritime enterprise, though it is attainable in any kind of commerce; the native, in either case, is certain to undertake long voyages. His manners will be easy, his speech fluent, and his promises faithless; his good fortune will be greater than his prudence, and he will be the heir of many. He will love his first wife and will not have many children. His chief dangers are from the malice of arsonists and from boiling water.

The woman who enters life under the auspices of this constellation will be amiable, cheerful, agreeable, sprightly, and reasonably fortunate. Her manners will be good, her speech persuasive, and her disposition sensitive and quarrelsome. She will be married either at seventeen or at twenty-three, and must beware of the same dangers as the male native.

8. The <term>Scorpion</term> is feminine, phlegmatic, and cold. It rules in the heavens from October 22 to November 21. Those who are born under its influence will be robust and stout, of medium height, with a broad face, a brown complexion, and black curly hair; the neck will be short, the legs thick, and the body hairy. Authorities differ regarding the disposition. By some, it is defined as simply reserved and thoughtful; others describe it at greater length and with opposing qualities. According to them, the person will be impudent, brazen, fawning, and crafty, concealing malice under an amiable exterior. The native will say one thing and think another, and will generally be underhanded and prone to deceit. He will be naturally changeable, will judge others poorly, will harbor ill feelings, and will be talkative but subject to fits of despondency. When he laughs, it will be chiefly at the expense of others, yet he will have some friends and will prevail over his enemies. He may expect substantial legacies. Among illnesses, he will be chiefly subject to disorders of the stomach.

The woman who is born under the influence of this constellation will be deceptive and subtle; she will behave better to her second husband than to her first; her words will be milder than her feelings; she will be sensuous, vivacious, and a great laugher, though also at the expense of others. She will be inconsistent in her actions, talkative in her speech, and evil in her thoughts toward everyone.

The diseases which are attributed to this constellation are similar to those of the last, but include scurvy. The color which corresponds to it is dark brown.

The foundation of the vast treatise by Junctin de Florence is a rare Greek treatise by Ptolemy of Pelouse, one of the last of the grand succession of Eastern initiates who glorified the Alexandrian school of philosophy. The original manuscript articulated the Hermetic traditions of Egyptian Eastern magic, while the commentary of Junctin de Florence contains the results of its author’s lifelong research into the depths of Chaldaic Astromancy, Jewish Kabbalism, and Arabian Theurgy. Julius Firmius Maternus also drew the foundation of his astrological methods from the work of Ptolemy, producing, in a smaller volume, an absolutely practical manual. Junctin de Florence was an illustrious doctor of theology and the almoner of François de Valois, the youngest brother of Henry III of France. Julius Firmius Maternus was a Sicilian priest of the fourth century. The fortunate owner of these works may soon find himself in possession of the secret code of Astrology, but the substance of both is embodied in Christian’s “*Histoire de la Magie*,” 8vo, Paris, 1870. This disciple of Eliphas Lévi gives a general theory of the Horoscope and the Keys of Astrological Science, which he calls almost infallible.
