# KABBALISM.

As in other branches of magical literature, there are two views current regarding Kabbalism. There is the view of skeptical criticism, which considers it historically to be a blatant forgery and, in itself, a barbaric and unintelligible jargon; and there is the view of uncritical belief, which accepts it as "the key to things kept secret from the foundation of the world." The truth, as in other cases, seems to lie between these two extremes. Before attempting to evaluate the science, it is necessary, for the benefit of the novice inquirer, to make a few preliminary explanations.

Kabbalism is an art or a science derived from the <term>Kabbalah</term>, a term which signifies "reception," in the sense of a heritage handed down from father to son. It is used to denote a substantial body of esoteric or mystical literature belonging to the Jews. Its most important divisions are the <term>Bahir</term>, which is said to be the most ancient book of rabbinical literature, but which seems to be completely unknown to many occult students today; the <term>Zohar</term>, or book of splendor; the <term>Sepher Sephiroth</term>, or book of emanations; the <term>Sepher Jetzirah</term>, which treats of the thirty-two paths of the Absolute; and the <term>Asch Metzareph</term> <note>A Kabbalistic-alchemical text known as "The Purifying Fire."</note>. The philosophical doctrines developed in these works are claimed to have been preserved through the secret method of oral tradition from the earliest ages of humanity. "The Kabbalah," says Dr. Ginsburg, when explaining the story of its origin, "was first taught by God Himself to a select company of angels, who formed a theosophical school in Paradise. After the Fall, the angels most graciously communicated this heavenly doctrine to the disobedient child of earth, to provide the <term>protoplasts</term> <note>The first-formed humans, specifically Adam and Eve.</note> with the means of returning to their original nobility and

<summary>This page introduces Kabbalism, defining it as a Jewish mystical tradition of "reception" and listing its core historical texts. It explores the conflicting views on its authenticity and recounts the legend of its divine origin in Paradise.</summary>
<keywords>Kabbalism, Kabbalah, Bahir, Zohar, Sepher Sephiroth, Sepher Jetzirah, Asch Metzareph, Dr. Ginsburg, Jewish mysticism, oral tradition, protoplasts</keywords>

<header>THE OCCULT SCIENCES.</header>

> felicity. From Adam it passed to Noah, and then to Abraham, the friend of God, who emigrated with it to Egypt, where the patriarch allowed a portion of this mysterious doctrine to leak out. In this way, the Egyptians obtained some knowledge of it, and other Eastern nations were able to introduce it into their philosophical systems. Moses, who was educated in all the wisdom of Egypt, was first initiated into the <term>Kabbalah</term> in the land of his birth, but became most proficient in it during his wanderings in the wilderness. There, he not only devoted his leisure hours to it for the entire forty years, but also received lessons in it from one of the angels. With the help of this mysterious science, the lawgiver was able to solve the difficulties that arose during his leadership of the Israelites, despite the pilgrimages, wars, and frequent miseries of the nation. He covertly set down the principles of this secret doctrine in the first four books of the Pentateuch, but withheld them from Deuteronomy. Moses also initiated the seventy Elders into the secrets of this doctrine, and they, in turn, passed them down. Of everyone who formed the unbroken line of tradition, David and Solomon were the most deeply initiated into the Kabbalah. However, no one dared to write it down until Schimeon ben Jochai, who lived at the time of the destruction of the second <note>The Second Temple in Jerusalem.</note>. After his death, his son, Rabbi Eleazar, and his secretary, Rabbi Abba, as well as his disciples, compiled Rabbi Simon Ben Jochai's treatises. From these, they composed the celebrated work called Z H R, *Zohar*, or Splendor, which is the great storehouse of Kabbalism."

This version of the history of Jewish transcendental literature is accepted today by many mystical thinkers who are not themselves uneducated, but whose reverence has dulled their critical thinking. Their imaginations are overwhelmed by the grandiose speculations of the Jewish mystics. Being naturally inclined toward belief, they accept the antiquity of the <term>Kabbalah</term> not necessarily for the reason that prompted the intense faith of Tertullian—*Credo quia absurdum* <note>Latin for "I believe because it is absurd."</note>—but because it is a grand concept, just as they believe in the antiquity of

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the legendary history of the Kabbalah, transitioning from its mythical origins to its association with modern secret societies and the scholarly evidence regarding its true historical timeline.</meta>

# KABBALISM.

the Rosicrucian Society, and in the connection of the Masonic Brotherhood with the splendid order of the Temple and with the vague wonders of the Eleusinian mysteries. The student who is in search of truth must, however, be guarded against the glamour of romance, and he will do well to realize that this supposed history of Kabbalistic origins is almost entirely mythical, and no evidence worthy of the name can be brought forward to support it. It is possible that a certain amount of secret doctrine and a certain number of secret interpretive methods were perpetuated by tradition in Judea, and that they were ultimately incorporated into the <term>Kabbalah</term>, but this is the sum total of its claim to antiquity. On the other hand, there is practically no doubt that the most prized, the most voluminous, and, philosophically, the most suggestive and valuable section of Kabbalistic literature is a work of the thirteenth century.

In his essay on "The Kabbalah; its Doctrines, Development, and Literature," Dr. Ginsburg enters into a detailed examination of the facts and arguments which have satisfied the most eminent authorities that the "Zohar" is of modern origin. It would be beyond the scope of our purpose to list them in these pages in an exhaustive manner, but it may be briefly remarked that this treatise provides a mystical explanation of the Hebrew vowel points which were introduced at the close of the sixth century; that it cites an author who is known to have lived and died during the eleventh century; that it mentions the Crusades; and that it records events which took place in the year A.D. 1264. It was a few years before 1291 that copies of the Zohar first began to be circulated by a Spanish Jew named Moses De Leon, who claimed to be in possession of an original manuscript by its reputed author, Rabbi Simon ben Jochai. The value of this claim was made evident after his death, when the wealthy Joseph de Avila promised the destitute widow of Moses de Leon "that if she would give him the original manuscript of the Zohar from which her husband made the copies, his son should marry her daughter, and that he would give them a handsome dowry. Whereupon the widow and"

In the same way, the consensus of authorities refers the *Book of Creation*, attributed to the patriarch Abraham, to "the ninth century of the Christian era, when it first became known," and the rest of this curious literature to a variety of periods ranging between the eighth and the thirteenth centuries. As early as the beginning of the fifteenth century, it may be added, the suspicious character of the *Zohar* was exposed by a <insert>learned philosophical Israelite of the University of Padua.</insert>

The mysticism of the <insert>Mishna</insert> and the <insert>Talmud</insert> must be carefully distinguished from that of the Kabbalistic writings. There can be no reasonable doubt regarding the comparative antiquity of the two former cycles of Jewish literature. Any antiquity belonging to the doctrines which are developed in the latter must be traced to the Alexandrian school of theurgic magic which, post-Christian as it was, may be admitted to have been the heir of many ancient traditions.

<margin>y</margin>
Having established, as far as possible in a short summary of the latest results of independent criticism, the actual antiquity of the chief books comprised in the Kabbalah, it is necessary to say a few words regarding its claims on the modern student of mysticism. It is clear that its philosophical value is not diminished by its modern origin; that value is completely independent of its position in history. It is said by its admirers to be an absolute guide to knowledge in all the most profound problems of life. It indicates the nature <insert>[</insert>and attributes of God, the Supreme Being, whom, however, it does not attempt to define. It indicates the origin and evolution of the cosmos; it describes the creation of angels and of men, the nature possessed by both, along with the nature of demons, and of the intermediate and elementary beings which fill the universe. It affords a satisfactory solution of the grand mysteries connected with the soul of man and expounds the

measure of the Invisible." And again: "The religion
of the Kabbalists is at once all hypothesis and all certainty,
for it proceeds by analogy from the Known to the
Unknown." In other words, there is a correspondence
between the seen and the unseen, and it is possible to
reason from the one to the other. <note>Pencil underline</note>Pseudo-Hermes has
given another and axiomatic presentation of the same
<margin>Pencil triangle pointing to this line</margin>notion: "That which is above is in proportion with
that which is below, and that which is below is in proportion
with that which is above." You can reason up from
man to God, from earth to heaven, from body to spirit.
This argument from analogy is something like the
argument from design; it is not satisfactory, it is not
reliable, and yet it is difficult to do without it. We
fashion the World Beyond in the likeness of the world
about us, because we are unable to imagine a wholly
different order of existence from that with which experience
has acquainted us. At the same time, the limitations
of our own nature cannot certainly, nor, indeed,
reasonably, be upheld as the <note>Pencil underline</note>measure of eternity, and a
philosophy which invests this limitation with an absolute
value may be bold, as it also may be ingenious, but there
is no obvious or irresistible reason for accepting it as a
sober guide.

When we turn from modern interpretations of the Kabbalah, such as those provided by Eliphas Lévi, to the grotesque and unmanageable typology of the original works—and become acquainted with their crudity, inadequacy, and arbitrariness—it becomes more than doubtful whether they will repay the extremely painstaking study required to master them. They may indeed be read for their occasional touches of sublimity, for their occasional insights, and at times even for their poetic suggestiveness; of course, they also have their place in the history of philosophy and Jewish literature. We do not believe that anything undertaken beyond this will be profitable. <margin>A red ink bracket in the margin marks the following sentence.</margin>The investigation of the numerical value of words, the effects resulting from removing their first letters, shortening them, or substituting new letters, and the mysteries concealed in the shapes of alphabetical symbols, are not actually more serious subjects than any other intellectual gymnastics found in the world of puzzles. In the paper on the summoning of angels at the beginning of this book, we have provided some extensive examples of Kabbalistic investigations conducted in the transcendental region of pneumatology through curious calculations. Their value is superficial and easily estimated; it is truly too late to attempt any serious revival of this kind of occultism. The best lesson we can learn from it is the necessity—on which we have continually insisted—of scrupulously separating the experimental knowledge of the mystics from their bizarre fields of speculation.

# PART III.

---

## THE MYSTICS.

The reader has already been made acquainted with the defined end of mysticism and with the broad division of the mystics into two classes—physical and spiritual. The physical mystics worked in accordance with <term>Hermetic mysticism</term> in the world of metals and minerals; the labors of the others were devoted to the development of the secret possibilities that reside in the subject of Man. For all practical purposes, the history of the alchemists proper begins with <term>Geber</term>, an Arab whose life is enveloped in an obscurity that has given a romantic warrant to a number of magnificent fables. He flourished most likely in the eighth century of the Christian era. The first ascertainable reference in Western literature to the <term>transmutation of metals</term> occurs, as already stated, in a writing of <term>Zosimus the Pomopolite</term> <note>Likely Zosimus of Panopolis, a Greco-Egyptian alchemist and mystic</note>, who belongs to the fifth century; but the origin of the great experiment has been very imperfectly investigated. There is a literature of <term>Oriental alchemy</term> existing in Arabic manuscripts, now scattered and practically entombed in the public libraries of Europe; by studying these, the origins of alchemy can be elucidated. But we lack a history of chemistry as it was known to the ancients. We lack, in particular, an exhaustive catalogue of all chemical subjects known to the ancient Egyptians, Greeks, Romans, and Jews. That would determine the actual nature of the materials, the knowledge of which was inherited by the followers of the Arab Geber. It would settle the debatable point of how far these men in reality contributed to the foundation and extension of <term>exoteric chemistry</term>. Existing histories of this interest—

<summary>This page defines the two branches of mysticism—physical and spiritual—and introduces the history of alchemy, beginning with Geber and Zosimus. The author argues that a comprehensive history of ancient chemical knowledge is necessary to understand the foundations of the field.</summary>

Thirdly, we are in need of a history of chemical development among the <term>Hermetists</term>, which could be compiled from a study of their voluminous writings in historical order. These matters must necessarily be handed over to an inner circle of specialists, working to some extent in common; but they are of undoubted importance to all students of <term>mysticism</term>, because they will determine the true place of <term>alchemy</term> in the history of the sciences. They will also manifest a plain line of demarcation between alchemists proper and the spiritual mystics, with whom they are too completely identified by the wonder-mongering theories of an uncritical <term>transcendentalism</term>.

From the period of <term>Geber</term> to that of <term>Paracelsus</term>, the literature of physical alchemy appears to have been sufficiently material, and the methods pursued in the achieved or attempted performance of the <term>Great Work</term> were simply so much wading through an enormous variety of processes. These frequently dissipated the fortunes and squandered the lives of the workers without any definite good being attained. When the erratic splendor of the mind of <term>Paracelsus</term> rose like a comet over the intellectual horizon of Germany, a change came. He was the first to import into the Hermetic experiment an element which was of a magical character; he admitted that gold might be made by the mundane process of common chemistry, but he denounced this as inefficient and poor when compared with the result which might be produced by an exercise of the <term>arcane potencies</term> of the human soul. There are certain operations in which one is inclined to believe that something of the nature of the worker passes into the manipulated substance. The cook, like the poet, is born and not made, and under the influence of the culinary mission something

Now, the alchemist is the cook of mineralogy, and the digestion of food and minerals is described in the French language by the same term. Yet it is extremely difficult to understand how a projection of vital fluid can accomplish metallic transmutation. Skill, genius, and willpower can modify forms and vary combinations, but they cannot change natures; for which reason one is inclined to believe that the substance produced by the psycho-chemistry of Paracelsus was simply a refined artifice—a modified form, a substance changed in appearance, but the same in its fundamental nature. On the other hand, a discerning critic might perceive in this magical psycho-mineralogy and pneumatic metallurgy a pleasant way of distinguishing between the alchemical methods of a talented man who infuses genius into his operations, and the barren stupidities of the unimaginative and coarse-minded <term>souffleur</term>. However this may be, it is certain that Paracelsus achieved an immense reputation and created a school of chemistry which subsequently produced the damask-like wealth and richness of that “mysterious flower” of allegory, the Rose-Cross. More completely than ever, the science of <term>alchemy</term> became involved in mysticism and centered in religion. It was a work which was impossible for the unregenerate; it was one of the secrets of God—*magnalia Dei et Naturæ*—and the emphasis on this idea more completely obscured it with mystery, for *sacramentum Regis abscondere bonum est*. From the psychic manufacture of gold, it is but a step to the conversion of the metallic subject into an allegorical idea, and men began to attribute to the philosophers of old a desire less base than the search for unlimited wealth, and to credit them with a spiritual intention. The theory of the Hermetic practice was made transcendental by Jacob Boehme. Alongside this new school, the followers of physical alchemy continued to increase their numbers until the beginning of the eighteenth century, when, for historical purposes, it died out with the mysterious Archimandrite Lascaris; a revival attempted by Cagliostro, some fifty years later, was probably fraudulent.

As fascinating as the history of alchemy and the men who pursued it may be, there can be no comparison between its interest and the history of the larger *opus* (work) of the spiritual mystics. It is at this point that one feels the need for a history of Christian mysticism in its relationship to universal mysticism—a work which would reward and require a lifetime of labor and study.

One definition of mysticism has already been given in the course of this work. There is another which, though appearing less precise, is also good. We must remember that mysticism is a science, a *moyen de parvenir* (a way of attaining); it provides the human mind with an instrument of knowledge. In the words of an encyclopedist who has treated this aspiration with a fair amount of respect—it is the endeavor of the human mind to grasp the divine essence or ultimate reality of all things, and to enjoy, while in this life and in this body, the bliss of an immediate communion with the Highest. It recognizes the totality of finite existence as a theophany—a “circle which always returns to the very same spot,” to that point “which has no parts and which has no magnitude”—God being the beginning and the end of all. Universalism is implicitly included in the terms of this proposition, as the law of the universe necessitates beatitude (supreme blessedness) as the end of all that is conscious—namely, all that is philosophically capable of a permanent existence—and thus it definitely determines the nature of

->“that far-off, divine event<-
->To which the whole creation moves.”<-

As in practical alchemy, so in spiritual mysticism, Paracelsus stands forth as a figure of considerable his-

<meta>This page continues the assessment of Paracelsus, specifically focusing on his role as a bridge between the physical practices of alchemy and the spiritual pursuits of the mystics, while situating the geographic center of Christian mysticism in Germany.</meta>

He has historical importance; due to the limitations of his character, it is entirely improbable that he reached the goal of mysticism. However, he was permitted to perceive it by virtue of his great intellectual clarity, and he was conscious in an extraordinary way of the mysterious possibilities that exist in the human soul. In him, physical and spiritual mystics may be said to have met and joined hands; for the alchemists were truly mystics. They sought to improve an individual substance by applying it to a universal substance, and this—in relation to a different subject—was likewise the goal of the spiritual adepts. In the enlightened philosopher of Hohenheim, this dual aspiration may not have reached its peak, but it did achieve union. Paracelsus is significant in the history of philosophy not for what he personally achieved, but for the intersection he represents. This intersection materially helps in realizing the unified nature of Hermetic work in all its fields of development. He is the signpost that indicates the location of the center of Christian mysticism. That is undoubtedly Germany, which stands philosophically supreme among all European nations and is the source of enlightenment and leadership for all forms of speculative thought, whether high or low. It is not there, needless to say, that we should seek the fundamental elements of that aspirational Science of the Life Within which, throughout the centuries, has spiritualized the Church of Christ. It has been truly said that the appearance of mysticism in the West coincided with the exhaustion of Greek and Jewish civilizations. But from this, one should not conclude that these aspirations are confined to periods of decline, or that they are in themselves a sign of weakness and religious disintegration in any disgraceful sense. The anxieties and desires of the soul are awakened in those periods of sorrow when the landmarks of spiritual life are temporarily erased. Out of the great fermentation that follows the work of a powerful dissolving force, a new religious order is being slowly called forth. The prevalence of mysticism during such periods is a sign of strength and vitality; it shows that the religious instinct

The foundation of Christian mysticism lies in the Neo-Platonic, or Alexandrian, quest for the ecstatic intuition of the Ineffable and Substantial All. It is likely derived through Philo, whose God—the ultimate goal—is defined in terms of "absolute transcendency." On the authority of the latest edition of the *Encyclopædia Britannica*, it may be stated that Neo-Platonism appeared during the first half of the third century. The influence of Philo was probably strong in the early Church. Josephus—the flexible and courtly Israelite, so strongly colored by the worldly elements of the Paganism surrounding him—was historically useful as an ally because he represented, for better or worse, the historical reality of the central figure of the new and more divine dispensation; but Philo, among all the Jews, most nearly approached the philosophic side of Christianity. Yet his influence was destined to be absorbed by that of the so-called Dionysius the Areopagite, who, at the end of the fifth century, incorporated Platonic theosophy with Christian dogma and developed his scale of the universe in harmony with the Hermetic doctrine of successive emanations. This scheme is essentially mystical and is a methodical application of Platonic and Hermetic concepts to the Christian realization of heaven and the angelic intelligences which inhabit the superior spheres. His transcendental tradition passed to Scotland and profoundly influenced the extraordinary mind of his translator, Erigena, whose lost works are a distinct and irretrievable loss to the broader school of historical Christian theology. The golden chain of speculation and the philosophy of transcendental desire were continued by Saint Bernard and his contemporary Saint Victor, who founded a school of thought in which the enlightened Bonaventura was a promi-

Medieval societies of mysticism sprang up by degrees in Germany, both for men and women, who devoted themselves to the cultivation of the interior life, and whose attributed writings were not without effect upon the powerful mind of Luther, despite its inherent coarseness. Along with these Christian schools, it is probable on the whole—though the evidence is indirect—that secret fraternities perpetuated the doctrines of Gnosticism and the illuminism of the Pagan religions for many centuries after their supposed disappearance, and thus originated the colleges of magic in conjunction with Jewish Kabbalism, which featured so largely in the practical mysticism of the period. Men like Eckhart and Tauler were the product of many elements, and, in a sense, all of them lead us toward Paracelsus as a central point, though the spiritual powers of Paracelsus—whatever they may have been—seem to have been possessed as a gift, and were not traceably the result of any distinct personal advancement in the spiritual life. It was chiefly through translations of this hierophant and of Cornelius Agrippa that mysticism was implanted in England after the searching fires of the Reformation, and their influence may be traced from the period of Thomas Vaughan <note>Vaughan deserves more than a passing notice. In our own day, the philosophy of Emerson and Carlyle was a type of modified mysticism, secularized for the first time; meanwhile, at this very moment, along with Spiritualism and Theosophy, there has occurred that "second spring" of transcendental aspiration which has been both the source and the justification for this book, and in which, with all humility, we desire to be included as discerning but earnest followers.

We cannot sufficiently warn our readers that this is a brief and meager sketch, but it is enough to indicate that there has been a tradition of the interior life perpetuated through all the ages of Christendom. By it, there is proclaimed the possibility of passing into a state which transcends normal consciousness and constitutes a partial union with a higher principle of being than that with which we are normally acquainted. This union was approached in the ecstasies of the Catholic saints, but it is not confined to the disciples of any special creed or dogma. Those who are in search of their souls must devote themselves to the study of the men called mystics. The most that can be offered by the phenomena of magic—however wonderful—and of spiritualism in its most exalted forms, is a factual guarantee that this search, if properly conducted, is no foolish quest. They afford a fundamental source of conviction to warrant the pursuit of that "grand experiment" with our own selves which the mystics claim to have conducted to a successful conclusion. In their large and suggestive literature will be found the way and the life which are intended to reach that end, but the student must remember the Hermetic warning to reject all that is superfluous. Furthermore, the aspirational flights of the mystics—and, as we are tempted to add, their achievements—were weighted and limited by all that was unworthy of Divinity,

# THE ROSICRUCIANS.

The subject of the Rosicrucian Fraternity has long been interesting because of the impenetrable mystery surrounding it, but that mystery is not its only interest. No student of occultism can afford to ignore Rosicrucian claims, which are an integral part of the great and universal claims of the transcendental sciences. While the only facts that can be determined about this arcane Fraternity have done much to dispel the romantic associations connected with the name Rosicrucian, that name—and not without reason—still casts a spell over people with imaginative temperaments and continues to transform facts that are otherwise ordinary. Although the name Rosicrucian originated among mystics during a specific historical period and has a defined scope, its original meaning is often discarded; it is treated as equivalent to <term>Magus</term>, <term>Man of Wisdom</term>, and other general terms, and is considered an appropriate title for any true mystic, whether or not they are affiliated with the Brotherhood that created the name. Used this way as a fanciful or decorative title, or as an honorary rank that is merely pleasant-sounding and pretty, it is certainly meaningless and childish; yet it is dear to some modern mystics who, more than the rest of humanity, are dominated by the pleasing tyranny of superficial words.

Now, at the start of a historical inquiry—however brief and imperfect—into the origin of <term>Rosicrucianism</term>, it is absolutely necessary to set aside all preconceived theories, the glamorous additions of romantic storytellers, and the fables of exaggerated historians, and to consider the Rosicrucian problem, as all historical problems should be considered, in the light

Was there really and truly, at any point in the past, a genuine Rosicrucian Fraternity? If so, what is its history, what powers did it possess, and what has become of it? When we hear of Rosicrucian associations today, should we consider them affiliated with the mysterious order of the past, or are they simply unimportant gatherings of a semi-antiquarian nature, just as much in the dark as we are about the secret history and unacknowledged goals of the Brotherhood whose name they have taken? These are the primary questions concerning the Rosicrucian mystery.

Now, whether the laudable and honorable Fraternity of <term>R. C.</term> had a real or an imaginary existence, the origin of its name is a point of considerable importance and sheds some light on the uncertainty surrounding the subject. It is not derived from the name of its supposed founder, <term>Christian Rosenkreuze</term>, because the recorded history of the founder is that of a legendary person and is therefore worthless from a linguistic perspective. <note>A red bracket in the left margin marks the following section from "We believe it is" to "gold or of ebony."</note> We believe it is not derived from the Latin words *Ros* (dew) and *Crux* (cross), although this explanation was adopted by the historian <term>Mosheim</term> and has been supported by his great reputation. It was based on the assumption that the founders of Rosicrucianism, along with numerous alchemists, considered the "dew of the philosophers" to be the most powerful solvent for gold, while the cross was, in chemical symbolism, equivalent to light—another important factor in performing the <term>Magnum Opus</term>—because the shape of a + was thought to show the three letters that make up the word *Lux*, or light. We believe that the name Rosicrucian is derived from *Rosa* (a Rose) and *Crux* (Cross). Based on this opinion, the general symbol of the supposed Order was a Rose crucified in the center of a Cross, usually made of gold or ebony. But

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the Rosicrucian symbols and history, specifically examining the red cross-marked heart emblem and its connection to Martin Luther, before introducing the 1614 publication of the Fama Fraternitatis in Germany.</meta>

# THE ROSICRUCIANS.

There is a variation of this emblem which has long been practically unknown. Yet if Rosicrucians ever existed, it was their true and arcane badge. This was a red, cross-marked heart in the center of an open Rose, which appears more than once in an old Rosicrucian book of the last century. It has no connection with the sublime symbolism of the Oriental world; Egypt, Thebes, <term>Eleusinia</term>, and the sanctuaries of ancient initiation are ignorant <margin>208</margin> of its meaning. It is a development of the monogram of the monk Martin Luther, which was a cross-crowned heart rising from the center of an open Rose. We think that this is a point of importance and the connecting link in a long chain of evidence which has been formed by our independent research.

Separately taken, the Rose and the Cross are sacred symbols of the highest antiquity. Their union is modern—that is, it occurred after the Renaissance and the Reformation. So far as history is concerned, the name Rosicrucian was unknown before the year 1598. The history of Rosicrucianism proper begins in Germany, where, in the city of Cassel, in the year 1614, the world of alchemists, <term>Paracelsians</term>, <term>Theosophists</term>, and professors of magic and mysticism was startled by the publication of a pamphlet of unassuming appearance but massive importance, which bore the title, "*The Fame of the Fraternity of the Meritorious Order of the Rosy Cross*, addressed to the learned in general and the Governors of Europe." It embodied the universal proclamation of certain anonymous men, endowed with great wisdom and raised up by God to renew and perfect all arts—to instruct mankind in its own worth and nobility, and to reveal the true and occult nature of the <term>Microcosmos</term>. It informed the unrefined world of quarrelsome scholars that by combining their forces, they might establish a synthesis of science, the true <term>Librum Naturæ</term>, the Perfect Method of all the arts. But they were fostering squabbles and divisions, were loath to leave their old ways, and clung blindly to the traditions of Aristotle and Galen, and the outdated authorities of <margin><unclear>al / net / your</unclear></margin> the ancient world. Yet never since the beginning of Christendom had the wise and all-merciful God so poured

The movement gained prominence in 1614 with the publication of the "Fama Fraternitatis," which called for a universal reformation of science and the arts.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introduction to the Rosicrucian *Fama Fraternitatis*, describing the spiritual and scientific revolution it promised and beginning the legendary history of its founder, C.R.C. (Christian Rosencreutz).</meta>

<meta>Red ink markings: vertical brackets in left margin, a vertical line in right margin, and underlinings of specific names and terms.</meta>

...to pour out upon mankind the sublime knowledge of His Son, Jesus Christ, and of the sacred secrets of Nature. A reformation had taken place in religion, and the sanctuaries of the Church had been cleansed; now, a grand and radical reformation was being prepared in science and philosophy. "Europe is pregnant," cried the *Fama*. "It will bring forth a strong child who will need a great baptismal gift." They, the highly-enlightened Brotherhood, enthroned on the pinnacles of initiation by a godly and highly-enlightened father, were the pioneers of this glorious revolution. Heirs of the ages of occultism, children of light and blessing, initiated into the mysteries of the Grand Orient, they were exalted in the midst of the obscurity—a miraculous and magical star to light and lead the age.

And this was their lineage and history.

The founder and original member of their Fraternity was an illustrious and immortal Teuton, anointed with the most choice oil of the supreme, magical <term>hierophant</term>, who was at that time a blessed participant in the uncreated light of God and in the everlasting triumph of their King, Christ. At five years of age, C. R. C., the offspring of the kisses of angels and beautified by the blessing of the stars, was placed in a cloister where he learned Greek and Latin; at the age of fifteen, he was associated with one Brother P. A. L., who intended to travel to the Holy Land. Now, this brother died at Cyprus, but the angelic boy, fired with heroic resolve and protected by angelic hierarchies, pressed on to Damascus, intending to proceed to Jerusalem. At Damascus, he heard news of an enlightened circle of <term>theosophists</term> who lived in an unknown city of Arabia called Damcar, of the wonders which they performed there, and of the mysteries of Nature which were revealed to them. The vision of the Holy Sepulcher faded from the boy's mind; he burned with the noble ambition to become acquainted with these marvelous men, and he bargained with certain Arabs to carry him for a sum of money to Damcar. He reached that unknown city when he was sixteen years of age, and there he was cordially welcomed

He was a pure, virginal boy; they took him at once into their confidence and instructed him daily in the mysteries of occult mathematics and occult physics. He easily attained proficiency in the Arabic tongue and completed the translation of the divine <term>Book M</term> into good Latin. At the end of three years, he departed from Damcar, sailed across the *Sinus Arabicus* into Egypt, and from there sailed to Fez, where his magical instructors had directed him. There he performed evocations of the elementary spirits under the tuition of new masters and became familiar with the secrets of the Invisible World. He stayed for two years in the city of Fez and completed the period of his initiation; when, enriched by the possession of many costly secrets, he proceeded to Spain to confer with its best scholars and convince them of the errors in their arts. He recounted the results of his travels and prescribed for them a series of axioms for the foundation of a universal natural science. But the enthusiasm of the young initiate was simply a laughing matter to the fossilized scholars of Spain. Had not the devil himself instructed them in the principles and practices of the <term>Black Art</term> at the renowned university of Salamanca?

said they. He retired in just indignation, but the same treatment awaited him among several nations. The true and infallible <term>Axiomata</term>, derived from all faculties, sciences, and arts—the "concentrated center of Nature"—were generally disregarded, and he returned disappointed to Germany, where he ruminated in solitude and seclusion on his travels and his universal philosophy. The latter was set down in writing, and he also constructed several fine instruments through the power of <term>natural magic</term>.

No; a society should be established to secretly instruct the elect in the profound mysteries of Nature and in the resources of Art, its handmaid. “In this way,” says the *Fama*, “began the Fraternity R. C.—first with only four persons,” who invented a magical language, a cipher writing equally magical, and a large dictionary full of sublime wisdom. They caused a House of the Holy Spirit to be built, healed the sick, initiated further members, and then, under orders from the founder, C. R. C., departed as missionaries into several countries to spread their wisdom in secret. Many years passed away, and at length their loving Father C. R. C., having finished his labors and his pilgrimage, gave up his initiated spirit to God in the odor of sanctity. The secret of his tomb was concealed for the space of one hundred and twenty years; the earliest members of the Fraternity died; it is doubtful whether those who succeeded them had attained as much wisdom, but when the fullness of time had come, a great and profitable discovery was made by the adepts of the third generation. This was the mystic tombstone of their illustrious founder, C. R. C., the lost treasure of the Society, and the repository of the most hidden mysteries. It was discovered by chance during the repair of a secret dwelling of the Rosicrucians, and was, in fact, a seven-sided vault, illuminated by the concealed Sun of the Magi and inscribed with mystic characters. The corpse of their “careful and wise Father” was found perfectly preserved, along with the <term>minutum mundum</term>, and a select variety of exceedingly high-class marvels. These operated so strongly on the enthusiasm of the existing members that they decided to share the unexpected increase in their possessions, their powers, and their knowledge among all worthy persons who might apply to them for initiation.

# THE ROSICRUCIANS.

number of suitable and like-minded persons to join their Fraternity. The writers, however, declined at first to provide their names and addresses; they were testing the climate of the times. Those who wished to communicate could do so by publishing printed letters, which would not escape the notice of the Brethren. Furthermore, having stated their absolute faith in the reformed Church of Jesus Christ, they solemnly and categorically denounced the countless deceptions of <term>pseudo-alchemists</term>, who claimed to possess a thousand secrets superior to the art of making gold. They also warned the wicked, the dishonest, and those greedy for worldly wealth that they would never find the Fraternity, and that their House of the Holy Spirit—though it had been seen by a hundred thousand people—would always be hidden from the world.

This was the first proclamation of the Rosicrucian Society, and it is not surprising that, during the peak of alchemy, astrology, and magic, it caused unprecedented excitement. Countless letters were printed throughout Germany by people seeking initiation. Dozens of pamphlets were published criticizing, attacking, or defending the Society and its manifesto. The legendary history of C. R. C. was full of obvious discrepancies: there was no city named Damcar; there was no House of the Holy Spirit in existence; a building seen by a hundred thousand people could not be hidden from the world; a fifteen-year-old boy could hardly have astonished the Turks with great medical skill; and the very existence of a boy mystic must have seemed somewhat extraordinary even for that era. Yet the story was generally believed. Twelve months passed, and then, in the same modest pamphlet form and anonymous style, the "*Confession of the Rosicrucian Fraternity, addressed to the Learned in Europe*" appeared in 1615. It still insisted on the need for a reform in philosophy and still offered initiation through gradual

stages to deserving applicants; but, for the rest, its energies were spent on scurrilous abuse of the Pope. It charged him with blasphemy, publicly cursed him, and looked forward with intense eagerness to the time when his “asinine braying” should be put an end to by the strange process of tearing him to pieces with nails.

Fresh excitement, renewed confusion, and further controversy followed; and again twelve months passed away. In the year 1616, the “*Chymical Nuptials of Christian Rosencreutz*” made known for the first time the full name of the supposed founder of the Order. This is a wonderful allegorical romance, which relates in an autobiographical form how an artist, a prepared student, was present in the year 1459 at the completion of the *Magnum Opus*. The *Fama Fraternitatis* embodied the history of a divine boy who obtained initiation in his teens. The “Chymical Marriage” recounts the initiation of an old man who had devoted the whole of his life to a search after the Grand Secret. The history of C. R. C. is not, therefore, the history of Christian Rosencreutz.

After the publication of this tedious but meritorious romance, a new element was introduced into the controversy. People had sought initiation and had failed. Their feelings were naturally hurt, and they adopted a hostile tone. Alchemists and theosophists compared notes; if they had failed, who deserved to be successful? Had anyone succeeded? No; the Society was cheating them. Such was the general decision; the tide turned, the craze ceased, and by 1620 the subject, exhausted to death, relapsed into its original obscurity for a considerable period of years.

The authorship and origin of the Rosicrucian manifestoes have occupied the attention of numerous learned investigators. It is impossible in the space of this brief essay to do justice to the theories which have been proposed. After an adequate consideration of the evidence, we are forced to conclude that none of their views are sustainable, while that one in particular which has been most widely accepted is open to the most

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the origins and authenticity of the Rosicrucian Fraternity, arguing against the theory that the movement was merely a joke or a hoax.</meta>

# THE ROSICRUCIANS.

<note>Pencil brackets in the left margin highlight the first and second paragraphs of the text.</note>

serious, and indeed to numerous objections. We refer to the theory that credits the theologian Andreae with the production of the Rosicrucian manifestos as a kind of elaborate joke.

We have briefly traced the origin of Rosicrucianism as far as its public history is concerned. On the one hand, the narrative contained in the *Fama Fraternitatis* is about a mythical person; on the other, all the manifestos bear the undeniable marks of a deeply serious purpose—that purpose being a reformation of the principles of philosophy and the methods of scientific research. It is, therefore, absurd to consider them a planned hoax. On the contrary, it is reasonable to assume that they were the work of a man, or of men, who hoped to find collaborators by publishing these works for a cause close to their hearts. It is not unbelievable that they originated from a secret sect of theosophists: the existence of such an association was almost inevitable during that age of alchemy. So, in answer to the first question—Was there ever at any period a genuine Rosicrucian Society?—it may be affirmed that there probably was. There is fair circumstantial evidence to show that some corporate body of this kind did exist, and may have published the manifestos called Rosicrucian. But the documents that are the basis of this belief also give evidence that the association did not originate in the way it claimed, while its subsequent history clearly shows it lacked the powers it claimed. Its prophecies have not been fulfilled; its opinions have not been substantiated; its anti-papal hysteria has fallen into utter contempt; and it has never in any way contributed to the reformation or improvement of philosophy. As far as we can tell, it became an alchemical Order; whether it succeeded in performing the transmutation of metals, there is no evidence to decide.

The true origin of the Rosicrucian Fraternity has been a source of constant speculation. Romance, which credited its adepts with all the superhuman attributes ever imagined by alchemists, Kabbalists,

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the origins of the Rosicrucian Fraternity, specifically addressing and dismissing claims of their ancient lineage while linking their intellectual and organizational roots to Paracelsus and the mystic Simon Studion.</meta>

## THE OCCULT SCIENCES.

and retailer of magical traditions, has been working on this problem and has interpreted the mystery in various ways. People of excessive credulity who are inclined toward the grandest views have represented it as an Order of preternatural antiquity, and its principles as the origin of every theological system. They are seen in solar mythologies and in phallic worship; their symbolism has permeated both the East and the West; it exists in Hindustan today just as it did in Egypt, Greece, and Scandinavia at various times in the past. There is not a trace of true evidence for all this romantic extravagance, but it has gained some believers because it is grand, wondrous, and incredible.

The religious, scientific, and philosophical opinions found in Rosicrucian manifestos are the foundation of the most acceptable hypothesis concerning them. These opinions are essentially modern. In magico-scientific matters, they were followers of Paracelsus. This statement is based not merely on the fact that, like that medieval hierophant, they believed in alchemy, astrology, and the occult forces of nature, but on the similarity of their alchemical, astrological, and occult theorems to those proposed by Paracelsus, who was essentially an innovator in such matters. In religious matters, they were equally modern. They were theosophical disciples of the great Lutheran reformer; we believe their chief symbol, and indirectly their name, were borrowed from his own allegorical seal.

The actual date of their origin is to be found in the grand old town of Nuremberg, which in 1598 was the home of a unique mystic and alchemist named Simon Studion. This individual, whose personality is shrouded in mystery, established a *Militia Crucifera Evangelica*, which held periodic meetings in Nuremberg. Its proceedings are reported in an unpublished work by Studion, and it can be shown that its opinions and objectives were substantially identical to those of the later Rosicrucian Society. Both associations were ultra-Protestant; both believed in the inner meaning and mystical interpretation of Scripture; both expected the end of the age and the

<summary>This page argues that Rosicrucianism is a modern movement rather than an ancient one, tracing its intellectual roots to Paracelsus and its organizational origins to Simon Studion's Militia Crucifera Evangelica in 1598.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument that the Rosicrucian Fraternity was not an ancient order, but rather a development of the late 16th-century sect known as the Militia Crucifera Evangelica, founded by Simon Studion in Nuremberg.</meta>

second coming of Christ; both abhorred the Pope and called him the Antichrist; both coupled him with the detested name of Mahomet; both studied the secret characters of nature; and both appealed to the appearance of new stars in the sky as portents of future events.” Finally, the founder of the *Militia Crucifera Evangelica* was a self-declared alchemist, and he was the first to develop the symbolism of the Rose-Cross. Clearly, the Rosicrucian Society of 1614 was a transformation or development of the obscure sect established by Simon Studion.

This view receives unexpected confirmation in a work from the eighteenth century. After nearly a hundred years of silence, the Rosicrucians reappeared in Germany. In 1710, a writer calling himself *Sincerus Renatus*—whose real name was *Sigmund Richter*—published "A Perfect and True Preparation of the Philosophical Stone, according to the Secret Methods of the Brotherhoods of the Golden and Rosy Cross." To this treatise, which is highly intriguing in its own right, were added the “Rules of the Rosicrucian Fraternity for the Initiation of New Members.” These rules, which essentially serve as the laws of the Order, not only prove the society’s existence during the period in question, but also establish the important fact that it still held its meetings in Nuremberg, where the *Militia Crucifera Evangelica* was originally founded by Studion.

In 1785, the publication of the “Secret Symbols of the Rosicrucians of the Sixteenth and Seventeenth Centuries” took place at Altona, showing that the mysterious brotherhood was still active. This was their final manifesto; it established the true nature of the Rose-Cross symbolism and appropriately concluded the records of the Fraternity.

Several important points have now been confirmed. The existence of a secret society in or before the year 1614 has been shown to be necessary to explain the successive publication of the numerous Rosicrucian manifestos; it has been traced to a rational origin; the merit of its claims has been evaluated, and

As early as the year 1618, Henricus Neuhusius testified in a little-known Latin pamphlet that the “high Rosicrucian adepts” migrated to India; and it is claimed today that they inhabit the plateaus of Thibet. Their supposed Oriental pilgrimage may be traced as far east as the Island of Mauritius. The recent dispersal of a valuable private library has revealed a very curious and seemingly genuine manuscript, which relates how the Comte de Chazal, having completed the performance called the *magnum opus*, or great work, proceeded to initiate Dr. Sigismund Bacstrom into the mysteries of the Rose Cross Order, requiring many extraordinary conditions and many solemn promises. This remarkable event took place on September 12th, 1794, on the Isle of Mauritius, in the District of Pampelavuso. It is the last known act of any member of the society, real or supposed. It remains to be stated, based on personal research, that the modern Rosicrucian Fraternities which are frequently heard of—both in England and America—such as the Rosicrucian Society of England (which has branches in several of our largest towns and a Metropolitan London College) and the Rosicrucian Society of Boston, etc., have no connection other than the names they have adopted with the original association, and they are devoid of mystical tradition. For all that is known to the contrary, the genuine Order of Rosicrucians may still exist, but its members keep the secret of their initiation well.

This theory identifies them with the builders of Stonehenge and Carnac, and perceives in their philosophy the historical basis of all the religions of the world. It has gained a considerable following among uncritical people; but his opinions, whether considered on their own or in the manner of their presentation,

# THE FREEMASONS.

The existing connection of Masonry with the science of the mystics is now confined to its emblems, but the fraternity originated in magic, and among alchemists and magicians. Here it is necessary to distinguish. <gloss>Underlined in red ink:</gloss> There are two branches of Masonry as there are two of alchemy. <gloss>Underlined in red ink:</gloss> There is the ancient Craft Masonry which is unconnected with occultism, and there is the more <gloss>Underlined in red ink:</gloss> modern symbolic form which absorbed the operative degrees, and it is that which originated in mysticism. The present constitution of the brotherhood as an association for the diffusion of benevolence, of the principles of humanity, and the development of moral perfection, naturally offers a suitable field for the labors of the modern mystic. <margin>Handwritten pencil bracket in right margin</margin> The foundation of all transcendental philosophy is the doctrine of interior regeneration, and its end is the Perfect Man. This also is the foundation, and such is the end, of Masonry. In considering the institution, we must have regard to its true principles. Undeniably these have been obscured in the passage of time; it has been corrupted by worldly wealth and magnificence; it has turned its eyes away from its objects; the transcendental eucharist has been abandoned for the banquet, and the pomp of power has out-glittered the spiritual splendor of the great reconstructive work; but the principles are there, and <gloss>Underlined in red ink:</gloss> let us hope that within the ranks of the brotherhood—or outside of them, if not within—it will be possible to inspire them with new life.

The present paper will necessarily be of a brief character, and is intended merely to indicate the line of thought which the student would do well to follow in his researches into the mysteries of the <gloss>Underlined in red ink:</gloss> symbolic architects. <meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between mysticism and Freemasonry, warning the seeker that while Masonry is surrounded by an attractive romantic aura, it currently lacks the deeper spiritual light often sought by students of the soul.</meta>

### THE FREEMASONS.

a many-splendored halo of romance and esoteric fable, which is very likely to attract the student of the soul at the threshold of mystic research, and to tempt him to seek within the inner sanctuary of this unique institution a certain guiding light, until the day dawns and the morning star rises from on high within his mind. We must advise him to overcome this pull of his desires toward Masonry. There is no light there; there is no secret of the soul enshrined in the depths of its suggestive ceremony; whatever it may have been in the past, at the present day it neither is, nor claims to be, more than “a beautiful system of morality veiled in allegories and illustrated by symbols.” It is true that the allegories and the symbols allow for a profound interpretation, but of its nature Masons, as a body, have no understanding. If anyone desires to be affiliated with the Fraternity so as to participate in the good work of a benevolent society, to share in the privileges of an influential body, or because they are attracted by “the boast of heraldry” and the “pomp of power,” let them do so by all means; but not in the search for mystic truth, nor in the hope of discovering the secrets of the regeneration and reconstruction of humanity; for though these are ostensibly the objects of the association, it is clear that since the advent of symbolic Masonry, it has accomplished nothing of the kind, and no one is seriously concerned with the realization of such an idea. The current relationship of the mystic to Masonry should be that of an apostle toward a field which is suitable for his labors, and not that of an earnest seeker toward a source of light. Otherwise, for the student, it is simply wasted time to master the ritual and the mysteries of Freemasonry, or to concern himself in any way with the false hypotheses that often serve as a sketch of its early history. It is interesting only because of those aims which are so largely forgotten, though they are still written about, and because of the original connection it possessed with magic and alchemy—a repeated statement that brings us back to a former point.

These attempts are consistently limited to the following superficial generalizations:

> "Architecture could only have been preceded by agriculture, so it was in this science that the first efforts of human skill must have been tested, and man's first successes over nature achieved. The first architects, therefore, would have been philosophers; the knowledge gained individually would be shared with others in the same profession, and an association for the exchange of knowledge and self-improvement would naturally be formed. Initiation ceremonies would be adopted to inspire respect for the profession in outsiders. Such a society did exist in Egypt, and it is only there that we find the first traces of Freemasonry."

Once the logic of this reasoning is accepted, it is only a small step further to claim that the origin of Masonry does not just date back to the early ages of human history, but is actually identical with the first man. He was likely an architect in a primitive sense, and his basic experiences were passed down to his descendants. Needless to say, several naive authors have made this claim. Since there was no logical reason to stop at Adam, the claim has moved beyond the earthly world; because the universal Creator can be seen as the Architect of the Universe, some have forcefully declared Him to be the first Freemason. Thus, in the minds of romantic historians, the Order is logically seen as possessing a divine character.

->THE FREEMASONS.<-

Fortunately, such reasoning has passed away with the early stages of Masonic literature. Later and more serious historians are content to attribute it to Solomon and the architects whose exceptional genius created the eastern splendors of the first Temple; to the mysteries of Eleusis and Bacchus; to the mystical sect of the Jewish Essenes; to the Christian Gnostics; to the secret assemblies of pagans who maintained the forbidden worship of Greece and Rome after the death of the Emperor Julian, and very possibly continued their existence throughout the entire Dark Ages; to the orders of chivalry, and especially to that of the Templars; to the secret tribunal of Free Judges <note>Referring to the Vehmgericht, a system of secret courts in medieval Germany.</note>; to the medieval Kabbalists; and to many other sources which, because they are mysterious themselves, are thought sufficient to explain the origin of an association which is supposed to have existed in all ages and in all nations, yet has practically no recorded history.

It is impossible in a brief space to do justice to these opinions, which are all very curious, very interesting, and an essential part of the romanticizing of history. But, as far as possible in the words of Masonic writers, we shall cite what has been argued in support of the most important of these theories.

The Reverend George Oliver claims to have most carefully and impartially weighed an enormous mass of evidence, collected from all quarters of the historical horizon, on the question of the alleged connection that exists between Masonry and the Ancient Mysteries. He is personally of the opinion that the latter were simply an idolatrous corruption of the sacred, ancient principles embodied in the great fraternal order, and that Masonry was the source of initiation.

> "The rites of that science which is now known by the name of Freemasonry were practiced in the antediluvian <note>The period before the biblical Great Flood.</note> world, revived by Noah after the Flood, practiced by mankind at the building of Babel . . . , and at the dispersion spread into every settlement . . . , and shaped into a form, the major outlines of which can be clearly traced in the mysteries of every pagan nation, showing the broken remains of one true system from which they were

# THE OCCULT SCIENCES.

derived." But the analogies—real or supposed—are independent of personal opinions, and it is with those alone that we are concerned.

The research of various writers seems to have established the fact that all ancient mysteries of which we have any trace, whether in Egypt, India, Greece, or Rome, were fundamentally identical, though formally different. Those of Egypt, says Oliver, contained all the secrets of their religion and politics, and inspired dread and terror throughout the world. By the uninitiated, they were regarded as vehicles of knowledge more than human, and their dispensers were reputed to possess some high and peculiar attributes of divinity. When Greek philosophy began to prevail, the mysteries were applied by wise men to a particular investigation of the nature and attributes of the Deity. After Pythagoras had remodeled them, and different sects of philosophers began to entertain new and irreconcilable opinions on the subject of research, the same speculations were continued with untiring eagerness. They had very indistinct notions of the true God, and while holding gross and sensual ideas of His nature, it is no wonder that vice became deified, and that the commission of every unnatural lust was not only permitted but even made a test of reverence to the deity. Purity fled from these institutions as they declined from the true worship of God. All, however, preserved a disguised tradition of the creation and fall of man, as well as of the Great Flood; their most significant emblems were the theological ladder, the triple support of the Universal Lodge—called by Masons Wisdom, Strength, and Beauty; the Point within a Circle, and many other undoubted emblems of Masonry. They used the same form of government which is now common to the whole Fraternity, the same system of secrecy, allegory, and symbolic instruction, all tending to the same point—the practice of moral virtue. No one was admitted without previous probation and initiation; candidates were bound by solemn oaths, united by invisible ties, taught by symbols, distinguished by signs and tokens, and impelled by a conscientious adherence to the rules of their Order.

## THE FREEMASONS.

They professed to practice the most rigid morality, justice toward men, and piety toward the gods.

Those who connect the first Temple of the Jews with Masonic architects do not lose sight of the Mysteries, though they consider them from another perspective. They affirm on historical grounds that the people of Attica went in search of better settlements a thousand years before Christ and settled in Asia Minor; the provinces they acquired were called Ionia. For a short time, these Asiatic colonies surpassed their mother country in prosperity and science. Sculpture in marble and the Doric and Ionic orders of architecture were the creations of their skill and experience. They returned to instruct their mother country, and the world is indebted to the Dionysian artificers for the noblest of the classical styles. The Dionysiæ were an association of scientific men who possessed the exclusive privilege of building temples, theaters, and other public buildings in Asia Minor. They existed under the same name in Syria, Persia, and India. They supplied Ionia and the surrounding countries as far as the Hellespont with theatrical equipment by contract, and built the magnificent temple at Icos <note>Likely referring to the city of Teos, famous for its Temple of Dionysus.</note> to Bacchus, the founder of their Order. The members of this association, which was intimately connected with the Dionysian mysteries, were distinguished from the uninitiated by the knowledge they possessed and by specific words and signs devised for the purpose of recognition. Like Freemasons, they were divided into lodges, which were distinguished by different names. They occasionally held social meetings in houses built and consecrated for this purpose, and each separate association was under the direction of a master and president, or wardens. They held a general meeting once a year, which was celebrated with great pomp and festivity, and where the brothers shared a splendid feast provided by the master, after sacrifices had been offered to the gods. They used specific tools in their ceremonial rituals, some of which are exactly similar to those still used by the Fraternity of Freemasons, and the wealthier artists were required to provide for the needs

The inscriptions they bear strongly express the gratitude of the Fraternity for the distinguished efforts of their departed superiors on behalf of the Order, for their generosity and benevolence to individual members, and for their private virtues, as well as for their conduct in public. If it is possible to prove the identity of any two societies, says Mr. Cross, an earnest American Mason, from the coincidence of their external forms, we are authorized to conclude that the Fraternity of Ionian architects and the Fraternity of Freemasons are exactly the same.

Now, it is maintained by the same writer that the Dionysiæ existed in Judea, while, on the authority of Josephus, the architectural style which they created was used in the construction of the Temple.

> "The vicinity of Jerusalem to Egypt, the connection of Solomon with the royal family of that land, the progress of the Egyptians in architectural science, their attachment to mysteries and hieroglyphical symbols, and their probable employment by the King of Israel, are together a considerable argument for the Syrian establishment of Masonry in ancient times."

It will be seen that the writers whom we have cited are by no means content with tracing the origin of Masonry only to the Mysteries or to Solomon. Depths beyond depths, heights above heights, vistas behind vistas, were discerned by their capacious intelligence until the prospect was lost in the hazy splendors of a primeval revelation—indefinite, yet dimly sensed. These reasoners are, on the whole, more acute than the limited visionaries who are content to refer Freemasonry to one mythical-historical source; and as the analogical value is fairly well balanced in either case, they are equally relevant for citation. Once it is accepted that the <term>Dionysiæ</term> lived in <term>Judea</term> and built the <term>Temple</term> planned by the wisest of men, it is important to know what became of them in later ages; fortunately, it can be shown to the complete satisfaction of the theorists that there did exist an association of men in <term>Palestine</term> who had striking similarities to the <term>Freemasons</term>, and that they were called the <term>Essenes</term>. When a candidate was proposed for admission to this society, his character was strictly scrutinized. If his life had been exemplary until then, and if he seemed capable of controlling his passions and regulating his conduct according to the strict principles of the Order, he was presented—at the end of his <term>novitiate</term>—with a white garment as a symbol of his consistent conduct and pure heart. A solemn oath was then administered to him: that he would never reveal the mysteries of his Order, that he would make no changes to its doctrine, and that he would continue in the honorable path of piety he had already begun. Like Freemasons, the <term>Essenes</term> taught their young members the knowledge they had inherited from their ancestors; they admitted no women into their society; they had special methods of recognition strongly resembling <term>Masonic</term> signs; and they had secluded colleges where they gathered to practice their rites and manage the society's affairs. After performing these duties, they assembled in a large hall where a meal was provided by the President or Master of the Cottage, who distributed a specific portion of food to every individual. All distinctions of rank were abolished; if any preference was ever shown, it was based on piety, generosity, and virtue. Treasurers were appointed in every town to provide for the needs of poor strangers. The <term>Essenes</term> claimed higher degrees of piety and knowledge than the uninitiated common people, and although their claims in this regard were significant, they were never questioned by their enemies. Though a strictness of manner was one of the chief characteristics of the initiated <term>Essenians</term>, they frequently assembled in con-

If we move to a later period, one of the most interesting hypotheses is that which endeavors to establish a connection between Masonry and Chivalry. However, in this case, we are fortunately not left to conjecture, for we are explicitly informed by a foreign author, who was well † acquainted with the customs and history of Syria, that the Knights Templar were actually members of the Syrian Fraternities.”

The diversity of all these views is a sufficient indicator of the uncertainty surrounding their subject, and it justifies the statement already made that the origin of Craft Masonry is historically untraceable—a fact which, however, is not in itself undeniable proof of immense antiquity. The *Encyclopædia Britannica* accounts for the similarities with the great secret institutions of the distant past through the doctrine of psychical identity, "one of the most important results of anthropological science." It considers the medieval building corporations to be the true historical precursors of Freemasonry, a view recommended by its simplicity, which contradicts no probabilities while adequately covering the facts. These corporations were originally grouped around the monasteries, and the previously cited authority affirms that in the twelfth century, "there are distinct traces of a general association acknowledging one set of craft laws, one set of secret signs and ceremonies, and, to a certain extent, one central authority at Strasbourg. <margin>A blue bracket appears in the left margin alongside the next paragraph section.</margin> Albertus Magnus is supposed to have introduced many of the Jewish and Arabian symbols which were adopted by the craft," while the initiation ceremony is said to have been copied from a Benedictine consecration. The architectural knowledge and lodge organization were imported from Germany into England, where the authentic documents of operative Masonry date back to the year 926 A.D. <margin>The marginal line continues downward.</margin> About two centuries later, the principles of the craft were imported into Scotland, and they are said to have continued there long after they had become extinct in continental kingdoms. "In this manner, Scotland became the center from which these principles again emerged to enlighten not only the nations on the continent, but every civilized part of the world."

It is impossible to attempt even a brief sketch of the progress of Craft Masonry in Great Britain here. The first signs of its transformation are found towards the close of the seventeenth century, when the celebrated alchemist Elias Ashmole was, for some unexplained reason, accepted as an ordinary member of the great trade guild. Under his influence, and also, it is said, under that of Lord Wortley Montagu, it rapidly developed into a purely symbolic institution; the operative craft disappeared, and its ostensible objects became purely of a moral and intellectual kind. In this form it spread rapidly, and about a century later took root in France, where it spread a great light and attracted universal attention at a period of social upheaval and restless research after every kind of novelty.

It is there and in Germany that symbolic Freemasonry became largely and almost inseparably identified with magic, alchemy, and the rest of the secret sciences. It is there that its ranks were recruited from the members of the mystic sects of the Martinists; it is there that the so-called adept Cagliostro established his Egyptian grades and revived the wisdom of the Magi; it is there that Baron Tschoudy instructed Masonic apprentices in the hidden doctrine of practical alchemy; it is there that the Masonic grade of Rose-Cross was developed from the symbolism of the Rosicrucians; it is there that the mysterious Misraim Rite was imported from the coast of the Adriatic, bearing Chaldean statutes, distributing symbolic and mystic degrees, conferring alchemical titles, and possessing veiled masters; it is there that the Masonic children of the Primeval Light pretended to accomplish the work of human reconstruction; it is there that the mysterious Count de Saint-Germain "traveled through the Masonic Lodges, dealing in immortality, and recounting his experiences in previous centuries"; it is there that the charitable and interesting *Loge de la Bienfaisance* shone at Lyons with a pure light of advanced mysticism; it is there that the doctrine of the Fraternity was adorned by the magnetic discoveries of the Austrian doctor Anton Mesmer; it is there, and in Berlin, that the allegories of the Order were interpreted in a transcendental sense. # PART IV.

## MESMERISM.

The student who has followed us this far in our account of the methods and processes of the mystics will be aware that the wonders of old magic were accomplished, in most cases, by <note>a pencil bracket begins in the right margin here</note> a certain suspension of the senses and by a powerful, artificial exaltation of the internal faculties. An elaborate, if unrefined, ceremonial surrounded the operator for the time being with a sort of new environment. Prayers, fasts, ritual washings, vigils, and strange incantations combined to elevate his intellectual nature into another plane of existence.<note>the pencil bracket in the right margin ends here</note> At times, the process may have failed its intended purpose, and the Magus may have been plunged into a chaos of hallucination. At other times, he emerged with cleansed and clarified perceptions into a higher "magnetic" atmosphere and created for himself a brief connection with transcendent intelligences in superior forms of existence. Clairvoyant, clairaudient, and clairsentient, he invoked spirits, and they responded to his will because he was "within their plane." Now, this supernatural condition, produced by the formulas of practical magic, was also attained—and apparently to an advanced degree—by <note>the following is underlined in pencil:</note>the contemplation and absorption of the purely internal mystics, who, in solitude and isolation, entered into the world of thought and searched within themselves for that instrument of connection which was to unite their better nature with the higher law and higher intelligence of the universe. In the monasteries of the Latin Church, the saintly men of Christendom appear to have achieved

<summary>The author explains that ancient magical wonders were achieved through sensory suspension and the exaltation of internal faculties, techniques similarly used by solitary mystics to reach higher states of consciousness. This "magnetic" state allowed practitioners to connect with transcendent intelligences or higher universal laws.</summary>

<keywords>Mesmerism, magic, mystics, Magus, magnetism, clairvoyance, Latin Church, spiritual exaltation, internal faculties</keywords>

the highest spiritual heights possible for man in physical form through the process of introspection. This process, however, is not limited to any specific age or religion. It was practiced in the Far East many centuries before the first words of the Christian Gospel resounded through barbaric Europe; and it is undoubtedly a more advanced branch of that unique condition which, after much skepticism and misunderstanding, has finally been recognized by modern science under the name of hypnotism. The hypnotic state is, however, only one of many series of phenomena that have long been familiar to scholars and students of that unexplored region of pathology termed mesmerism and animal magnetism.

Magic is older than history; it is older than the Records of the Past; it is older than the Book of the Dead and the hieroglyphic literature of ancient Egypt. Whether in Egypt or Chaldea, or in those highlands beyond the Himalayas which are the supposed cradle of the Aryan race, wherever magic flourished, it has always been intimately associated with rites and rituals intended to produce in the practitioner one or more magnetic states. Mesmerism—to use the older term that commemorates an illustrious, if not entirely noble, discoverer—was known to the ancients. Meanwhile, the trances and ecstasies included among its most common phenomena coincide with the entire history of mystical religious experience. These constitute, in fact, that state of internal rest which, according to Platonic philosophy, is the source of inspired visions, where "the light of eternity . . ., the light revealed to Zoroaster, and all the sages of the East," is revealed to the mind’s eye. It will also be obvious, even to the casual reader, that the trance artificially induced by magical ceremonies, by the vigils of the saints, by the meditation of the quietists, by the passes of the disciples of Mesmer, and by the hypnotic disc of the modern pathologists of France, is essentially connected to the natural trance of cataleptic patients. Now, the history of cataleptic conditions has an antiquity
