# MESMERISM.

which is so generally accepted as to be practically beyond dispute.

The power of the hand, the power of the word, and the power of the human eye are among the most prominent subjects in ancient symbolism. In a remarkable representation on a mummy case, preserved in the invaluable volumes of the famous Abbé Montfauçon, we find
> "a bed or table, on which lies a sick man, before whom stands a person clothed in a brown garment, with his eyes open. His face is turned toward the patient; his left hand is placed upon the breast, while the right is held over the head of the sick man, in the exact position of a magnetizer."

A French writer, in the "Annals of Animal Magnetism," seems to have been the first to draw attention to the mesmeric significance of this picture, and he mentions other mummies "where standing figures touch the feet, the head, the sides, or the thighs" of reclining patients. Elihu Rich, in a short cyclopedic account of magnetic healing, has assured us that these scenes are not unique.
> "Figures appear on the amulets or charms known as 'Abraxei,' all more or less showing an acquaintance with magnetism. The priest with the dog's head, or mask of Anubis, appears repeatedly, with his hands placed in various ways on the supposed patient. Some of these figures are provided by Montfauçon. In one of them, the masked figure places one hand on the feet and the other on the head of the patient; in a second, one hand is laid upon the stomach and the other on the head; in a third, the hands are on the loins; in a fourth, the hands are placed upon the thighs, and the operator's eyes are fixed on the patient's face."

These are facts that could be multiplied almost indefinitely if reference were extended to the medical marvels of the temple of Serapis at Memphis, where cures are alleged to have been performed by the hierophants <note>high priests</note>, partly by the laying on of hands and partly by inducing an artificial lethargy in the patient; or again, the thaumaturgic <note>miracle-working</note> rituals of initiation used by the Greek mystics at Thebes, Lemnos, and Samothrace.

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the historical origins of mesmerism and magnetism, transitioning from the practices of ancient Egypt and Greece to the revival of these concepts by Western mystics and Renaissance thinkers like Paracelsus.</meta>

# THE OCCULT SCIENCES.

The traditional secrets of magic that Greece acquired from Egypt and other regions of remote antiquity, and which she imparted in turn to the Latins, were combined with the Babylonian and Assyrian magic of the Jews. These were somehow preserved through the Christian centuries and inherited by Western mystics. The saints of the Catholic Church required no traditional secrets to initiate them into the transcendental possibilities of ecstasy, for they pursued independently—and, as it were, by a natural instinct—the path of hypnotic sense-suspension, and acquired a forced connection with the spiritual world through a life isolated from all but the most inevitable contact with earthly things. 

But the healing branch of that science—which at its highest is supposed to have made souls whole by connecting them with the source of the soul’s health, and at its lowest is supposed to have cured the diseases of man’s flesh—reappeared after a slumber of centuries. Or, at the very least, it was first openly mentioned by Paracelsus in the sixteenth century; and, as far as we can trace, it was he also who, from the analogies of the natural magnet, first appears to have elevated it to the status of a magnetic hypothesis.

“This remarkable thinker maintained,” says a recent French work of high medical authority, “that the human body was endowed with a double magnetism; that one part attracted the planets to itself and was nourished by them, from which came wisdom, thought, and the senses; that the other portion attracted the elements to itself and disintegrated them, from which came flesh and blood; that the attractive and hidden virtue of man resembles that of amber and the magnet; and that by this virtue, the magnetic power of healthy persons attracts the enfeebled magnetism of those who are sick. After Paracelsus, many learned men of the sixteenth and seventeenth centuries—Grocenius <note>Likely referring to Rudolph Goclenius, a 17th-century physician and proponent of magnetic healing.</note>, Burgrave, Helmont, Robert Fludd, Kircher, and Maxwell—believed that in the magnet they could recognize the properties of that universal principle by which minds prone to generalization thought all natural phenomena might be explained,” and the external mineral loadstone was

<summary>This page discusses how ancient magical traditions were preserved by Western mystics and how Paracelsus later introduced a "magnetic hypothesis" to explain human health and spiritual connection. It lists several 16th and 17th-century scholars who followed Paracelsus in viewing magnetism as a universal explanatory principle.</summary>
<keywords>magic, Paracelsus, magnetism, Western mystics, ecstasy, healing, Goclenius, Helmont, Robert Fludd, Kircher, loadstone</keywords>

...combined with the interior Paracelsian *magnes* for the alleviation of all disorders.

## MESMERISM.

It has been generally concluded that Mesmer profited from the magnetic speculations and experiments of his predecessors, and indeed, in his capacity as a physician, he could scarcely have ignored what was once so important a branch of empirical healing. Moreover, his explanatory theorems are positive proof that he was very largely indebted to the theosophical physics of the past. Even if it is evident from the monuments of remote antiquity that the most enlightened nations of the ancient world healed by the imposition of hands, by the willed transmission of “vitality,” and by the <term>passes</term> called magnetic, it is still more certain that the practitioners of the all-curing lodestone and the healing qualities of the hand and eye were completely unacquainted with this fact. Although the literature of mesmerism is exceedingly comprehensive and large, it does not inform us by what process of observation or reasoning the physician of Geneva was led to the secret of the old sanctuaries—or more accurately, the information it gives is insufficient to account for the fortunate guess which recovered a lost science for the benefit of the modern world.

Mesmer, a German by birth, was born in the year 1734, and he took his degree as a doctor of medicine at Vienna in 1766. He immediately preceded the Revolution, and he just outlived Waterloo. His doctrine was spread at a time when the pure mysticism of the Christian centuries seemed destined to perish with the reclusive philosopher Saint-Martin; speaking from a historical standpoint, his discovery was the one thing that saved mysticism from complete oblivion.

The connection between mysticism and mesmerism will naturally seem remote to the ordinary student of pathology, but experiments in dual consciousness, in the possibilities of suggestion, in the transference of thought, and in the production of a profound artificial sleep appear, after a careful study, to be only the outer fringe of an ancient secret knowledge by which the sanctuary of the inmost man might be unlocked, and whereby the

<summary>This page details the emergence of Franz Anton Mesmer's theories, noting his debt to ancient healing traditions and Paracelsian physics. It credits Mesmer with preserving the tradition of mysticism during the secular shifts of the late 18th and early 19th centuries.</summary>
<keywords>Mesmer, Franz Anton Mesmer, Mesmerism, Paracelsus, lodestone, Saint-Martin, mysticism, vitalism, history of medicine, animal magnetism</keywords>

an inner mystic might enter into the realities which transcend the superficial truths of the physical world. We speak tentatively and hypothetically, but we base our statement upon investigations other than our own, and upon authorities in occult science who are greater than ourselves. It is open to anyone who has the necessary qualifications of aspiration, patience, and energy to learn through their individual experience that mystic practice is not a hallucination, and that it is not a fraud. One need not go further—though it is true that one must proceed with care—than the modern phenomena of *Spiritualism* to find out that there is an intelligent world transcending the senses; and based on the evidence of all the mystics, we must conclude that it is possible to enter that world, and that the door to that entrance is *mesmerism*. By this, we must be understood to refer to the actual thing—whatever it may be—and not to the multitude of false conceptions which are attached to the name. As we are writing a handbook for seekers, it is once again necessary to observe that if anyone wishes to become acquainted with the quality of this evidence, he must be referred to the source, to the unwritten history of mysticism as it exists in the lives of the mystics. There, he will find in what manner the ecstatics of all the Christian centuries performed the internal *Itinerarium Mentis ad Deum* <note>Journey of the Mind to God</note>, and attained the blissful absorption of *Nirvana in Christ*. Then he must compare what he has learned with the facts of medical history regarding natural and artificial *catalepsy*; and if he can distinguish between disease which is rooted in organic weakness and the disruption of mind and body, between the plunging of an unprepared nature into a condition which is outside of nature, and between the sanctified introspection of a pure mind, a cleansed will, and an exalted aspiration and purpose, he will be in a position to understand what is essentially identical and what is incidentally different in the mystic and magnetic trance. The mystics will tell him that it is also open to him to participate in the *bona Dei in terra viventium* <note>good things of God in the land of the living</note>, but that if he wishes to share their privileges, he must also live their life.

We are conscious that we have been unwillingly speaking in somewhat enigmatical terms, and it will be well, therefore, if—avoiding all involved details—we can provide the student with a few lines of plain information on the goal that was sought by the mystics through their profound process of introspection and transcendental <term>self-hypnosis</term> for the suspension of sensory connections and the manifestation of the interior light and life. There was neither secret nor mystery about it; it was always openly referred to, precisely defined, and described. At the same time, the methods were necessarily of an arcane or, at least, obscure character, because they were concerned with the mystery of spiritual growth and that great psycho-alchemical reconstruction which has been emphasized by one term of many heights and wonders—the **New Birth**, or **Regeneration**. Here is what we read in a popular handbook of mysticism, which circulated widely in France in the middle of this century:

> “The union of God with the soul is the principle of all mystic life. But this union—the fullness and final consummation of which cannot be experienced until death has been passed through and eternity has been achieved—can be accomplished on this earth in a more or less perfect manner. The entire literature of mysticism has no other end than to unveil to us, by a full and profound analysis of the different stages of evolution in the spirit of man, the various successive degrees of this divine union. Seven distinct stages of the soul’s ascent towards God have been recognized by mystics, and they constitute what has been symbolically called the castle of the interior man. They represent the seven absolute processes of psychic transfiguration. The first link in this arcane sequence is called the state of prayer, which,

It has, however, a higher aspect, comprised in the second evolutionary process, and called the state of mental prayer. Here, the illusory phenomena of the visible world are regarded as being filled with an inner pneumatic significance; to discern this is a chief end of mysticism. In order to make progress in this and so attain the third stage, it is necessary that the aspirant—shaping all practical life in conformity with his theory—should perform no outward act except with a view to its inward meaning, all things which are of time and earth and man being simply figures and symbols of earth, heaven, and God.

The postulant, as he advances, will perceive that the inmost thoughts of his own conscious being are only a limited and individual reflection of the speech or Word of God, which is concealed even in its apparent revelation and is itself a veil over that divine truth which must be removed for the contemplation of the absolute truth behind it. When he has reached this point, the mystic will have entered on the third stage of his illumination. This is the most difficult of all. It is termed by mystics the "obscure night," and here it is necessary that the aspirant should become stark naked, should empty himself completely, and should be stripped of all his faculties, renouncing all his own preferences, his own thoughts, and his own will—in a word, his whole self.

Aridity, weariness, temptation, desolation, and darkness are characteristic of this period, and they have been experienced by all who have ever made any progress in the mysteries of mystical love. The fourth condition is named the prayer of quietism. Complete immolation of self and unreserved surrender into the hands of God have repose as their first result. Such quietism, however, is not to be confounded with insensibility, for it leads to the only real activity: to that which has God for its impulse. The fifth degree in the successive spiritualization of the human soul is called the state of union, in which the will of man and the will of God become sub-

On the extreme further limit of this condition, the mystic enters the sixth state, which is that of ecstatic prayer, which is the soul’s transport above and outside itself. It constitutes a union with divinity by the instrument of positive love, which is a state of sanctification, beatitude, and inexpressible torrents of delight flowing over the whole being. It is beyond description, it transcends illustration, and its happiness is not to be conceived. Love, which is a potency of the soul, or of that *anima* <note>the vital principle or soul</note> which vivifies our bodies, has passed into the spirit of the soul, into its superior, divine, and universal form; this completed process comprises the seventh and final stage of <term>pneumatic</term> <note>spiritual</note> development, which is that of ravishment. Renouncing all that is corporeal about it, the soul becomes a **pure spirit**, capable of being united, in a wholly celestial manner, to the Uncreated Spirit, whom it beholds, loves, serves, and adores above and beyond all created forms. And this is the mystic marriage, the perfect union, the entrance of God and Heaven into the interior man.”

The history of Christian supernaturalism informs us that in these seven stages of transcendental adoration the body of the mystic was seen to rise from the ground, and to poise itself mysteriously in space. Ravished by interior visions, he became insensible to all that was passing around him, and at the same time his physical senses, which had suspended correspondence for the moment with the normal exterior environment, were ministered to in a manner which we should term magical; he saw, heard, felt, and tasted, but on another plane of being; and occasionally his indescribable ecstasy was manifested in the apparition of lights and halos about him, and in the diffusion of an unearthly fragrance. ### THE OCCULT SCIENCES.

...as literally true, they surpass in degree what has been achieved by psychological experiment today, but in respect of their kind and class, they are the phenomena of ecstatic hypnotism.

We have sufficiently indicated the historical foundation for the antiquity of those classes of transcendental phenomena which are generally embraced by mesmerism, and we have gone beyond the limits of our purpose to establish a connection between them and the highest altitudes of mystic goals and actions. If it is impossible to do justice to the latter subject in an elementary treatise, it is equally beyond our scope to provide the student with an account of the various methods by which the hypnotic and mesmeric conditions can be induced in suitable subjects. Throughout, it has been our endeavor to make this handbook as practical as its design would permit, and in the ceremonies and ritual of occult science, we have given information at first hand from the most rare and authentic sources. In the present case, however, we are dealing with a matter which is almost common knowledge, which has a vast literature in nearly every form and language, while its methods and processes may be learned from any one of the innumerable pamphlet guides that can be obtained at a minimal cost. What is unnecessary due to its widespread availability may therefore be reasonably spared. On the other hand, in the elaborate courses of experiment which have been undertaken by investigators who have become authorities through experience, there is obviously more than can be included in a preliminary sketch. It only remains for us to categorize the several classes of phenomena which occur in the various phases of mesmerism, and to refer the student who desires to extend his knowledge beyond the scope of this paper to the authorities whom he will need to consult.

Both mesmerism and hypnotism, insofar as they can be distinguished from one another, may be divided broadly into two sections. The first and most important of these refers to <term>therapeutics</term> <note>the branch of medicine concerned with the treatment and curing of diseases</note>, and is, in fact, the development of the so-called magnetic condition for the cure of diseases in the subject, for the performance

### MESMERISM.

...of painless operations, and for the diagnosis of medical cases, when the stage of <term>clairvoyance</term> is reached. In a sufficiently susceptible subject, the passes and will of the mesmerist induce a condition of profound trance, which, before the discovery of anesthetics, was used by Dr. Elliotson in India with precisely the same success as anesthetics for the amputation of limbs and the removal of cancers; these were endured by the patient unconsciously and in the absence of any of those subsequent ill effects which not infrequently follow the use of chloroform and stupefying gases.

The curative effects of mesmerism are no less astonishing than its power in the suspension of consciousness during agonizing operations, nor do they by any means always depend upon the subject being in a trance. Passes made over affected portions of the body, sometimes the simple laying on of hands, and at other times that process which is now termed <term>Massage</term>—being a department of medical rubbing and fundamentally a mesmeric treatment—have produced wonders in healing which rest upon unimpeachable evidence, and listing them would fill volumes. It would be scarcely an exaggeration to say that almost every species of disease, other than hereditary ones, has been successfully eradicated by the methods which <term>transcendental therapeutics</term> have inherited from Anton Mesmer and from the psychologists who succeeded him. Painless childbirth, the cure of paralysis, and the restoration of sight and hearing are included in the long list. Yet the nature of the agent which is at work in these remarkable physiological reconstructions is entirely unknown. The hypothesis of an imponderable <term>magnetic fluid</term> projected by the will of the mesmerist is now very generally rejected; the influence of the imagination is insufficient to account for the facts. The transmission of a <term>vital fluid</term> would seem to have some truth to it, for the operations of curative mesmerism exhaust the vitality of the mesmerist, but this again is insufficient, for the cures of some healers have been performed on a scale so colossal as to be out of all proportion to the extent of their own vitality. The inadequacy of existing explanations has led many to have recourse to

An acquaintance with the methodical forms of mesmerism is by no means indispensable to the healer, and as a rule, the most extraordinary cures have been performed without any planned process. In the present century, the Prince of Hohenlohe and the Zouave Jacob performed cures that, in both nature and number, might well be called miraculous. In the last century, we have the authority of Prince Bartenstein, who witnessed on one occasion the complete restoration of four hundred people who were blind, deaf, mute, and paralyzed. The simple *attouchement* <note>touching</note>, which in the case of a royal hand was once considered potent enough to cure the most deep-rooted of diseases, "the king's evil" <note>scrofula</note>, appears to have been the single formula adopted by both of these phenomenal people. Meanwhile, the mere presence of Jean Baptiste Viannay, the saintly Curé D'Ars, during public services at the altar or in the private *séance* <note>session</note> of the confessional, is said to have been all that was needed to overcome a variety of complaints commonly called incurable.

Healing phenomena occurring in the presence of the holy are another phase of the grand mesmeric mystery, which is as ancient as the suspension of the senses, trances, and hypnotic visions. Miraculous healing, indeed, has always been considered a prerogative of saints and a test of a divine mission. Today it is still believed, practiced, and sought after by many who would scorn to degrade it by associating it with the name of Mesmer. These people, disregarding fundamental similarities, would deny that it has any connection with "animal magnetism," the transmission of vitality, or the existence of an <term>odic force</term>. By these individuals, it is commonly classified among the miraculous effects of prayer; and Faith-Healing, in one form or another, is notoriously a fact of the present time. It is practiced in many ways and is associated with many types of religious opinion. By some of its advocates, it is connected with certain dogmas, the belief in which is held to be indispensable; others would identify it with no creed or doctrine; for

In many cases, the belief in this abnormal power and personal experience of its workings have created currents of enthusiasm that have exceeded reasonable bounds, discrediting testimony that might otherwise have been more generally accepted and bringing disrepute upon movements that were just and good in their aspirations.

There is one form of psychic cure that its practitioners carefully distinguish from the medley of marvels that pass confusedly under the name of Faith-Healing. It is called Christian Science Healing, and it claims to have shaped the subject into a systematized spiritual gnosis. Its disciples may charge us with confusion for referring to it in a paper on Mesmerism, but we are treating matters in a broad and comprehensive manner that, at their deep center, are either identical or, at least, radically joined. Defects of terminology are a common difficulty for which patience should be shown, as psychological research is still in its infancy. Among many systems and claimants, we recognize in the principles and followers of this so-called Christian Science the most profound elements that have entered into the higher mysticism of all ages. Whatever results have been achieved—and we have heard personal testimony of real weight and value from cultured and thoughtful people—we recognize that Christian Science Healing is, to all appearance, on the path of the mystics. It follows the path of Vaughan, Böhme, Saint Martin, and the best of the Quietists; and it is our duty in this section to refer the student to its literature for the purest light on the obscure problems of the spiritual reconstruction of physical man, which is said to be performed through the mysterious powers of prayer and faith, whether or not it is identified by the name of Faith-Healing.*

<header>THE OCCULT SCIENCES.</header>

We have said that the cures attributed to mesmerism and its related fields are not to be explained by the power of imagination, regardless of the stimulus applied to it. This statement was not made in the interest of any hypothesis, but simply in view of the evidence. At the same time, imagination is a great curative agent, and accordingly, it has been the fashion for over half a century to explain all the phenomena of mesmeric therapeutics by that alone, or by that in conjunction with a theory of planned imposture when the former explanation would be obviously misapplied. Both methods are now practically abandoned. Scientists have discovered that, in addition to the phenomena of healing, there are other branches of mesmeric experiment which imagination would wholly fail to explain, and under the name of hypnotism, the whole subject is receiving gradual investigation. Scientific processes are like the mills of the gods; they grind slowly, but in the end, they grind exceedingly fine. The current generation usually does good work by exploding the hypotheses, if not the methods, of its predecessor. This very hypothesis of the imagination in its relation to curative mesmerism is beginning to be reduced to its proper proportions, and the power of the agent in question is now being limited to those diseases which are said to have been caused by imagination. The existence of real diseases which have originated in this manner is proved by the fact that imaginary or fictitious complaints can have no existence; and, positively speaking, by the secondary fact that complaints which have sometimes taken a genuine hold of a patient have demonstrably begun in his fancy acting on a nervous and abnormally susceptible temperament.

The second of the two sections into which the phenomena of mesmerism and hypnotism may be broadly divided includes all experiments which are not of a therapeutic character. Their variety is enormous, and their psychological significance as investigations into the undeveloped potencies of the mind is so great that it is difficult to attempt to express it without

# MESMERISM.

...using terms that might seem to be sensational. We are speaking of experiments that reveal to us the existence, deep down in our being, of faculties that may be called unknown, of senses other than those we normally use, and of apparently foreign personalities behind our legitimate manifested personality—which we have always considered our most inalienable part and as that which constitutes our *selves*.

The phenomena included in this section allow for further subdivision. There are those connected with simple muscular conditions that are the first results of the trance state, in whatever way it is produced. What is generally observed at the beginning is, in the words of a recent writer, a remarkable quivering of the eyelids, a “contraction and then a dilation of the pupil of the eye,” followed by a gradual closing of the eyelids and “by all the outward appearances of deep slumber. The arms fall listlessly by the side, the limbs are relaxed, and to anyone not in on the secret, the subject would appear to be sleeping heavily after some great and tiring exertion.” The nervous centers that control the action of the muscles are at the same time stimulated into abnormal excitability. “Even a gentle stroking of the skin is enough to produce the contraction of the underlying muscles; a continued irritation of the same spot will cause the contraction to extend to neighboring and distant groups of muscles. . . By employing appropriate stimuli, we are enabled to produce the continued contraction of any set of muscles we may select, and even to induce rigidity of the whole body.” This rigidity constitutes a second stage in physical entrancement. The senses are only partially suspended in ordinary, normal sleep. In the hypnotic or mesmeric state, the subject is unmoved by the strongest light directed straight at his eyes, or by a loud noise at his ear, and he passes into a condition that closely resembles catalepsy. Experiments connected with the artificial suspension of the senses in hypnotic states—just as in mesmeric states—are of a very interesting nature, but they are exclusively of an elementary kind. “In our

> waking moments we enter into relation with the outer world by means of the external senses; and the organic functions of the body are controlled by nerve centers, which are to a large extent subject to the higher faculties of sense. In this state our senses reveal to us the outer world as it is, and direct the functions of the body appropriately to the nature of the external agents by which they are impressed. But in the hypnotic sleep the functions of external and inner sense are perplexingly disordered. The hypnotized subject does not see what is before his eyes, or hear the sound in his ears; he sees and hears only the sights and sounds suggested to him by the magnetizer. And the lower functions, such as nutrition, which are dependent upon the perceptions of sense, are affected in like measure. This power of controlling the sense perceptions enables the operator to make the patient blind or deaf to the present sights and sounds, and to make him see and hear—and so of the other senses—what has no present existence outside the suggestions of the magnetizer. . . . This kind of hallucination has been called *positive*, to distinguish it from the former, to which the name *negative* has been assigned.”

What is suggested to the entranced sight of the subject must not, however, be identified with the normal figments of imagination. What is imposture on the part of the operator is a vivid actuality to the patient. The entranced senses are, moreover, the slaves of the hypnotist; what his methods have deadened, they can also quicken; he can arouse them into abnormal activity, and, if he so wills it, his own arbitrary motions may be imitated automatically by the subject with eyes gripped close in the vice of catalepsy.

The control of the external senses must obviously affect the intellectual faculties and the scope of will. In a lesser or greater degree, as the power of the operator and the susceptibilities of the subject may vary in different persons, both are under the direct influence of the mesmerist. It is said that under circumstances which, however, are seldom met with, the interior personality of the subject may pass away from the influence of the

<summary>This page explains how hypnosis disrupts sensory perception, allowing a "magnetizer" to dictate what a subject perceives as reality. It describes how these induced hallucinations are experienced as vivid truths and can lead to states like catalepsy, where the subject mimics the operator's movements.</summary>
<keywords>hypnosis, mesmerism, sensory perception, positive hallucination, negative hallucination, catalepsy, magnetizer, suggestibility</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the mesmerist's influence over the subject, specifically focusing on the enhancement of mental faculties and the total control over the subject's identity and will.</meta>

<page-num>243</page-num>
<header>MESMERISM.</header>

operator, but as a rule, he controls for the time being the whole individuality of his patient. As a rule, the intellectual faculties are also exceedingly quickened; a dull person becomes a sharp reasoner; he will give answers on subjects with which, in his normal state, he is practically unacquainted; he may occasionally be addressed in a language which he notoriously does not understand, but he will reply appropriately, his mind appearing to be for the time identified with the understanding of the mesmerist. It is said that he will himself speak in what are, to him, unknown tongues, but evidence in this direction should be received with extreme caution. The memory is also quickened to a surprising extent; there are instances of uneducated servant girls automatically repeating in the <term>magnetic condition</term>, with absolute accuracy, long passages from the Greek poets which they had heard recited by their masters at a much earlier period. The creative faculties are also frequently developed, with some manifesting a remarkable lyrical gift and improvising lengthy poems with obvious fluency, while others exhibit an astonishing and previously unknown aptitude for musical composition or painting.

The suspension of will which accompanies the <term>hypnotic state</term>, placing the subject—in mind, morals, and body—at the complete disposal of the operator, also controls the individuality, which can be erased and transformed according to the needs of any experiment. "Tell the sleeping individual he is a child, and he will begin to play children’s games; that he is a girl, and he will begin to sew; that he is a priest, and he will begin some <term>sacerdotal</term> function; that he is an old man, and he will call for a stick to support his steps; that he is a general, and he will draw himself up, give the word of command, etc.; that he is a dog, and he will crawl on all fours and bark." These are, however, only elementary and surface phenomena. In some cases, a new and underlying personality will spontaneously manifest; and if the hypnotic condition is prolonged, several of an opposite kind, and all more or less foreign to the patient's normal nature, will appear in periodical succession.

Where new personalities are not developed, we frequently have new senses or new forms of perception; the chief of these are clairvoyance, clairaudience, and the phenomena which constitute psychometry. CLAIRVOYANCE is the development of the faculty of interior sight. It was discovered for the modern world by the Marquis de Puységur, a disciple and friend of Mesmer; but vision at a distance was familiar to the old practitioners of those forms of divination which have been described in a previous paper. That enlargement of mind to which we have already made reference is in itself a part of clairvoyance, and, under favorable conditions, it is put to the test by the subject showing acquaintance with mere matters of fact—as, for instance, the private history of another person outside the sphere of his knowledge. In other cases he perceives what is taking place at a distance, can read in a closed book, in a locked slate, or in a sealed letter. There is also a therapeutic variety in which the seer perceives the interior condition of a sick person, and occasionally of himself, and indicates the nature of the required remedy. The third degree is closely connected with the phenomena of modern spiritualism, and in some cases perhaps no less intimately with those of hallucination and illusion. In those cases, which may be reasonably included in the first of these categories, the interior being of the seer seems elevated into another form of existence, and he enters into communication with the spirits of the departed, and with other hierarchies of extra-worldly intelligence. CLAIRAUDIENCE in the hypnotic and mesmeric states is of uncommon occurrence, and this also more properly belongs to spiritualism. It is that state in which Swedenborg, Böhme, and the mystics of all ages have conversed with voices from the unseen, while absorbed in any one of the known types of entrancement. It takes a

<header>MESMERISM.</header>

more readily intelligible form when the spirit of the subject is supposed to travel to distant earthly scenes, where it hears the conversation as well as observes the actions of other human beings.

PSYCHOMETRY is a relatively new development in psychic science. Here, the clairvoyant is placed in physical contact with any object whatsoever and perceives within its internal state the main points of its history. A lock of hair will reveal the entire personality of the man from whose head it was taken; a ring, a fragment cut from a garment, a letter, etc., will produce identical results—whatever has been intimately connected with a human being presents that person to the mind’s eye of the seer. It is also claimed that the natural history of a metal or mineral will be revealed simply by pressing it against the forehead of the hypnotic subject; some important and apparently genuine books have provided details of countless experiments in psychometric mineralogy and geology.

The highest and rarest states of mesmerism are those of prophetic frenzy and pure lucidity, but these phases are currently beyond the experience of professional scientists, and they border on a region of mysticism that exceeds basic limits.

Much has been written, and with good reason, about the dangers of hypnotic experiments and the power they grant to people who might possibly abuse it. Body and mind, will and imagination, consciousness and personal identity should not be lightly surrendered to any guardian. Nevertheless, when an operator possesses all the proper qualifications, we find that the entranced subject, in most cases, enters a higher state of moral development. It is undoubtedly possible through hypnotic experimentation to instill in a patient the intention of committing a murder; when he has apparently returned to his normal life and senses, that impression will remain, and he will commit that murder exactly according to the instructions given by the mesmerist. On the other hand, it is equally possible to create an aversion in a subject that will turn him away from a habit of vice; it

is possible to awaken positive virtues that were previously lacking; and, quite likely—though sufficient experimentation is lacking in this direction—it is possible to initiate a sharpening of the intellectual faculties during a trance that will remain permanent in the ordinary state. Mesmerism and hypnotism have been called the philosophers' stone in action, and they certainly lead us to the knowledge of a spiritual agent that is powerful in both the breaking down and rebuilding of the inner self. As such, it should be considered one of the essential tools of mysticism, and the student of mysticism may consider the careful, respectful, and earnest development of its possibilities as essential to all progress in the experimental knowledge of the soul.

The literature on Mesmerism is so vast that it is difficult to recommend specific authors. Deleuze and Dupotet were great authorities in France, as were Elliotson and Gregory in England. Esdaile is valuable for his account of surgical operations performed during the trance state. Sandby is an informative writer on the general subject, and there is interesting material in some letters by Harriet Martineau. The work of Binet and his collaborator ranks high among modern books.

# MODERN SPIRITUALISM.

We have seen that the traditions of medieval magic in the West were to some extent perpetuated in the doctrines and practices of mesmerism. The occult force, which was first made known to the modern world by the physician of Geneva, constituted at least a kind of connecting link between the phenomena of the old theurgic art—which was familiar to the Christian masters of mysticism—and the long sequence of marvels with which everyone at the present day has become more or less acquainted under the conventional name of spiritualism. The <term>pronaos</term> <note>The inner porch or entrance-hall of a temple.</note> of the spiritual temple has been said to be animal magnetism; and at a period prior to the date of the Rochester knockings, the German historians of magic were accustomed to interpret the thaumaturgic mysteries of the past by the light of the science of Mesmer. Insufficient as it was, this explanation had a certain basis in fact, and it may be safely affirmed that both magic and modern spiritualism have had many phenomena in common with animal magnetism. Things which are thus interlinked cannot be fundamentally dissimilar; in all ages and nations, the science of the soul must be reasonably supposed to consist in the development and application of the same arcane forces. Between ancient and modern psychological phenomena, there may be a difference of mode and degree, but their manifestation must undoubtedly take place by virtue of the same laws.

In the several sections of this book, we have already repeatedly referred to the present condition of psychic science, to the facts with which it is connected, and to the investigations involved in it. We have said that the paths which we are now attempting to travel were traveled by the old mystics, and by such land—

It suggests that animal magnetism serves as the foundational link between ancient theurgic practices and contemporary psychic phenomena.</summary>

Spontaneous occurrences, such as apparitions, hauntings, obsessions, and other phenomena which are not to be explained by the application of the known laws of nature, have been common to all countries and all times; in the great majority of cases, if genuine, they involve the supposition of an intelligence acting to some extent outside the visible world. The practical magic of the past was a series of methods and processes by which men endeavored to enter into a conscious communication with the powers which produced these wonders, and to become acquainted—for whatever purpose—with the mysteries of the invisible. Modern spiritualism is simply another series of methods and processes, necessarily similar in character, whereby men in the nineteenth century are seeking to accomplish precisely the same end. All that we have ascertained in the present by our independent psychological researches seems to substantiate the claims of the old magicians and mystics up to the point which our researches have reached; but this statement, of course, presupposes a discriminating separation of what is obviously fabulous and impossible in the history of magic. If the mystics seem to have surpassed us along our own lines, it must be because our methods are less perfect and our perseverance is less. One of the most important differences is indicated by the fact that the magicians claim to have been acquainted with many hierarchies of invisible intelligences, while spiritualism is solely concerned with communication with the souls of the dead; in this sense, if in no other, it is, to all intents and purposes, the necromancy of the nineteenth century. We have seen how the Magus of past centuries summoned the apparitions of the departed, how elaborate was his ceremony, how extensive were his rites, how enormous was the pneumatic force which he claims to have been able to exercise, and how impressive were the results which he appears to have achieved in the process. He worked, for the most part, alone, and, indeed, isolation was essentially a part of the prepara-

element of success in its investigation. True phenomenal spiritualism, as explained by its reasonable followers, is a scientific investigation into the nature, cause, and operation of certain forces that appear to originate outside the normal plane of Nature, and give evidence of human intelligence that cannot be identified with that which resides in the observers. Like that practical branch of mysticism which is more strictly called magic, its declared goal is the demonstration of a spiritual world around, within, and beyond the visible horizon of things called material—a world which is usually imperceptible to the senses of embodied man, but which, under certain circumstances, can be openly manifested in a way that rules out the hypothesis of hallucination. These circumstances are the “conditions” to which reference has already been made, and which we have shown to be of equal importance in both the operations of spiritualism and ancient magic. The conditions that are commonly required for success in the experiments of modern psychology are—

I. An intention on the part of the observers to enter, if possible, into communication with spirits; it is this intention that constitutes the difference between the phenomena of Spiritualism and those of spontaneous apparitions.

II. The presence of a certain harmony of will and desire, and of general good feeling among a given number of investigators who have decided to conduct experiments together.

III. A favorable state of the atmosphere, which should be free from electrical and magnetic disturbances, and should be dry and moderately warm.

IV. The formation of the circle.

V. The absence of bright light.

VI. The presence of what is called a medium.

Speaking broadly, these are the conditions that are laid down as generally essential. Phenomena which are recognized by spiritualists may, however, occur in the absence of any one of these rules, and, as far as the conscious preparation of the observer is concerned, they appear to have taken place on occasions when they have

Accounts of spontaneous manifestations from the other world, occurring in the absence of any thought, expectation, or desire on the part of the witnesses, have constituted—as already stated—a large section of supernatural history in all times and countries. Similarly, the harmonious relationship between the individuals comprised in the circle may be balanced by other advantages; storms and tempests cannot always interfere when a strong force has been developed by other conditions. Clasped hands during the formation of a circle are as much a guarantee against imposture—when light is obscured or excluded—as they are an essential element of success. Under certain circumstances, any class of phenomena may be produced in open day or in the midst of brilliant artificial lighting, while many private circles are claimed to have been established with the most gratifying results even in the absence of any developed medium.

Experiments that are usually dependent for their success upon atmospheric conditions, upon the disposition of the observer, and upon those unknown faculties which constitute what is called <term>mediumship</term>, cannot be readily conducted, because it is only up to a very limited point that we are able to ensure the necessary conditions. The investigation of the phenomena of Spiritualism is, therefore, beset with uncertainties; the communication between the two worlds is occasionally accomplished very easily and in a very complete manner; at other times, it is difficult and slow in the process without any assignable reason. It is evident, therefore, that the student of <term>mysticism</term>, who desires to assure himself that the modern branch of magical practice can offer the conscientious experimenter a reliable instrument of communication with supersensory planes and otherworldly modes of existence, should endeavor by the most patient and exact observation to increase his knowledge of these conditions. Those who have directed their special attention to this branch of the subject have frequently assured us that their study is the one thing needful—the beginning and the end of all true progress in psychological knowledge. This is most emphatically a point on which we may look for assistance to the processes of the <term>hierophants</term>.

There can also be no reasonable doubt that research into soul mysteries should be exclusively undertaken by spiritual minds, and that they should be conducted in such a manner, and with such an end in view, as will make them an integral part of their individual spiritual development. It would be extremely unbecoming to suggest that a large proportion of spiritualists are devoid of the interior qualities to which we refer, but no one who is acquainted with the movement can fail to be aware that the motives of investigation are frequently extremely frivolous, that it is frequently conducted under circumstances which make it morally unprofitable, and as a mere matter of curiosity, wonder, and amusement. These facts are indeed notorious, and to expand upon them would be a superfluous task. It is more important to observe that the merely scientific spirit—the simple thirst for knowledge, the desire, lawful and commendable as it is, to extend the field of observation and to investigate unknown forces—is an insufficient reason for spiritual research, though what is thus undertaken may occasionally awaken in the mind which is devoted to science an aspiration and a purpose which transcends ordinary science, noble as it undoubtedly is. A spiritual inquiry should be undertaken with spiritual objectives. In the most exalted sense of the term, it is legitimate only for two classes—those earnest seekers into the interior problems of being who desire to attain the permanent actualities which they believe underlie the appearances of the external universe, who are sufficiently absorbed by the fundamental hypotheses of religion to be anxious for their rigorous verification, who yearn, in the language of the mystics, to find their souls, who are probing the bases of faith, and who are in search of an instrument of communication with the divine source of life. That is the first class, of which the second is the fruit, being those who have passed through this initial stage wherein the faculties of being are exer-

Such, at least, is the rule which is laid down by the mystics; to such people alone is promised what they term the opening of the closed eye of the unknown darkness, and the gate which gives entrance into the garden of cucumbers <note>A biblical metaphor (Isaiah 1:8) used by mystics to signify a place of spiritual seclusion or a specific stage of the interior life.</note>, and into the closed palace of the king. It is needless, therefore, to say that the required disposition is essentially religious. Sanctity of thought, sanctity of life, and sanctity of soul and body are the first conditions of the process, for the work is one of reconstruction, which is the significance of the word religion; the system of spiritual communication which is proposed by Spiritualism being the way of the soul’s return into a conscious life in God.

Now, this end which we have mentioned has been present to the minds of many persons connected with the spiritual movements of modern times, and it is identical with the ambition of the mystics, but it is far from having been generally achieved at present. Communication, on the faith of the evidence, has been indeed established; the path has been opened, but we have not as yet passed through the first avenue. In plain words, we have discovered that there is intelligence on the other side of life, that under certain circumstances it can be manifested to our own, and that it can even give evidence of itself to our physical senses, but it is the lowest scale in the hierarchy of spiritual subsistence with which we have alone communicated; or, if this be too pronounced a statement in view of our ignorance, we have evidence, notwithstanding, that the “spirits” which frequent our séances are in no sense appreciably advanced beyond ourselves. Communications which claim to come to us from high intelligences and from the superior spheres are occasionally received, but it is seldom that they bear the impress which we have a right to expect from those altitudes, much less from the divine apex towards which the spiral paths of being are supposed to lead us.

In considering the phenomena of Spiritualism, we have taken a lofty standpoint because from our knowledge of the old mystics, and from a reasonable review of the

The vast body of testimony which has been recorded by an innumerable variety of observers belonging to all classes of life and character seems equivalent to a positive proof that the experiments of Spiritualism can be, and are, conducted in the absence of any exalted motives. They are obtained among the frivolities of the drawing room and among the vulgarities of the public séance; they can be produced in the presence of debauched and vicious persons. Only the nature of the manifestations and the class of the communicating intelligences corresponds to the dispositions of the investigators, and he that is himself filthy, perverse, or debased will attract what is unclean and low on the other side of life.

As in other divisions of this volume, we have abstained from attempting a philosophical defense of Spiritualism, or of debating its *à priori* possibility; it is equally impossible to array the evidence which exists in its favor. We are solely concerned with supplying to the student who is in search of instruction an account of the classes of phenomena which, it is alleged, are produced, and with putting him in possession of the means by which he may verify them for himself. The conditions which are generally requisite have been already enumerated; we have refrained from discussing their value, for they constitute an uninvestigated region of exceptional law, and in the present state of our knowledge it is futile to attempt an explanation of the "how" and "why" of our phenomena—a disability, it may be added, which we possess in common with the professors of all branches of physical science. As in chemistry, or as in the formation of crystals, we know that certain methods and processes produce certain results, but it is a matter of experience only. Our disabilities, however, are greater than those of the physicist, for he is concerned with blind forces acting inevitably, but we are the investigators of a power which, under given circumstances, can at any rate assume intelligence.

# MODERN SPIRITUALISM.

...that observers have told us about the nature of what is called <term>mediumship</term>. There is a lack of agreement among the old practical mystics on one important point, namely, whether it was possible for all persons to become magicians and to evoke spirits with success. The presence of a certain occult qualification which is nowhere explained in their writings—because in all probability it was not understood—is considered indispensable by some; however, it seems to have been the opinion of the majority that evocation could be achieved by all who were willing to lead the life of a <term>magus</term> and to fulfill the rites of magic. Both parties distinguished, nevertheless, between the natural and artificial magician: between the man who was magically predestined from birth, and the man who by will and labor could effect such a transformation in his nature as would make him capable of a work to which he was not predestined. Since the latter person was held in higher regard, we must understand a predestined magician not as a person who was ordained by mere automatic fate to a certain arbitrary end, but as one who by his birthright was constitutionally gifted with a certain quality which was favorable to the matter. The presence of a natural magician at the experiments of a master of the art was always deemed a desirable combination. Now, the natural magician appears to have been identical with the modern <term>medium</term>, and the term used by the mystics is in all respects preferable to the sloppy designation which has been overused past all endurance by the impostures and stupidities of the past forty years.

When a given number of persons undertake, for whatever purpose, an investigation of the phenomena of <term>Spiritualism</term>, they commonly seek the assistance of what is called a medium. In its actual significance, the name is identical with the ecclesiastical term <term>Pontifex</term>; he is the bridge of communication, the intermediary between the seen and the unseen worlds; it is through him that the other side of life manifests to this one, and if there be truth in the claims of <term>Spiritualism</term>, his, most assuredly, is an exalted and holy vocation, which should be prized more than life itself by its possessor.

<summary>The author compares the modern spiritualist medium to the "natural magician" of historical occultism, viewing the medium as a sacred bridge (Pontifex) between the physical and spiritual realms. He argues that while anyone can practice magic through labor, certain individuals are naturally gifted intermediaries.</summary>

<keywords>mediumship, magicians, magus, magic, Spiritualism, Pontifex, occult qualification, natural magician, artificial magician</keywords>

--- Page 272 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of spiritual communication, transitioning from the moral requirements of practitioners to the corruption and mysterious nature of mediumship.</meta>

<header>THE OCCULT SCIENCES.</header>

...which should never be debased, but instead exercised only for the highest ends. However, the <term>Pontifex</term> and the priest of the past too frequently abused their office, and we must not be surprised when we hear that the power of this new priesthood has gradually been converted into a mere commercial trade. It is daily bought and sold; it has been made subservient to the most sordid interests; every element of consecration has been successively stripped away from it; it has been used for aiding almost every class of imposture; and it has become so degraded in the hands of its owners that the genuine gift can often scarcely be distinguished from the fraudulent deceptions of mere charlatans.

If we search through the vast literature of <term>Spiritualism</term> for some account of the nature and qualities of <term>mediumship</term>, we find little that is satisfactory. There are many plausible theories; there is much skillful speculation and a quantity of uneducated guessing, but there is nowhere solid knowledge. Nor is the reason far to seek. Where is the scientist who can explain to us why mustard is hot or sugar sweet? Beyond the surface of phenomena, no penetration is possible; we must be content to observe and record; we have no way of attaining the ultimate reason for things.

> "What is the state of mediumship? What is the peculiarity of organization, and how is it acquired?" asks one writer who himself claims to be in possession of one of its gifts. "It would be difficult to tell what it is. It is often, and usually, possessed at birth; or it may be slowly or suddenly acquired. The spirit seems to have less hold upon the body, and to be more sensitive for that reason."

The explanation is poor enough; but it is useful in its evidence for one undoubted fact: that the medium is as ignorant of the nature of his strange power as the most profound philosophical mind is of the nature of its own consciousness. We must be content with the simple affirmation that there are certain persons who, by their constitution, are endowed with a force which defies fundamental investigation, but by which force it seems possible for spirits to become the temporary associates of embodied man, to manifest be—

<summary>The author critiques the commercialization and corruption of modern spiritual mediumship while noting that the actual mechanism of mediumistic power remains scientifically unexplained and mysterious even to the mediums themselves.</summary>

<keywords>mediumship, Spiritualism, priesthood, corruption, occult forces, consciousness, spirits</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of mediumship, exploring the debate over whether it is a universal potential or a rare gift, and introduces practical guidelines for spiritual circles.</meta>

# MODERN SPIRITUALISM.

...is said to appear before him in an assumed human shape, and to perform many acts which, based on existing evidence, are of the kind we call supernatural. The same lack of unanimity on one point that we have noticed in the writings of the mystics abounds in the literature of <term>Spiritualism</term>. <term>Mediumship</term>, on the one hand, is declared to be the gift of only a few; others consider it possible for everyone. True philosophical prudence would have withheld both statements. While it is difficult to suppose that one section of humanity enjoys qualities that are entirely foreign to the nature of the rest—and while it therefore seems reasonable to assume that the qualities in question are latent in all mankind—it is rash to claim that they can be developed in everyone. Analogy and experience alike contradict that statement. It seems, at the same time, probable in all respects that the power, whatever it may be, is not only inherent but would allow for development in a large number of persons who, by their circumstances, are prevented from any attempt to exercise it, and even from all knowledge on the matter. The faculty is commonly connected by its students with a passive or negative temperament. Like magical power, it is said that it may be induced by narcotics, stimulants, or by long fasts and vigils, by exhaustion of the body, and by an advanced degree of mental excitement; but its natural and reliable development is achieved by sitting with investigators in <term>spiritual circles</term>, especially in the presence of an already developed <term>medium</term>.

The rules for the formation of a circle and the conduct of research have been given by a number of authorities; they have little substantial difference. The most simple, complete, and practical is perhaps that which has been widely circulated under the initials <term>M.A. (Oxon.)</term> by Mr. <term>Stainton Moses</term>, who is well known as a leader of <term>psychic research</term> in England and as a medium for manifestations of a very advanced kind. The directions are, of course, written from the standpoint of one whose experience has abundantly verified the genuineness of all classes of <term>spiritual phenomena</term>, and they are designed for the instruction of those who

THE OCCULT SCIENCES.

...intend to conduct an inquiry through private sittings involving a few earnest people who can thoroughly rely on one another. It is in this way, we are told, that the majority of spiritualists have reached their convictions.

The circle of investigators should consist of four to eight people, and they should be divided as equally as possible into positive and negative temperaments; the latter are more commonly found among women. It is understood that the specific goal for all members of the circle is to enter into communication with the intelligences of the unseen world. The possibility of such communication involves three things in addition to the actual and conscious existence of unseen intelligence. First, there is the proximity of an invisible world of thinking beings, mentally conditioned in much the same way as ourselves, though the nature of this proximity should not be too closely tied to concepts of space and location. Second, there is the willingness of the unseen intelligence to respond to attempts at communication by embodied humans. Third, the possibility we are considering assumes the existence of a power which, if inherent, can be exercised, and if external, can be adapted to establish the desired communication. If we assume these conditions exist, the next thing to determine is a mode of communication; since at least one side is invisible to the other, it must obviously take the form of signals. Now, the code of signals commonly used requires no materials other than an ordinary table, preferably circular in shape. The investigators take their seats around this, alternating positive and negative temperaments, in comfortable and relaxed positions, with the palms of their hands placed flat upon the uncovered top surface of the table. The remaining directions are best given in the words of Mr. Stainton Moses:

> “Do not focus your attention too fixedly on the expected manifestation. Engage in cheerful, but not frivolous, conversation. Avoid dispute or argument. Skepticism has no discouraging effect, but a bitter spirit of opposition in a person of determined will may totally

It is practical to use a single tilt for No, three for Yes, and two to express doubt or uncertainty.

> "The signals may take the form of raps. If so, use the same code of signals, and as the raps become clear, ask that they be made on the table, or in a part of the room where they are demonstrably not produced by any natural means; however, avoid any troublesome imposition of restrictions on free communication. Let the Intelligence use its own means; if the attempt to communicate deserves your attention, it probably has something to say to you and will resent being hampered by useless interference. It rests greatly with the sitters to make the manifestations elevating or frivolous, and even deceptive."

Regardless of how absolute or well-grounded a circle's confidence in its individual members may be, the certainty derived from manifestations that do not obviously transcend the capabilities of the sitters—assuming bad faith in any of them—is at best of a moral kind; it is still founded on faith, specifically faith in the honesty of the circle. Now, such certainty is not scientific in character, and investigative efforts should be directed toward establishing experiments on the most satisfactory basis possible. Once it has been fairly demonstrated that unmistakable raps are being produced in clear light and at a reasonable distance from the sitters, we shall have entered the region of positive knowledge regarding the genuineness of the phenomena called spiritual; we shall have proved to ourselves the existence of a force still unknown to physical science that possesses the qualities of intelligence. It will give relevant answers to questions and occasionally provide tests that identify it with the mind of some person we know to have once lived in a human body on this earth, and who is now dead to us. In other cases, it will impart information which, though not a test of identity, is of a kind unknown to those present and is verified by subsequent research. In this way, by successive steps, the circle is led to recognize an independent understanding in another form of existence, and through this one tear in the

But should it turn out that one of the company possesses the qualities of <term>mediumship</term> in an advanced degree, perseverance will ultimately be rewarded by more astounding phenomena. The course of their development will vary in every case, and the following list must not be interpreted as the actual sequence of evolution, but merely as a categorization of the primary classes of manifestation of which instances have been recorded so far.

I. Increased force in the raps, which occasionally become so powerful that they destroy the objects at which they are directed.

II. The movement of stationary objects, limited in the initial stages to articles of small size and weight, but subsequently extended to the transport of things which no human being could lift by his individual efforts.

III. The <term>levitation</term> of human beings, in most cases the person who serves as the medium.

IV. The transport of small articles from a distance and through closed doors.

V. In rare instances, the transport of the medium from a distance and through closed doors.

VI. Other developments in experiments concerning the passage of matter through matter, such as the introduction of a coin into a sealed box, or placing a metal ring upon the wrist of the medium when his hands are tightly locked in the grasp of other sitters, or when he is otherwise secured.

VII. The transport of fruit, flowers, fish, and small animals from a distance; the choice of species is left to the circle, and the astonishing nature of the performance is sometimes increased by the selection of items that are out of season or native to distant countries.

VIII. Performances on musical instruments, usually of a small size, such as the <term>Oxford chimes</term> <note>A specific type of musical instrument or bell set frequently used in 19th-century spiritualist séances.</note>, and occasionally on the piano.

IX. The appearance of lights, generally phosphorescent

These, indeed, may be counted among the most frequent and attractive of phenomenal displays at a <term>séance</term>, and, as might be expected, they increase as the light is dimmed.

X. The appearance of living human hands, other than those of the circle; they are seen to move objects, they grasp the hands of the sitters, pluck at their garments, and in many ways give evidence of being controlled by an intelligence like our own.

XI. The entrancement of the <term>medium</term>, which is apparently a magnetic state induced by the will of the unseen. Usually it is a condition of the more advanced phenomena. One of its most common consequences is a temporary possession of the human body by a foreign intelligence, while the intelligence of the medium is for the time being absent or suspended. High moral teaching is occasionally given in this state, but it is one of the phenomena of <term>Spiritualism</term> which is most easily faked by impostors, and its value as evidence is therefore small. So far as it can be considered genuine, it seems one of the states of <term>mediumship</term> which can be most easily induced, and those who have once surrendered themselves to it appear to be able to command it at any time, and under the most varied and least favorable circumstances.

XII. The spirit voice speaking through the medium is called *Trance Speaking*. Related to this, and, under proper conditions, far more valuable as evidence, is the *Direct Voice*, which is heard in all parts of the room, and under circumstances which rule out the possibility of ventriloquism. During such manifestations the medium is generally in a state resembling a coma, and it is supposed that his organs are to some extent used in the production of the direct voice.

XIII. A frequent phenomenon of the spiritual trance is unconscious impersonation by the medium, acting

In this state, the subject is made to pose as a Form Manifestation of the spirits and is even draped in materials which are said to be transported for that purpose by the controlling Intelligence. Since these phenomena are uniquely suited to hide the frauds of professional mediums, and as they are at best a deceptive trick by the spirits themselves, they should not be encouraged by any intelligent circle of inquiry.

XIV. The *magnum opus* of the spiritual séance is the appearance of human figures that are demonstrably not those of any person present in the room, and which could not have been brought into the room to facilitate any fraud. Commonly, this occurs when the medium is in a deep trance, and total darkness seems to be the preferred condition. For this latter reason, what is called a séance for materialization is unsatisfactory from the perspective of evidence. But we are assured by many investigators whose honesty on any other subject would be accepted without question, that it is possible to obtain the materialization of spiritual forms in broad daylight—even in gardens, woods, and forests—and certainly in a dimmed light that is sufficient for all purposes of investigation. When the séance is held with shut and bolted doors, in a room where there has been no prior preparation and where there are no secret entrances, if the few people gathered find more than just themselves among them, they have reached the farthest point to which phenomenal Spiritualism currently claims to lead. They have demonstrated the reality of what has been the dream and wonder of all the ages. The figures manifested under favorable circumstances appear in rapid succession, differing in height and size, in clothing and appearance; occasionally they exceed the normal height of humans, while others are small children. The manifestation is sometimes so slight as to be almost like a thin vapor; at other times, it is so solid and perfect that it can remain for a considerable period, visible and tangible to everyone—speaking, walking, and embracing those nearby. On rare occasions, more than one of these mysterious beings appears at the same time, one forming out of the side of the medium, while another is developed out of the apparition thus obtained. The first detected presence of materialization is usually a little cloud or column of luminous vapor from which a figure is gradually built up. Those who desire a practical acquaintance with this most important form of mediumship will do well to consult the literature that exists on the subject before attempting an experiment.

XV. Automatic Writing is one of the most common phenomena of a séance, and it may be distinguished broadly into two classes. First, there is involuntary writing obtained in the normal state, either by holding a pen lightly and waiting for the hand to be controlled, or by the use of the little instrument called a Planchette, which is so universally known that it requires no description. Automatic communications of the same kind are also obtained when the medium is in a state of trance. In either case, simulation is so easy, and the motives that drive imposture are so numerous and so difficult to trace, that no value can be considered as attaching to either class unless information is provided by the controlling agent which is obviously beyond the knowledge of the automatic writer, or in a language that is demonstrably unknown to him.

XVI. Spirit-writing is not confined to the automatic type, and a medium for Direct Writing is justly considered the most valuable of all his kind, as he is also the most uncommon. The phenomena in connection with this group of manifestations are familiar to the majority of people by report, if not by experience. In the test cases which are cited on the subject, a fragment of chalk or pencil is placed within a folding slate, which is locked, tied with cord, and sealed. Intelligent observers take care to obtain one for themselves, and see that it does not leave their possession. It is placed on a table in front of the medium, and the most he is permitted to do is to place one of his hands upon it while it is still being held by its owner. Investigators inform us that, despite these and still more elaborate pre-

It should be added that **Direct Writing** is not confined to the methods of the locked slate. Instances are recorded of a pencil being placed in full light upon a sheet of paper; this pencil, under the eyes of the observers, was raised by an invisible force, assumed the upright position it would in an ordinary hand, and wrote intelligible messages.

XVII. The last and most rarely successful of all the experiments we have been listing is **Spirit Photography**, which should not be confused with the extremely simple process of photographing the materialized form of a spirit. The experiment consists of obtaining upon the exposed plate of a camera the impression of a form which, because of its extreme thinness, is invisible to the ordinary eye. The result, when successful, is usually the appearance of a ghostly human figure. The presence of a medium is indispensable, and the best method of procedure is to photograph such a person by ordinary means and trust to circumstances favoring the development of a spirit from within the sphere of his magnetic atmosphere. The introduction of the dry-plate process has greatly encouraged trickery in these experiments, and the opportunities for fraud, combined with the complete uncertainty of every genuine method, make this feature of the spiritual séance the most laborious and unsatisfactory of all. It is well, however, to bear in mind that when a specific figure is obtained in front of the person being photographed, this result is entirely beyond the skill of the known processes of photographic art. When a plate has been previously tampered with, the shadowy figure intended to represent the ghost will appear behind or to one side,

and will generally be unrelated to the person whose portrait is subsequently taken on the same plate. The literature of Spirit Photography is very limited, but portraits of people whose hands are held or who are embraced by the ghostly arms of some spectral visitor are fairly numerous, and they seem to defy explanation by any theory of fraud.

Such are the most important branches of the phenomena called spiritual. We do not intend to explain them, nor to enter into a philosophical defense of their possibility. They concern matters that are entirely outside the legitimate sphere of belief, and the evidence that exists in their favor should only be considered as a justification for personal inquiry. We have placed in the hands of the student a clear summary of the alleged facts and a sketch of the methods of experimentation. If the investigations of a private circle are halted at an early stage for want of the necessary power, one must turn to a professional medium when other resources have failed. A professional medium is a person who claims to possess the required qualities to an advanced degree and who uses them for financial profit. As the exercise of such gifts is usually very draining, and as those who devote themselves to it have, like others, a living to earn, it is theoretically right and reasonable in every respect that they should receive adequate compensation; however, the practice has encouraged every kind of fraud. The anxiety to give every investigator their money’s value, lest they should leave unsatisfied and the trade should cease to be lucrative, has prompted the genuine medium to supplement a power—which is not at all at his command—with the tricks of a conjuror. Moreover, the general interest in the subject has attracted impostors into the profession who have never possessed these gifts. Every precaution should therefore be taken by investigators to protect themselves against fraud when it becomes necessary to hire the services of a public medium. They should especially beware of those who refuse to sit under reasonable test conditions.

<header>MODERN SPIRITUALISM.</header>

It is useless to attempt, in the space we have available, to provide a student’s guide to the vast literature of Spiritualism. For convincing testimony from men of science, one may refer to Alfred Russell Wallace’s “Miracles and Modern Spiritualism;” to Crooke’s celebrated “Researches into the Phenomena called Spiritual;” and to Zöllner’s “Transcendental Physics.” For the experiences and testimony of an advanced medium—who is also an advanced thinker and a man of high culture—the reader should consult the numerous published works of the Rev. Stainton-Moses (M.A. Oxon.). For the history of the movement, there are “Modern American Spiritualism” and “Nineteenth Century Miracles,” both by Mrs. Emma Hardinge-Britten. In the category of inspirational writing, there are the works of Andrew Jackson Davis, who is considered the Jacob Böhme of Spiritualism.

Spiritualism is the only branch of the occult sciences that has achieved the popularity indicated by the publication of weekly newspapers. The best of these is undoubtedly *Light*, and reading this journal will keep the aspiring student up to date with the progress of the movement throughout the world.
