<meta>This page transitions from the bibliography of Modern Spiritualism to the beginning of a section on Theosophy, exploring the origins of mystical tradition and the relationship between Western and Eastern occult thought.</meta>

# THEOSOPHY.

However much they apparently disagreed, magicians and alchemists agreed on one point: that they received their knowledge through tradition. What, then, was this tradition, and where did it come from? Nominally, in the case of magicians, it was attributed to Solomon and the <term>Kabbalah</term>. But it is clear that Western <term>mysticism</term> had its counterpart in Eastern mysticism; that both had strong mutual affinities; and that <term>Sufis</term>, <term>Brahmans</term>, and <term>Buddhists</term> owed nothing to the Jews or their wisdom. Mysticism preceded the Kabbalah, and magic existed before Solomon. The appeal to the wisest of men was clearly a device; the Kabbalah was a greater puzzle than the transcendentalism supposedly derived from it. What, again, was the tradition, and where did it originate? Many writers grappled with this problem. Some, like the author of “A Suggestive Inquiry into the Hermetic Mystery,” believed they had reached a solution and were alarmed by their magnificent discovery. Few, however, achieved any clear insight or permanent satisfaction. But what may be called a partial revelation on this subject claims to have appeared within a relatively recent period. It reduces the claims of Western mystics to measurable proportions; while it affirms the validity of their <term>psychic</term> knowledge, it defines its limitations and claims to possess the two keys—philosophical and practical—which grant entrance into the secluded sanctuary of universal mysticism. These keys have been obtained from the East, to which we are told we must turn for light in psychic matters as well as physical ones. *Ex oriente Lux* <note>Latin: "Light from the East."</note>. There it bids us look upward and see, for another morning has dawned. The

<summary>The author explores the origins of mystical tradition, arguing that while Western occultists often cited Solomon and the Kabbalah, the true roots of mysticism are universal and find their highest expression in Eastern wisdom. A "recent revelation" (Theosophy) is presented as the key to reconciling these traditions and providing a clear path to universal psychic knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>Theosophy, mysticism, Kabbalah, Solomon, alchemy, Eastern mysticism, Sufis, Brahmans, Buddhists, Hermetic Mystery, psychic knowledge, Ex oriente Lux</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of mystical traditions, transitioning from the history of Western arcane knowledge to a definition of Theosophy as a synthesis of Western and Eastern mysticism.</meta>

# THE OCCULT SCIENCES.

The herald of which we have been speaking is called Theosophy.

To all who are acquainted with the literature of Western mysticism, the term Theosophy is familiar in various ways. We find it in magical rituals and in the writings of Jacob Böhme. It was used in the seventeenth century to distinguish the divine wisdom of the magus and kabbalist from the theology of the schools—a distinction that was intentionally provocative, contrasting the realities of experimental research into the hidden mysteries of God, humanity, and the universe with the wordy speculations of scholastic thought. Concerned with facts and not words, the Theosophy of the mystics was considered to be a different and higher thing than any current theology of the time. Now, that elaborate system of transcendental life and doctrine, that master-key to the mysteries of arcane science and philosophy which, under the old name of Theosophy, has become so extensively spread, has been so much talked about, and in a certain sense has been in vogue today, attaches fundamentally much the same significance to the same word. 

Modern Theosophy is the latest and most extreme development of supernaturalism. Both in the character of its claims and in the intellectual qualifications of its promoters, as well as in the work which it has accomplished, it deserves very serious investigation at the hands of the student of mysticism; nor is it possible to ignore it in any evaluation of current psychological opinion. It is the legitimate daughter of universal mysticism, and, in a certain way, it may be regarded as a mystic synthesis. It absorbs the transcendentalism of the West while acting as the mouthpiece of Eastern magic. In it, the Oriental arcane wisdom of the distant past may be said to have renewed its youth. In its principles, comprehensiveness, and tolerance, it claims the mark of catholicity, and its present spread is possibly to be regarded as only a foreshadowing and promise of a geographical universality which is yet to come.

Could Spiritualism be viewed as, in its origin and development, a historical parallel to Christianity, then Theosophy would be the gnosticism of the new Gospel,

<summary>This page defines Theosophy as a bridge between Western transcendentalism and Eastern magic, distinguishing its experimental approach to divine wisdom from scholastic theology. It positions modern Theosophy as a comprehensive synthesis of universal mysticism and a potential intellectual successor to Spiritualism.</summary>
<keywords>Theosophy, Western mysticism, Jacob Böhme, magus, kabbalist, scholasticism, transcendentalism, arcane science, Eastern magic, Spiritualism, Gnosticism</keywords>

and the words used by Gibbon regarding that unique mysticism may be equally applied to this. "It comprised the most refined, the most learned, and the most wealthy of the name." But although people of respectable intelligence have believed in the identity of primitive Christianity and modern Spiritualism, the affinity—historical and otherwise—between Theosophy and the faith of the gnosis is much closer than any that can be legitimately traced on the other side.

It is beyond the limits of our present purpose to undertake a defense of any specific system of doctrinal mysticism, new or old. Here we are simply concerned with a non-critical statement of the character and objects of Theosophy, and, with this fact in view, no expression of personal allegiance or disagreement should on any pretext be admitted in a short explanatory paper. It is a subject which is still in evidence; it has not yet received an impartial and thorough analysis, and its proponents are still living. For these reasons, an abridged summary of an unfinished case should not be followed by an attempt to anticipate a verdict. Yet, as we have ventured to guide the student in other matters, so in this it seems legitimate to say that he will find in Theosophy an exalted form of transcendental mysticism which will not only reward investigation, but which, as one of the psychological facts of the day, he will find himself forced to investigate very early in his philosophical inquiry. For the rest, we shall, in the main, rely on the unmodified opinions of its best proponents; we shall make no reference to existing controversies or existing divisions, nor indeed to any matters of a merely historical or personal character. There will be, moreover, little need to distinguish between Theosophy as it actually is and as it appears under the veneer of misrepresentation, or through the medium of public ignorance. Its unadorned presentation will be a sufficient answer to the caricatures of the moment. It will be well, nevertheless, to establish at the outset that Theosophy is not to be regarded as a refined form of Spiritualism, though Spiritualism alone has undoubtedly made it possible to

<meta>This page continues the introduction to Theosophy, clarifying that while it acknowledges the observations of Spiritualism, it considers itself a far older, comprehensive spiritual science distinct from—and prior to—major world religions like Buddhism and Christianity.</meta>

...promote Theosophy; and though Theosophy, as would naturally follow, broadly admits the facts of the Spiritualist, if we are to regard it from the standpoint of its promoters, we would be wrong to think that it is integrally associated with the science of the circle as an originating cause of its existence; for Theosophy, from that standpoint, predates historical religions. Nor does it differ from modern Spiritualism simply by offering an alternative hypothesis to explain the existence of those psychic facts which are equally acknowledged by both. Strictly speaking, it claims to be a complete spiritual <term>gnosis</term>, developed—like all sciences—very slowly and gradually, with much pain, suffering, and superhuman perseverance, from a remote period of antiquity, and now offered for the spiritual illumination of the Western world, which is ready to participate in the hidden knowledge of the East. From this view, the psychic facts of the period are simply indications of that stage of spiritual development in this hemisphere which our race necessarily had to reach before its initiation into the mysteries of an absolute divine knowledge.

Furthermore, Theosophy is not to be regarded as a modernized form of Buddhism adapted to the intelligence of the nineteenth century; nor, on the other hand, is it actually a return to the primeval fountain of Buddhist doctrine, even though its promoters have identified it closely with that dominant religion of the East; though it counts countless Buddhists among its foremost disciples; and though, finally, its distinctive name has become almost interchangeable everywhere with that of <term>Esoteric Buddhism</term>. It is undoubtedly identified with the underlying principles of Buddhist faith and teaching, but Theosophy recognizes a common foundation for all religions. Thus, <term>Esoteric Buddhism</term> is not to be distinguished from <term>Esoteric Christianity</term>, nor from the spirit of the <term>Zoroastrian</term> religion. The hidden principles, the arcane meaning, and the scientific basis of each actually constitute the divine wisdom of Theosophy.

# THEOSOPHY.

that Theosophy is not a new religion, though the term has been frequently applied to it. The common foundation of all theological systems can be a novelty only in the sense of being generally or previously unknown. But should its claim to this distinction be denied, it should not even then be regarded as a new departure in doctrinal belief; it is rather a new system of religious interpretation. It teaches nothing that is actually fresh to the student of comparative mythology, but it views much that has been previously taught in a new light, and from a new standpoint. Taking the much-perverted term religion in its real sense, Theosophy is certainly a religion, for it professes to provide its followers with a scientific instrument for the union of their individual being with the universal life of God. The instrument, however, is not new; it is that of all the mystics, though it claims to be more perfect than theirs.

Theosophy at the same time must not be regarded as a harmonious combination of many mystic systems. We have said that it is a mystic synthesis, but it contains something which is over and above all of these, and that again is the essential principle which is behind them all, and which for want of a more distinctive name has been termed by Theosophists the **Ancient Wisdom Religion**, a term which the discriminating student will do well to interpret somewhat broadly, for, as the Wisdom Religion is the <term>radix</term> <note>Latin for "root"</note> of all religions, it is therefore Theosophy itself from the standpoint of those who profess it. The principles of this religion may, to some extent, be recovered from the discriminating study of existing faiths and creeds, and thus Theosophy is, in one of its phases, an analysis of religious belief. To such analysis a considerable proportion of independent Theosophical literature is devoted at the present day.

> "The system of thought which has been described as 'Esoteric Buddhism,'" says Mr. A. P. <meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of Theosophy, explaining how the study of comparative mythology and esoteric principles leads to a synthesis of religious truth and personal evolution.</meta>

aspects with the study of the essential principles of religious belief. Theosophists have no preconceived attachment to one presentation or form of religious belief over another. They are in pursuit of pure and simple truth, convinced that there must be a real factual basis regarding problems such as the origin and destiny of the soul, just as there are facts regarding the chemical affinities of the elements or the relations of heat and electricity. They find that comparing various religious beliefs will often enable them to eliminate the superficial corruptions of each, so that the remaining doctrines—reduced to their simplest terms and most abstract expression—will then be found practically identical, even when a first glance at their outer aspects seemed to reveal great discrepancies. In this way, the study of any great religion in its esoteric aspect is a theosophical undertaking.

A theosophical inquiry into the laws of comparative mythology differs from an ordinary historical investigation in the vitality of purpose which the student brings with him into the field. He is not a mere observer; he does not merely collect, sift, and organize the facts of universal religious belief. When he has determined the governing principles of their existence to his satisfaction—in other words, when he has devised some theory which accounts for their existence to his personal satisfaction—he does not consider his task finished. He believes in the existence of an undying spirit in man, and his search is prompted by a lively desire to throw light upon the mysteries of the spirit. His search is therefore not dead but living, and the principles which he endeavors to discover are intimately associated with his own interior evolution.

But though the proper study of comparative mythology may result in much light for the student, it is not claimed that it will do more than provide him with a very partial theosophical illumination. It will put him in possession of the cardinal and ultimate principles of all religious belief. If he would test the truth of those principles, he must move beyond the level of history and enter the realm of psychology. The science of comparative...

Religion is indeed historical psychology, but it bears the same relation to experimental psychic science that the comparison of alchemical symbols bears to the practical search for the Stone of the Philosophers; and experimental psychology is a research into the activities and scope of the inner man, which must be pursued by every person within their own interior. It is therefore the main object of Theosophy “to push our knowledge of Nature’s laws ahead of the finest discoveries of modern civilization, proceeding hand in hand with these, but ever pressing onward into the region of consciousness and super-material existence, guided by the light already in the world—which has been in the world from the first beginnings of that union between divinity and matter which constitutes the sentient universe.” To distinguish the research of Theosophy from that of unaided mysticism in the West, it is necessary to point out that the light already in the world, alluded to in this citation from Mr. Sinnett, is something more than what can be obtained from accessible supernatural history. While Theosophy by no means underrates the value of recorded psychic facts, it claims to be in possession of a superior source of knowledge—that knowledge from which were born the fundamental principles of the mysterious Wisdom Religion. This religion has been preserved throughout the ages, has been expanded in the course of its preservation, and has its living custodians today, who have consented to its partial revelation; and it will depend on us whether we shall learn more from them.

Before we attempt to present the knowledge that has reportedly been shared in this way, we must speak briefly concerning its custodians and give some explanation of the means through which they have come into communication with the Western world.

The existence of a secret and psychological body of knowledge which was absolute and momentous has been affirmed by all mystics; and Éliphas Lévi, as we have seen, defines magic as the traditional science of the secrets of Nature which has come down to us from the wise men of old. [As you] are aware, the name "Wisdom Religion" was first applied to this science in the celebrated volumes of *Isis Unveiled*, published in America in 1877 and written in English by a Russian lady, Madame Blavatsky. The existence of high intelligences—who inhabit mortal forms and are the actual guardians of arcane knowledge at this hour—was more recently made known in two equally celebrated books by Mr. A. P. Sinnett: *The Occult World* and *Esoteric Buddhism*. While humanity in the West has devoted itself almost exclusively to physical science and has made the progress in that field with which we are all familiar, the East has been occupied with the study of the soul and has made even greater progress. The pursuit in the one case has been open and free to all; in the other, it has been secret, more slowly accumulated, and shared and known by few. But “checked and examined at every point, verified in all directions, and constantly under examination,” it has “come to be viewed by its custodians as constituting the absolute truth concerning spiritual things. . . . The secret knowledge is regarded not only by all its adherents, but by vast numbers who have never expected to know more of it than that such a doctrine exists, as a mine of entirely trustworthy knowledge.” In America, in Europe, and scattered all over the East, there are said to be individuals and associations who have entered upon the path of attainment, who possess more or less knowledge of spiritual mysteries, and who, by the fact of their attainment, would seem to be more or less in communication with the earthly center of illumination. That center is in the far East, and the designated home of the great initiates is affirmed, at the present moment, to be somewhere in the highlands of Tibet. They are not exclusively Orientals; all distinctions of race and creed are abandoned among them. They are unseen, but not inactive; they are a kind of guardian spirits, “masters of philosophy and metaphysics,” says Colonel Olcott, “who benefit mankind without their influence even being suspected.” They constitute the “rare flowering” of many generations of researchers into

other. “In the year 1874,” says Colonel Henry S. Olcott, “Madame Blavatsky and I met. I had been a student of practical psychology for nearly a quarter of a century. From boyhood no problem had interested me so much as the mystery of man, and I had been seeking for light upon it wherever it could be found. To understand the physical man I had read something of anatomy, physiology, and chemistry. To get an insight into the nature of mind and thought, I had read the various authorities of orthodox science, and practically investigated the heterodox branches of phrenology, physiognomy, mesmerism, and psychometry. . . . In the year above mentioned, I was investigating a most startling case of mediumship, that of William Eddy, an uneducated farmer, in whose house the alleged spirits of dead persons were appearing nightly and often talking. . . . With my own eyes I saw, within the space of about three months, some five hundred of these apparitions, under circumstances which, to my mind, excluded the possibility of trickery or fraud. . . . Madame Blavatsky and I met at this farm-house, and the similarity of our tastes for mystical research led to an intimate acquaintance. She soon proved to me that, in comparison with the *chela* of an Indian *Mahatma*, the authorities I had been accustomed to look up to knew absolutely nothing. Little by little she opened out to me as much of the truth as my experiences had fitted me to grasp. Step by step I was forced to relinquish illusory beliefs, cherished for twenty years. And as the light gradually dawned on my mind, my reverence for the unseen teachers who had instructed her grew apace. At the same time, a deep and insatiable yearning possessed me to seek their society, or, at least, to take up my residence in a land which their presence glorified, and incorporate myself with a people whom their greatness ennobled. The time came when I was blessed with a visit from one of these *Mahatmas* in my own room at New York—a visit from him, not in the physical body, but in the ‘double,’ or *Mayavi-rupa*. . . . His conversation sent my heart at one leap around the globe, across oceans and continents, over sea and land,

<note>Pencil bracket in left margin</note>
The outcome of this enthusiasm was the foundation of a Theosophical Society for the formation of a Universal Brotherhood of Humanity—the promotion of the study of Aryan and other Eastern literature, religions, and sciences—and the investigation of the hidden mysteries of nature and the psychical powers of man.

How this society grew and flourished, despite the idealistic character of its first objective; how its lodges exist at the present day in all parts of the world; how they are the centers of a systematic propaganda, are questions for the historical student. Also, how the zealous American soldier fulfilled his ambition of going to India; how he was joined there by Madame Blavatsky; how Mr. A. P. Sinnett became convinced of the extraordinary psychological phenomena which took place in the presence of Madame Blavatsky; how he also, like Colonel Olcott, saw the appearances of the adepts, are matters that may be mentioned in passing, but must be lightly passed over. We have stated as briefly as possible the essence of what is currently known concerning the source of the theosophical revelation, and all that need be said concerning their accredited representatives in the outer world. The nature of this latest and final message from "those who know" to those who would qualify to learn is the final stage of our evaluation.

<note>Orange-red line in left margin</note>
Starting from the broad and general claim that Theosophy, or Esoteric Buddhism, as it may be interchangeably called, "offers us the wisdom acquired by thought and experience on a higher plane," we shall find that its chief teachings may be classified under three heads—the Nature and Constitution of Man—the Destiny of Man—and the Way to Attain. In the first of these classes, seven composing elements, each capable of still further subdivision, are recognized as substantially existing in man as he is, though some of the highest of the series are still in an undeveloped state. First comes the Physical Body, and to that is united the Animal Vitality which

The fourth is the Animal Soul, which is the highest-developed substance of the animal creation; it is one of the superior elements and can be developed still further by a union with the fifth principle, which is the Human Soul, "the seat of reason and memory." The sixth principle is called the Spiritual Soul, and the seventh is the Pure Spirit.

Distinctions like these will appear to many students as mere idle refinements, but they serve their purpose in an exceedingly complicated body of transcendental doctrine. This doctrine includes not only the history and evolution of the cosmic world—the sequence observed in the development of the phenomenal universe—but also the experiment of positive knowledge, or perception of truth and reality, and the theory and practice of every species of the multiform art of magic.

From the second body of theosophical instruction, we learn that at death there is a final separation of the three lower principles from those which are above them, and an ascent of the superior constituents into what is called the Astral Plane. There, the complicated fifth principle is disintegrated; all that is highest in its nature seeks union with the sixth, while the rest becomes identified with the astral body and remains in the world of forms. Esoteric Buddhism philosophically bases all apparitions of the dead on the differentiation of the Astral Body from the real man. Meanwhile, the sublimated remnant of the fifth principle departs with the Spiritual Soul and the Divine Spirit into a spiritual condition—an interior state of absorption—where all the superior possibilities of "sensuous emotion" are developed by the operation of laws that are proper to this withdrawn form of visionary existence. There, the individual remains until the arcane process of retribution begins to work and he is reborn into objective existence, with reincarnation continuing indefinitely from body to body and from planet to planet. It is, in fact, a most complex presentation of the...

Beyond all incarnations, beyond all trances of an intermediate state, there is an abstract condition of pure being which transcends the phenomenal world of illusions and pain. It is possible to attain that state; it is possible to transcend the normal status of one's evolution in the refining process of the ages, to reach into the sphere of a higher law, to enter into a forcing-house <note>a place where growth is accelerated</note> of the interior man, to walk in the path of the <term>adept</term>, to tread the way of attainment. And that is the path along which Theosophy claims to lead you, after the manner of all the mystics, by detachment, abstinence, asceticism of the most rigid nature, contemplation, solitude, and instruction. It lays stress upon the necessity of instruction, and declares that it will certainly come, for those who earnestly seek it, if not in the life when the effort is first made, then assuredly in the next incarnation. It affords the promise of continued companionship through the ages—both in the spiritual realms of Nature, and again on the earth plane in periods of reincarnation—for those who are truly united by the ties of a loving sympathy.

There is no material hell in the theosophical economy of the universe, because the earth life itself, for the normally wicked, when they reincarnate, becomes their appropriate sphere of punishment; but there is a condition of spiritual wretchedness—the converse of the highest spiritual bliss, which is *Nirvana*—and this supremely evil state is denominated *Avitchi*. Between the terrible destiny of *Avitchi*, only possible for the spiritually wicked, and the supreme state of blessedness, there lies a condition which is all but annihilation for those who grovel hopelessly on the material plane all through the procession of the ages, during which the material life should be the school of spiritual aspiration.

But the complete exposition of the doctrine would be impossible here. With *Nirvana*, the higher consciousness—the knowledge which is above self-knowledge, the being

<summary>This page discusses the theosophical path to spiritual attainment through asceticism and instruction, explaining that reincarnation serves as an earthly punishment while contrasting the spiritual states of Nirvana and Avitchi.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, Glanvil, adept, reincarnation, Nirvana, Avitchi, asceticism, spiritual evolution, arcane wisdom</keywords>

--- Page 298 ---
<meta>This page concludes the discussion of theosophical philosophy, comparing its ultimate states to Christian mysticism and listing the movement's foundational literature and periodicals.</meta>

<page-num>282</page-num>
<header>THE OCCULT SCIENCES.</header>

...which is beyond existence, the student will compare to the absorption of the mystic marriage that was known to the Christian adepts. But whatever the final verdict of the unbiased mind may be concerning the evidence that supports the revelation, the philosophical system it expounds, and the compensation it can ensure to a “passionate, bright endeavor,” the investigator cannot fail to learn much by its study. Whether or not it can lead one into the whole truth, it is full of the beauty of wisdom, the suggestiveness of profundity, and the brightness of a mystic dream. If it has not transformed the world, it has permeated all forms of thought. It has attracted some of the most accomplished, most thoughtful, and most refined intellects. It is certainly worthy of study, and those who are wise will suspend their judgment until the time for judgment arrives.

The literature of Theosophy is already extensive. Its foundation is “Isis Unveiled.” Its culminating point is in “The Secret Doctrine,” by the same writer. The investigations of Mr. A. P. Sinnett are recorded in the “Occult World”; the elements of the arcane philosophy in which he was mysteriously instructed will be found in “Esoteric Buddhism.”

A volume of lectures by Colonel H. S. Olcott on “Theosophy, Religion, and Occult Science” has commanded a wide circle in the reading world, and the latest developments of the subject will be seen in the “Key to Theosophy,” which Madame Blavatsky had issued before her death in the form of a catechism for the benefit of her less-instructed disciples. There are also two official periodicals of high excellence and great interest—*Lucifer*, a monthly magazine which is published in London, and *The Theosophist*, which predates it by several years and is the organ of the headquarters at Bombay.

->TURNBULL AND SPEARS, PRINTERS, EDINBURGH.<-

## BY THE SAME AUTHOR.

# The Real History of the Rosicrucians.

->FOUNDED ON THEIR OWN MANIFESTOS, AND ON FACTS<-
->AND DOCUMENTS COLLECTED FROM THE WRITINGS<-
->OF INITIATED BRETHREN.<-

->By ARTHUR EDWARD WAITE.<-

### Opinions of the Press.

> "We desire to speak of Mr. Waite's work with the greatest respect regarding its honesty, impartiality, and sound scholarship. Mr. Waite has provided, for the first time, the documents with which Rosicrucianism has been connected in full." —*Literary World.*

> "There is something mysterious and fascinating about the history of the Virgin Fraternity of the Rose." —*Saturday Review.*

> "A curious and interesting story of the actions of a mysterious association in times when people were more ready to believe in supernatural phenomena than the highly educated, matter-of-fact people of today." —*Morning Post.*

> "The work not only of a refined scholar, but of a man who knows what he is writing about, and that is a great deal more than can be said for other books on the same topic. . . . Much of what he has to tell us has the double merit of being not only true, but new." —*John Bull.*

> "Mr. Waite's book on 'Rosicrucianism' is a perfect contrast to the one we noticed a month or two ago. The latter is a farrago of ill-digested learning and groundless fancies, while the former is, at all events, an honest attempt to discover the truth about the Society of the Rosy Cross. . . . The study of 'Occultism' is so popular just now that all books bearing on such topics are eagerly read; and it is a comfort to find one writer who is not ashamed to confess his ignorance after telling us all he can discover." —*Westminster Review.*

> "Mr. Waite is a great authority on esoteric science and its literature. Those who have read his extremely interesting work upon the writings of Eliphas Lévi, the modern magician, will expect in his 'History of the Rosicrucians' a treatise of more than ephemeral importance, and they will not be disappointed. . . ." —*Morning Post.*

> “Some of the most interesting chapters in the book are devoted to an account of the four great apologists for Rosicrucianism: Robert Fludd, Michael Maier, Thomas Vaughan, and John Heydon. Each of these chapters contains much curious matter, very metaphysical and very transcendental, but worth being studied by those who appreciate the influence which the many forms of occultism have exercised upon civilization.”—*St James’s Gazette*.

> “To many readers the most fascinating pages in ‘The Real History of the Rosicrucians’ will be those in which the author reprints Foxcroft’s 1690 translation of ‘The Chemical Wedding of Christian Rosencreutz,’ which had originally appeared in German in 1616. This strange romance is full of wonderful things.”—*Saturday Review*.

> “We would recommend Mr. Waite’s very painstaking volume to all who may be desirous to get to the heart of the Rosicrucian mystery. . . . So much nonsense has been talked and written about this imaginary order that it is quite refreshing to find a writer competent and willing to reduce the legend to its true proportions, and show how and when it had its origin.”—*Knowledge*.

# The Magical Writings of Thomas Vaughan

->Opinion of the Press.<-

> “Some of Vaughan’s reflections remind us of Jacob Boehme, but the Welsh mystic is, as a rule, more easily followed than his German brother. Indeed, with a few exceptions, the sense is clear enough to make the volume agreeable reading even to the uninitiated. . . . The seventeenth century was an age of plain speaking, and Vaughan, when differing from anyone, sometimes used terms more forcible than elegant. Mr. Waite supplies some interesting information about the history of occultism in his Introductory Lecture on the Esoteric Literature of the Middle Ages, and on the Underlying Principles of Theurgic Art and Practice in Western Christendom.”—*Glasgow Herald*.

<meta>This page continues the advertisements for Arthur Edward Waite's works, following the previous page's focus on "The Real History of the Rosicrucians" and "The Magical Writings of Thomas Vaughan." It introduces a digest of the works of the famous French occultist Eliphas Lévi.</meta>

<page-num>295</page-num>
<header>ADVERTISEMENTS.</header>

->*Demy 8vo, pages xliii and 349, with illustrations, extra cloth binding, 10s. 6d.*<-

# The Mysteries of Magic;
## A DIGEST OF THE WRITINGS OF ELIPHAS LÉVI,
### WITH A BIOGRAPHICAL AND CRITICAL ESSAY.
### By ARTHUR EDWARD WAITE.

->**Opinions of the Press.**<-

> “Of the many remarkable men who have gained notoriety for their skill, real or imaginary, in the Black Arts, probably no one presents a more strange and contradictory character than the French magician, Alphonse Louis Constant. . . . Better known under the Jewish pseudonym of Eliphas Lévi Zahed, this enthusiastic student of forbidden art caused some excitement in France and even in London. . . . His works on magic are those of an undoubted genius, and reveal a philosophy beautiful in concept, even if it is totally opposed to common-sense principles. . . . There is such a wealth of learning and attractive reasoning in these writings that Mr. Arthur Edward Waite has published a digest of them for the benefit of English readers. This gentleman has not attempted a literal translation in every case, but has organized a volume which, while reproducing a great portion of the more interesting works with sufficient accuracy, gives an excellent idea of the scope of the entire literary remains of an enthusiast for whom he holds a profound admiration. . . . The reader may profitably and carefully read the learned dissertations written by M. Constant upon the Hermetic art, treated as a religion, a philosophy, and a natural science. . . . In view of the remarkable exhibitions of mesmeric influence and thought-reading which have recently been given, it is not improbable that the thoughtful reader may find a clue in the writings of this cultured and amiable magician to the secret of many of the manifestations of witchcraft which formerly struck wonder and terror into the hearts of simple people. . . .” —*The Morning Post.*

> “The present single volume is a digest of half-a-dozen books listed by the author in a ‘Biographical and Critical Essay’ with which he introduces his project. These are the *Dogme et Rituel de la Haute Magie*, the *Histoire de la Magie*, the *Clef des Grands Mystères*, the *Sorcier de Meudon*, the *Philosophie Occulte*, and the *Science des Esprits*. To tackle the whole series—which, indeed, might be difficult to obtain now in a complete form—would be a bold undertaking, but Mr. Waite has endeavored to give his readers the essence of all six books in a relatively compact size. . . . The book before us is encyclopedic in its range, and it would be difficult to find a single volume better suited to provide modern inquirers with a general understanding of the scope and purpose of the occult sciences at large. It freely handles, among others, the grim topics of witchcraft and black magic, but certainly it would be difficult to imagine any reader being tempted to enter those paths of experimentation by the description of their character and purpose that Eliphas Lévi provides. In this way the fearless old Kabbalist, though never bothering his readers with grand exhortations in the interest of virtue, writes under the inspiration of an uncompromising devotion to the highest ideals, and all his philosophy tends toward righteousness.” —Mr. A. P. SINNETT, in *Light.*

> “We are grateful to Mr. Waite for translating the account of how Lévi, in a lonely room in London, summoned the spirit of Apollonius of Tyana. This very eerie composition is written in quite the finest style of the late Lord Lytton when he was speaking about the occult.” —*The Saturday Review.*

<summary>This page contains an advertisement and press reviews for Arthur Edward Waite's "The Mysteries of Magic," a comprehensive digest of the occult writings of Eliphas Lévi. Reviewers praise the work for its accessibility, its coverage of the Hermetic arts and Kabbalah, and its insights into the psychological secrets of witchcraft and mesmerism.</summary>

<page-num>296</page-num>
<header>ADVERTISEMENTS.</header>

->*Demy 8vo, pages 315, cloth, 10s. 6d.*<-

# Lives of Alchemical Philosophers,

->BASED ON MATERIALS COLLECTED IN 1815, AND SUPPLEMENTED BY RECENT RESEARCH.<-

->WITH A PHILOSOPHICAL DEMONSTRATION OF THE TRUE PRINCIPLES OF THE MAGNUM OPUS OR GREAT WORK OF ALCHEMICAL RECONSTRUCTION, AND SOME ACCOUNT OF SPIRITUAL CHEMISTRY<-

->BY ARTHUR EDWARD WAITE.<-

->TO WHICH IS ADDED<-

->A BIBLIOGRAPHY OF ALCHEMY AND HERMETIC PHILOSOPHY.<-

---

## Opinions of the Press.

> “The chapter about Flamel is one of the most interesting in the book, but the longest and most enthralling is that containing a full account of the career of the infamous Cagliostro, whom Carlyle has immolated. This is really a romance of the highest interest. . . There is an abundance of interest in Mr. Waite’s pages for those who have any inclination for occult studies, and although he founds his work upon a book which was published in 1815 by an anonymous writer, yet he adds so much fresh matter that this is practically a new work. A valuable feature for students is the alphabetical catalogue which Mr. Waite has prepared of all known works on hermetic philosophy and alchemy.”—*Glasgow Herald.*

> “Mr. Waite has undoubtedly bestowed a vast amount of patient and laborious research upon the present work, inspired by the double conviction that the original alchemists had in fact anticipated and transcended the highest results of chemistry in the metallic kingdom, and had discovered in the twilight of the Middle Ages the future development of universal evolution. The biographical sketches of the alchemists, both true and false, are curious reading, and the alphabetical catalogue of works on Hermetic Philosophy is surprisingly suggestive of ages when leisure was less scarce, and literature scarcer, than in modern days.”—*Daily News.*

> “The alchemists more popularly known, such as Albertus Magnus, Roger Bacon, Raymond Lully, Flamel, Paracelsus, and Basil Valentine are dealt with fairly and fully, and the travels and adventures of Joseph Balsamo, alias Cagliostro, with his somewhat peculiar developments of Egyptian Freemasonry, are excellent and interesting reading. . . Such an intelligent study of the subject must bring into relief the infinite possibilities which are contained in a combination of psychical insight with physical knowledge.”—*Light.*

> “The lives of the philosophers themselves are interesting and curious reading; the stories of Lully, Flamel, Valentine, Trevisan, and Zachaire are full of glimpses of medieval times. To us the most instructive and valuable of the lives is that of the prince of impostors, Joseph Balsamo, or Count de Cagliostro, who died at the end of the last century.”—*Spectator.*

> “The old alchemists . . . may, however, with justice be regarded as the first experimentalists in analytical chemistry, and on this account are entitled to the gratitude of subsequent generations. The lives of the principal alchemists are briefly recorded and their works mentioned. Among them are such familiar names as Thomas Aquinas, Roger Bacon, Paracelsus, Helvetius, and Delisle. The volume also contains an alphabetical catalogue of works on hermetic philosophy and alchemy.”—*Morning Post.*
